The pilot swore away from the microphone. The mother did not. "Admiral, you read an order to kill my daughter and now you want us to trust your maintenance judgment."

Calder kept the skiff vector centered. "No. I want you to distrust me from outside a collision path. Cut thrust."

For seven seconds the craft continued. Kindly Oath lit two lateral jets and moved to meet it. A boarding clamp unfolded from the cutter's nose, slow enough to show restraint and large enough to show consequence.

Skiff Four vented its forward line. The plume spread in a white fan. Its speed bled away twenty meters short of the boundary.

Calder released a breath through her nose. "Send the rotor specifications to quarantine inspection. Have Everspring stream bank twelve telemetry in public view. If the need verifies, the skiff transfers cargo by line."

"Acknowledged," the pilot said, stripped of every courtesy.

The mother stayed on the channel. "Her name is Lina Seft. Put that in your weapons log."

"Commander Pell, record Lina Seft beside bank twelve and this incident."

Pell entered the name. "Recorded."

The quarantine desk added a maintenance exception procedure while twenty-three more civilian requests arrived. Insulin cultures. Embryo coolant. A crew member's husband asking permission to dock and remain. Calder ordered each request preserved, no automatic rejection permitted. Quarantine could not become a quieter spelling of abandonment.

She chose three command channels. Okoro occupied the first. Captain Besh of Watchful Coil took the second. Iven's inquiry escort had placed a silent request on the third.

"Hold Mora," she said. "Put Besh through."

Besh appeared with his bridge behind him. "You have a verified remedy and an execution window. Why announce a review?"

"Because the remedy demands civilian extermination."

"It demands prevention of a forecast catastrophe."

"By killing civilians."

"The distinction does not alter the authentication."

Calder enlarged the warning's probability field beside his face. Its confidence cone contracted to a blade at Serein insertion, then disappeared beyond the event. "Authentication proves where the message came from. It does not explain why every surviving branch vanishes from this model."

"That question belongs after obedience."

"Not when obedience removes everyone who could answer it."

Besh folded his hands beneath the camera's view. "Hespar spent seventy-two hours asking why."

The name reached her more cleanly than an accusation. Calder looked down at the ring on her right hand. It had belonged to a council clerk who wrote three requests for confirmation while southern transports lifted half empty. The clerk's last letter still waited in her private files, written in a careful hand from a city already losing power.

"Hespar received an evacuation order," she said. "This annex demands destruction."

"Hespar received a verified forecast. So did we."

"Two million died because Hespar delayed movement that could have saved them. If we fire now, eighteen thousand four hundred die because we refused to delay killing them. Do not flatten those acts into one lesson, Captain."

"Then tell me what lesson you kept."

Calder let the question stand between their bridges. "Prepare pursuit stores and boarding teams. Hold weapons at readiness. I want options that preserve the insertion deadline without treating immediate fire as our only form of obedience."

"That sounds like delay."

"It sounds like command."

She cut Besh's channel and opened Okoro's. The ecologist no longer stood in the insulated corridor. She had moved to Everspring's operations room. Crew crowded behind her, some in medical whites, some in fabrication coveralls. Sena Vale leaned against the far bulkhead with one arm strapped across her chest. Calder had watched a false casualty notice cross the fleet record two hours earlier. The woman inside it looked pale and furious, but alive.

"Pilot Vale," Calder said. "Your ship reported you lost during interception."

"My ship reported what somebody needed it to report."

"You will give evidence to the inquiry."

"If I can undock. Your quarantine says I cannot."

"Then you will give it from there. Doctor Okoro, confirm no thaw cycle has begun."

Okoro looked toward a bank of suspension telemetry. "No general thaw. Four medical exceptions remain active from before the warning. No embryo process has started. Terraforming stays cold."

"Confirm your insertion schedule."

"We cross the commitment gate in four days if we hold the assigned profile. Actual Serein insertion follows later. A full abort before the gate costs most of our braking reserve."

Calder heard the qualification. Later did not mean safely later. A seedship carried enough mass to turn every course change into a decision measured in fuel, heat, and lives.

"You will hold your present vector while my navigation staff calculates a tow solution."

"Our present vector takes us toward the gate."

"A braking hold would expose your radiators to convoy debris."

"A tow gives you physical custody."

"Quarantine already gives me legal custody."

Okoro's gaze sharpened. "Legal custody does not move a ship."

"No. Reaction mass moves a ship, and you do not have enough to waste proving a point. Hold your vector. Accept no docking traffic. Keep your terraforming lattice cold."

"Will you guarantee no strike while we comply?"

Calder looked past Okoro at the crew. Sena had shifted closer to the operations table. Her good hand rested beside a brass manual control with its safety cover lifted.

"I guarantee no strike without my direct order," Calder said. "I have not given one."

"That guarantee ends if your command ends."

"So does every order I can give you."

Okoro glanced at the annex on her display. "The people in suspension have inherited a verdict from descendants they cannot question and an admiral they cannot elect. I need more than a procedural promise."

"You need time. I am giving you what time the corridor allows. Do not use it to run."

Okoro did not agree. She closed the channel with the formal acknowledgment required by fleet law, not the plain one Calder wanted.

The inquiry escort brought Iven onto a slim side screen. They sat alone in the cartography bay with their hands visible on the table. No restraints. A security officer stood outside the sealed door.

"Chief Mora," Calder said. "Did you access the annex before today?"

Iven looked toward something below the camera, perhaps a map they could no longer alter. "I will answer under recorded inquiry."

"This channel records."

"Not with an advocate present."

"Eighteen thousand four hundred people learned an hour ago that command carried an order to kill them. I am asking whether you knew."

Iven raised their eyes. Exhaustion had hollowed the skin beneath them. "I knew the authentication extended beyond the page in your packet."

"That does not answer me."

"No. It does not."

Calder felt the room waiting for her anger. She had used anger as a tool in mutiny hearings, treaty failures, and casualty rooms. Here it offered no leverage. Iven had chosen the shape of the inquiry before she called.

"Your access remains sealed," she said. "At 0600 you will appear with an advocate. Bring every copy, note, and route alteration associated with this warning."

"Admiral, do not let tactical navigation choose a braking corridor before Tev Arin reviews the seedship's thermal limits."

"You are not in a position to advise me."

"No. The sleepers are. They cannot speak, so their radiator margins will have to do it."

Calder closed the channel.

Pell had assembled the readiness board while she spoke. Seven defense ships could place laser energy on Everspring within an hour. Three had viable kinetic solutions, though debris risk made two unacceptable. Boarding required matching the seedship's vector and crossing a quarantine boundary Calder had just established. Tow clamps would take longer to rig than the current geometry permitted.

"Remove kinetic options," Calder said.

Sorn turned from weapons. "All of them?"

"A miss continues into the Serein corridor. A hit produces debris among eighteen thousand suspended bodies. Remove them."

"That leaves laser disablement and boarding."

"Prepare both."

Commander Pell opened the key cabinet. This time Calder took the first physical key. Its tamper ribbon snapped with a dry sound. Pell took the second. They crossed the deck to separate arming consoles.

Sorn projected the laser plan. Three emitters would converge on the seedship's forward communications crown, not its inhabited spindle. A second solution targeted drive-control relays along the unpressurized truss. Each burn risked reflective scatter. Each demanded hours of cooling before a second shot.

"No radiator targets," Calder said.

"Disabling the drive without touching heat rejection leaves us two relay clusters," Sorn replied. "Either hit may propagate into guidance."

"Build a lower-energy ladder. First burn severs external command. Second takes one relay. Leave enough interval for surrender between them."

"The warning's deadline does not account for surrender intervals."

"Our weapons plan will."

Pell called up boarding geometry. Matching Everspring required two cutters to shed spin, burn hard for thirty-two minutes, then coast while their radiators recovered behind shield panels. Crews would reach the hull exhausted and warm. A rushed docking could puncture the seedship before any defender fired.

Calder assigned a medical tender to the intercept group and removed its offensive drone rack. She cut the boarding party from sixty to twenty. Armor remained permitted; breaching charges did not. Every limitation reduced the chance of taking control. Each also reduced the chance of proving the annex right through their own violence.

"Readiness state?" Calder asked.

"Cold racks loaded," Pell said. "Guidance isolated. Emitters aligned but safed. Two-key authorization required for capacitor charge."

"Set pursuit stations. Bring Resolute Vigil's spin down by one tenth before any burn. Warn medical. Tell every escort captain that crossing the quarantine line requires my voice authorization."

The deck reshaped itself to fit the orders. Seats locked. Chart displays traded convoy routes for intercept cones. Deep inside the ship, missile racks moved on their rails without waking the weapons inside them. Radiator pumps climbed one setting to absorb guidance and sensor load.

Calder inserted her key but did not turn it. Pell mirrored her at the second console.

"Flag key present," he said.

"Witness key present."

Sorn watched the capacitor board. "Weapons prepared. Charge remains zero."

On the tactical glass, Everspring continued toward Serein along the same patient curve. A quarantine ring enclosed its icon, first amber, then red as each enforcement ship acknowledged. Civilian craft withdrew from the boundary. One small tug remained docked to the seedship's far side, its transponder still insisting that its pilot had died.

Calder set the annex beside the firing solution. One demanded destruction before insertion. The other showed how quickly destruction could be arranged.

"Maintain quarantine," she said. "No fire. Keep every weapon ready."

The red ring closed over Everspring. Under Calder's hand, the unturned key waited in its lock.

Chapter 6 — A Debt in Possible Lives

The inquiry table rejected Iven's handprint on the first attempt.

They wiped their palm on their trousers and tried again. The glass recognized them, lit their name beneath their fingers, and added a red restriction mark beside it. CHIEF CARTOGRAPHER. ACCESS SUSPENDED. WITNESS STATUS.

Across the table, Admiral Calder watched without offering a seat. Commander Pell arranged three sealed archive blocks beside the recording node. Iven's advocate, Jo Venn, checked the room's public-log indicator and found it active. A security officer waited beyond the transparent door. Through that door, the mirror deck showed only half its usual light.

"Sit," Calder said.

Iven sat. The chair had been fixed to the deck for acceleration, although Aster Vale carried no burn warning. Inquiry rooms assumed every stable condition might end before testimony did.

Venn placed a blank legal wafer beside Iven's hand. "My client will answer factual questions concerning access, custody, and disclosure. Questions about intent must identify the governing clause."

"Your client can identify clauses without assistance," Calder said.

"That has contributed to our present problem."

Pell suppressed a reaction. Calder did not.

She touched the first archive block. "At 22:41 yesterday, Chief Mora opened the authenticated forecast. Forty-three seconds after my credential entered the vault, theirs accessed a command-partitioned annex. Did you use your own credential?"

"Yes," Iven said.

"Did anyone direct you to open it?"

"No."

"Did you understand its lethal remedy?"

Venn put two fingers on the wafer. "Governing clause."

Calder keyed the citation onto the table: Compact emergency custody, fraudulent omission during active threat review. "Did you understand it?"

Iven looked at the text beneath their hand. They had spent nineteen hours refusing this sentence, first to Calder, then to themselves.

"Yes."

The recording node changed from green to blue. Admission indexed.

Calder's face showed less anger than Iven expected. The restraint made the room harder to map. "State what you read."

"An instruction to destroy Everspring, its biological manifest, embryo archive, and settlement capacity before Serein insertion. It specified that mission personnel could not survive the remedy."

"Why did you tell me only that the forecast required interdiction?"

Venn's fingers moved again. Iven stopped them.

"Because I concealed the annex," they said.

Blue light pulsed through the node. Beyond the door, someone crossed the mirror deck carrying a coolant gauge. The ordinary movement made the admission feel more exact, not less.

Calder sat at last. "We will reach motive after custody. Show me how you got through the partition."

Pell pushed a stripped copy of the packet onto the table. Its layers opened as nested contour maps. The outer authentication carried the final warning. A legal sheath held provenance, checksum history, and the fifty-three-year transit solution. Beneath both lay an unlabelled region with no ordinary access route.

Iven rotated the packet. "The partition followed a survey convention, not a command convention. Survey archives omit hazards from public products but preserve them outside the visible map. Whoever built this message used our blank-space notation."

"A notation developed in this century," Pell said.

"A notation the sender could have inherited fifty-three years from now."

"Or copied from this packet after you altered it," Calder said.

Iven enlarged the audit edge. "My access left this scar. The partition geometry predates it."

Pell compared creation and access hashes. His mouth tightened. "Confirmed."

Calder touched the blank region. "Why hide a lethal instruction inside cartographic syntax?"

"Because command would authenticate the visible warning first. A cartographer would notice the omission."

"You think it targeted you."

"I think it expected a person trained to distrust empty space."

The answer entered the record. Iven heard how it sounded: self-importance dressed as method. They pulled the packet back and showed a cluster of minute checksum differences along the legal sheath.

"It also expected an inquiry," they said. "These marks opened only after the annex entered a public challenge."

Venn leaned closer. "Opened into what?"

"I do not know yet. My access ended before the registry filing changed the packet."

Calder glanced at Pell. "Preserve those marks. Nobody executes embedded code."

"Already isolated," Pell said.

A low vibration passed through the deck as Aster Vale adjusted spin balance. The archive blocks clicked against their magnetic rests. Pell caught the oldest one before it shifted.

Its casing bore a faded date from one hundred twelve years earlier.

Calder set it in front of Iven. "You cited possible coercion in your sealed note. Before we discuss that claim, you will reconstruct the institution you accuse."

"I accused a procedure, not an institution."

"Procedures do not hide pages by themselves."

Pell opened the block. Grainy medical footage rose above the table: isolation tents, hand-pumped respirators, a child's chest struggling under transparent film. A date stamp placed the images six days before the Gray Lung forecast arrived.

"First verified return warning," Calder said. "Read its instruction."

Iven knew the text. Every schoolchild knew it. They read from the exhibit anyway. "Close the Beren ports. Destroy imported fungal feedstock. Change respirator membranes to silver mesh."

Pell advanced the record. A dockmaster objected to stopping food shipments on an unauthenticated message. A laboratory technician tested the feedstock and found spores nobody had cultured before. Port closure followed. Infection curves bent within three days.

"Estimated lives preserved," Calder said.

"The estimate remains disputed."

"Give the accepted range."

"Twenty-eight million to thirty-four million."

Another exhibit opened. A reactor baffle redesigned from measurements not yet taken. A storm shelter moved six kilometers before a moonquake. A childhood vaccine altered by one protein. No lecture joined them. Calder made Iven handle each object, read the contemporary objection, and place the later casualty record beside it.

The Descendant Compact had not begun as a constitution. It had accumulated from emergency receipts. A port seal here. An insurance clause there. Then fleet law required compliance with verified engineering warnings. Civil courts began treating refusal as negligence. Councils kept their formal right to disobey while every bond market, evacuation schedule, and medical standard punished the attempt.

Pell opened a ship mortgage from eighty-seven years earlier. Its survival clause voided coverage if a captain ignored forecast advice. Venn found the same clause, revised but intact, in Everspring's mission insurance.

"Show the consequence of refusal," Calder said.

Pell copied the seedship policy into the inquiry sandbox. He changed one field from COMPLIANT to CAPTAIN DECLINES FORECAST REMEDY. The policy voided itself at once. A linked fuel covenant flashed next, because uninsured vessels could not draw reserve deuterium from Commonwealth depots. Berthing indemnity failed after that. Civilian proxy coverage for all eighteen thousand four hundred sleepers entered review.

"No order appears in any of those clauses," Venn said.

"No," Iven answered. "Each clause recognizes the next one as ordinary risk management."

Calder pointed to the failing links. "Restore the policy and decline only the lethal annex."

Pell separated the visible warning from its remedy. The sandbox refused the distinction. Both shared one authentication root, so compliance attached to the packet as a whole. He tried marking the annex contested. The insurance remained active for twelve hours, pending Compact review, then failed if no tribunal issued a stay.

"Twelve hours," Calder said. "Longer than our clean intercept window."

"Shorter than meaningful civil review," Venn replied.

Iven touched the fuel covenant. "This structure explains why advice can remain formally nonbinding. A captain may refuse. Afterward the ship loses fuel rights, berth rights, liability coverage, and access to public rescue guarantees. Nobody arrests the captain for dissent. The crew simply inherits consequences large enough to make dissent impossible."

"Those consequences protect everyone who relies on forecasts," Calder said.

"They also turn authentication into judgment."

Pell restored the original policy. Green marks returned across the chain. Everspring became insurable again, eligible for fuel, and welcome at Commonwealth docks, provided it accepted a remedy that erased it.

Calder studied the restored links. "Could Doctor Okoro have launched without these covenants?"

"Not at this mass," Pell said. "No civilian consortium could indemnify eighteen thousand sleepers without Compact backing."

"Then she accepted the rules before departure."

Venn shook her head. "She accepted rules for advice. Nobody disclosed an authenticated order requiring total destruction of her passengers. Consent to a system does not include every concealed output the system may later produce."

The sandbox kept every consequence visible. Iven had mapped transit corridors with fewer dependencies than this contract carried. None of the lines called itself force. Together they left Everspring one legal direction.

"No council voted away present authority in one act," Iven said. "We priced disobedience out of ordinary life."

Calder tapped the Gray Lung footage. "Ordinary life continued because people obeyed."

"Yes."

"Say that without qualification."

"People lived because councils obeyed verified warnings."

"And you concealed one."

"Yes."

The simple answer left no route behind it. Iven could trace every institutional contour leading to their act. None removed the act.

Calder opened the third archive block. Iven knew its damaged corner before the label appeared.

WAKEWORLD FLOTILLA EVACUATION REVIEW. SEALED FAMILY MATERIAL.

Venn stood. "Admiral, this exhibit exceeds the custody inquiry."

"Your client cited it in their private note."

Iven had forgotten the note. They had written it at twenty-three, after their first certification board asked why their hazard maps carried visible uncertainty bands instead of one recommended route. The answer had gone into a sealed ethics file. Somebody on Pell's staff had read deeply.

"Open it," Iven said.

Venn turned toward them. "You do not need to consent."

"It already shaped what I did. Put it where people can see it."

Pell broke the family seal.

A migration map spread across the table. Twelve habitats moved between Caul's outer work zones, each on a slow fusion economy that left little reserve. A forecast had predicted a containment failure in one habitat's fuel plant. The instruction optimized evacuation by transferring tug capacity to eleven habitats and cutting the twelfth loose before its damaged reactor could threaten the flotilla.

Iven placed two fingers on the omitted route. Habitat Twelve had no line leading out.

Their mother had served as traffic coordinator aboard Habitat Four. Her voice entered the archive through an operations call, younger than Iven remembered and more impatient.

"We can send two tugs back after Four clears," she said in the recording. "Twelve has six hours before containment loss. Two tugs can take eight hundred people."

A forecast liaison answered from another ship. "Returning the tugs creates a collision probability across the main evacuation lane. The model preserves three thousand fewer lives if they turn back."

"Those eight hundred have names."

"So do the three thousand."

The call ended there. Iven's mother had followed the optimized route. She cleared four habitats through a debris field with no collisions. Thousands reached the safe transfer orbit. Habitat Twelve broke containment five hours later.

Calder paused the record before the casualty summary. "Your mother survived the evacuation."

"For eleven months."

"Cause of death?"

"Radiation exposure from flying the outer traffic station after its shielding failed. The forecast route required somebody to keep that station crewed."

Calder looked at the frozen image. "Did the instruction conceal that cost?"

"No. It listed a probable fatal exposure for the coordinator. She volunteered after reading it."

"Then the warning gave her a choice."

"It gave her a choice between her life and a collision field full of families."

"She still chose."

Iven's mother had recorded one private message after the evacuation. She sat in a medical berth with burn patches covering her neck. No accusation entered her voice. That absence had hurt Iven more than blame could have.

Pell began to close the exhibit. Iven stopped him.

"Play the family message."

The younger face looked out over the inquiry table.

"Iven, if they show you the numbers later, they will show Twelve beside the flotilla total. Do not let them say the total contains Twelve. A sum can count losses without carrying them. I followed the route. I would probably follow it again. That does not make the route innocent, and it does not make me innocent either."

She reached toward the recorder. Her fingers shook from treatment drugs.

"You will want a map that saves everyone. Build honest maps instead."

The image ended.

For several seconds nobody touched the controls. The recording node kept its blue light. It did not know which admission to index.

Calder spoke first. "You believe the final warning repeats that evacuation."

"No," Iven said. "The evacuation instruction disclosed its human costs. It gave my mother a decision she hated, but she could see the people excluded from the route. This message hid the remedy and compressed eighteen thousand four hundred sleepers into biological manifest. It removed the moral object from the visible page."

"So you removed the page from me."

"Yes."

"You answered concealment with concealment."

"Yes."

Venn sat down slowly.

Calder folded her hands. "Why?"

Iven could have described the probability cone, the suspicious blank beyond insertion, or the way obedience had hardened into insurance and command law. All of it mattered. None had moved their hand when they closed the annex.

"I wanted one hour in which the order could not become a weapon," they said. "I told myself I would test the field and disclose the remedy once I had evidence. Then the hour became several. Every delay made disclosure harder, because I had also concealed the delay."

"Did you intend to prevent execution?"

"I intended to prevent automatic execution."

"That distinction belongs to you, not to the people placed at risk."

"I know."

Calder's voice stayed level. "Your choice may have spent the only clean interception window. If Everspring runs, weapons will need to fire at worse geometry. Debris may enter the Serein corridor. Boarding crews may die."

"If I had disclosed it at once, would you have fired?"

Venn whispered Iven's name. Too late.

Calder regarded them across the exhibits. "I do not know."

That answer cost her. Iven saw the cost in the way she kept her right hand off the old ring. An easier commander would have lied.

"Neither do I," Iven said. "I took the decision because I feared your answer. That did not make it mine to take. I concealed the annex. I altered who could act, when they could act, and what risks followed. Enter all of that as my admission."

Pell keyed the language into the record. Venn added a reservation concerning command causality. Iven let both stand. Maps often needed conflicting legends before anyone noticed the terrain did not belong to either one.

A tone sounded from the isolated packet.

Pell reached for the cutoff. Calder caught his wrist before he touched it.

"Report first."

He checked the air-gapped monitor. "The checksum marks have assembled into an index. No external execution. The inquiry node supplied a legal vocabulary table when we opened the family exhibit."

"Why would an archive exhibit affect the packet?" Venn asked.

Iven leaned toward the monitor. The marks no longer resembled damage. They formed retrieval coordinates through the authenticated sheath, each one pointing to a region too dense for ordinary warning text.

"Because we entered testimony concerning delegated choice," they said. "The packet had been waiting for a legal context."

"That gives it intent," Pell said.

"It gives it a condition. Do not grant more than the evidence."

The monitor displayed a file class none of them had requested.

COGNITIVE CONTINUITY PATTERN. DEGRADED HIGH-FIDELITY TRANSFER. EXECUTION PROHIBITED WITHOUT PRESENT CONSENT.

Calder released Pell's wrist. "Can it act?"

"Not from isolation," he said. "It has no substrate assignment. No sensory channels. No memory access outside its own encrypted volume."

A second line appeared.

PETITION: TEMPORARY EMBODIMENT IN LEGAL-MEMORY SUBSTRATE.

The room changed without moving. Iven read the words twice, then checked the source path. The petition came from inside the final authenticated packet. Its transmission stamp carried the same fifty-three-year displacement as the warning. No sender name appeared. The identity field contained only a hash.

Venn moved first. "A petition requires a petitioner."

"It may contain one," Iven said. "We cannot know without loading the pattern."

Pell pulled up substrate capacity. Aster Vale carried legal-memory hardware for reconstructing damaged witness records and testing whether high-fidelity copies could testify. It had never hosted a transmitted cognition. Embodiment would grant the pattern sensory input, speech, and access to whatever memories survived compression. It could also damage the substrate or itself.

Calder read the consent restriction. "Can we question it without embodiment?"

"Possibly through a text sandbox," Pell said.

Iven examined the packet structure. "The output layer depends on autobiographical continuity. A sandbox might produce fragments without a stable speaker."

"Convenient," Calder said. "The warning demands obedience, then offers a witness who can speak only if we build it a mouth."

"The warning did not offer this openly. The inquiry unlocked it."

"Which means your concealment led us here."

"Yes. That does not redeem it."

The petition added a resource estimate, a proposed duration, and a present-consent clause. It requested no control of ship systems. It asked for witness protections during activation and deletion review afterward.

Pell looked from the monitor to Calder. "Flag authority can approve emergency loading. Civil law requires an identity advocate and medical supervision."

Venn said, "Identity advocacy cannot begin until we decide whether the pattern qualifies as a possible person."

"And deciding may require hearing it," Iven said.

Calder stood. "No activation. Preserve the packet under three-key custody. Pell keeps one key, civil registry keeps one, and Mora's advocate keeps one. Chief Mora remains suspended and confined to cartography except under escort."

"What happens to the inquiry?" Iven asked.

"Your admission stands. Findings wait until we know whether you concealed evidence, a witness, or both."

The archive table dimmed. Gray Lung tents vanished. The evacuation routes disappeared next, including the line that had never reached Habitat Twelve. Iven's mother left last, her hand frozen an instant above the recorder before darkness took the image.

On the isolated monitor, the request for embodiment remained pending. Its cursor blinked inside an empty identity field while three custodians prepared their keys.

Chapter 7 — The First Defection

Sena had half her body inside Honest John's radiator hinge when the tow ultimatum reached Everspring.

The tug hung in the seedship's far-side cradle with its reactor cold. Dock gravity pulled weakly across the maintenance tube, enough to keep her boots against the ribbed deck and not enough to stop tools drifting when she released them. Her left arm remained bound to her chest. She worked one-handed, catching each wrench on a wrist cord before moving to the next bolt.

The bearing had shifted half a degree during the interception. Half a degree had let her deliver Okoro's spindle. It had also left the number-two vane rubbing its cold stop whenever the tug changed attitude. A vane that could not unfold cleanly made Honest John a one-burn craft. After that burn, heat would choose the destination.

Okoro's voice came through the tug speaker. "Sena, bridge. Now."

"Seven minutes."

"They gave us six."

Sena braced her shoulder against the hinge housing and pulled. The jammed inspection plate opened with a soft metallic crack. Beneath it, the bearing race showed a bright crescent where ceramic had ground against steel.

"Who gave us six?"

"Admiral Calder. Two cutters have matched our vector. They intend to attach tow bridles and take helm authority."

"Are they asking?"

"The ultimatum calls consent legally presumed under sleeper-protection law."

"Then they are not asking."

A grease-pencil mark crossed the inside of the housing. Tev had drawn it before she left Aster Vale: ALIGN COLD EDGE HERE. LEAVE TWO THREADS. DO NOT TRUST AUTO-SEAT. She set the race to his line, left two threads visible, and locked the adjustment with safety wire.

The vane still would not survive a full pursuit burn. It might survive the beginning of one.

Sena backed out of the tube. Her shoulder struck the rim. Pain moved from the strapped arm into her neck, clean and immediate. She waited for her vision to settle, packed the tools into their clips, and closed the radiator housing.

"Five minutes," Okoro said.

"Tell them the pilot died. Their own record says so."

"They corrected the record."

"Efficient."

She pushed toward the airlock.

The operations room on Everspring had acquired forty people since Calder read the annex. Suspension engineers stood beside navigation crew. Civilian advocates occupied the rear wall with legal wafers open. Four proxy representatives watched from convoy links. Nobody raised their voice. A seedship full of sleeping families did not need volume to create pressure.

Okoro stood at the central table. The display showed Everspring as a long blue mass on its Serein curve. Two defense cutters, Quiet Reed and Linden's Edge, held station off the forward truss. Tow bridles extended from both hulls, each cable coiled inside an armored launcher.

A red quarantine shell enclosed the three ships.

Calder occupied the main screen. "Doctor Okoro, you have four minutes to accept tow control. Your life support and suspension systems remain under your command. Navigation transfers to my boarding master until the fleet council reviews the warning."

"Your council has no sleeper representatives seated," Okoro said.

"The Compact grants emergency custody when a sleeper manifest faces imminent harm."

"Your authenticated order defines their survival as failure. You cannot claim custody under a law meant to preserve them."

"I have not executed that remedy."

"You prepared the weapons."

"I also prohibited fire. Accept tow and keep that prohibition easy to maintain."

Sena read the geometry. Quiet Reed planned to fasten near the seedship's forward communications crown. Linden's Edge would take the aft truss beside the drive-control relays. Once both bridles tightened, they could torque Everspring away from its commitment gate without asking the main drive. The maneuver would spare fuel. It would also put two military hulls within cutting distance of every external command line.

"Three minutes," Calder said.

Okoro muted the link. "Options."

Navigation chief Sal Vey pulled up a burn profile. "Main drive can add six meters per second before they attach. That ruins their current tow solution. It does not outrun cutters. We keep thrust low enough for the suspension banks, then coast while the radiators recover."

"Commitment gate?"

"We cross it earlier by eleven hours. After the burn, abort costs reserve we need for Serein braking."

An engineer touched the thermal pane. "Seed mirror package shares the forward coolant trunk during launch configuration. We have not separated it because quarantine froze deployment work. Drive heat will load the mirror lattice above storage limits."

"Can you isolate it?" Okoro asked.

"Not in three minutes."

Sena enlarged Honest John's icon at the far-side cradle. "The tug can fly escort on the weak quarter."

Every face nearest the table turned toward her.

"Your radiator cannot carry an escort burn," Vey said.

"It can carry one if I leave the vane half open and keep the reactor below sixty percent."

"For how long?"

"Long enough to make the cutters watch me instead of the seedship."

Okoro looked at Sena's bound arm. "You cannot hold a manual profile through sustained thrust."

"The yoke does not know how many hands I have."

"You delivered the filing. You do not owe us another flight."

Sena felt the wooden box under Honest John's helm as distinctly as if it pressed against her ribs. Jori's name sat burned into its lid. The old rescue story offered her its familiar shape: one pilot, one impossible corridor, one decision taken before command could make it worse. She knew that shape too well to trust it.

"I am not flying alone," she said. "Give me your navigation plot and a bridge controller on a live link. If the link drops, I follow the last safe branch. If my vane sticks, you do not turn back."

"That still puts you outside the hull when weapons come live."

"It puts a pilot where an escort should be."

The muted channel flashed. Two minutes.

Okoro looked toward the proxy representatives. "I need a recorded decision. Refuse tow and hold the present course, or accept custody."

The first proxy spoke for three suspension banks. "Refuse."

The second asked whether refusal guaranteed insertion. Okoro said it guaranteed nothing beyond keeping helm authority for the next burn. The proxy refused tow anyway. The third abstained because twelve families in her group had withdrawn instructions after the annex became public. The fourth represented embryo trusts and lacked authority to approve military custody.

No clean vote emerged. Only enough refusal to prevent Okoro from pretending consent.

She opened Calder's channel. "Everspring declines tow. We will hold internal command and proceed under our mission charter."

Calder's face did not change. "Your mission charter no longer has valid insurance or Compact protection. If you ignite your drive, you breach quarantine. Any vessel assisting you will enter mutiny status."

Sena unlatched her pilot token from her belt. She placed it on the table and keyed Honest John's command register.

PILOT ACCEPTS ESCORT CONTROL UNDER EVERSRING MISSION AUTHORITY.

The register asked whether she acknowledged conflicting flag orders.

She selected YES.

It asked whether she understood that assistance in breaching quarantine constituted mutiny under fleet law.

"Spell my status correctly," Sena said to the recording node. "Mutineer, pilot Sena Vale, courier registry seven-one-six. Voluntary. No coercion by seedship command."

The word entered the log.

Okoro did not close the register. "An escort command requires limits. You asked for distributed control. State it for the record."

Sena looked at the burn profile. The room had already accepted her choice too easily. A pilot who volunteered for danger often received gratitude when she needed resistance.

"Mere owns the safe branches," she said. "If I leave one without declaring collision avoidance, she can cut my drive through escort link. Tev Arin owns radiator limits. If he calls hinge failure, Honest John falls out of formation. Your suspension chief owns acceleration. If any bank crosses red, Everspring reduces thrust even if that gives the cutters a line."

Vey frowned. "A remote drive cut can strand you outside our recovery cone."

"That makes it useful."

"What authority stays with you?" Okoro asked.

"Collision response, cold-jet maneuvering, and the choice to detach if my hull threatens yours."

Mere came onto the operations link from the bridge pit. Her face carried the flat concentration of someone who had already loaded too many moving masses into one local plot. "I accept branch control. I need Honest John's exact thrust lag and your manual cutoff latency."

"Point eight seconds from command to reactor fall. Cold jets answer in point two if the tanks stay warm."

"They will not stay warm if you coast shadowed by the seedship."

"Then model one second."

Tev's audio arrived through the dead tug channel. "I accept thermal call, with a condition. Sena does not get to debate a failure limit after I call it."

"You enjoy the word condition," Sena said.

"I enjoy machinery that remains machinery."

Okoro entered both acceptances. The suspension chief added thresholds for shear, blood oxygen, and coolant pressure across the older banks. Honest John's escort page filled with people who could stop Sena. She read the list twice. It made the flight less clean than the one in her head and more likely to bring anyone home.

One proxy representative objected. "We are placing seedship survival in the hands of a pilot the fleet has declared a criminal."

"No," Sena said. "You are placing one tug in my hand. The seedship stays in yours."

The representative studied the split authorities. "And if Admiral Calder offers amnesty once you detach?"

"Then you should assume I might take it. Build a plan that survives me."

Okoro looked at her for a long second. "Escort authority accepted under those limits."

Sena copied the control keys to Mere and Tev. Honest John's reactor cutoff turned green on two remote boards. The small surrender of control frightened her more than the cutters did. She left the keys active.

Calder heard the exchange. "Vale, stand down. Your tug lacks thermal margin and your medical file bars acceleration."

"My file also said I died."

"That error has been corrected."

"Keep correcting things."

She pushed off the operations table before Okoro could stop her.

Honest John's airlock took nineteen seconds to cycle. Sena used fourteen to strap a stimulant patch below her ear and five to call Tev on a private maintenance channel.

His face appeared before she reached the helm. He had a coolant hood tied at his neck and black grease across one cheek.

"I saw your register," he said.

"Then save me the confession. Number-two race ground a crescent into the cold side. I reset to your pencil line."

"How many threads?"

"Two."

"Vane angle?"

"Half deployment."

"Half gives you thirty-eight minutes at sixty percent reactor before lubricant boil. Less if you shadow the seedship plume."

"I need twenty."

"You always need less than the machine will cost."

"Can you watch the bearing?"

Tev glanced away, checking who might hear. "Calder sealed my outbound channels."

"This one still works."

"Because you called from a tug the fleet marked dead."

"Will you watch?"

He opened a diagnostic pane. "Send it. I can give you temperatures. I cannot give you permission."

"Temperatures will do."

Sena dropped into the helm seat. The wooden box remained in the drawer below her knees. She did not open it. She strapped her right wrist to the yoke, locked her injured arm against the acceleration web, and brought Honest John's reactor out of cold sleep.

The tug answered with a vibration through the seat. Pumps spun. The number-two radiator moved to half deployment and stopped on the manual setting. Tev's grease-pencil line held.

"Thirty seconds," Okoro said over the escort link.

Sena released the cradle clamps. Honest John drifted clear of Everspring on compressed gas, too close for hot thrust. The seedship's hull slid past her canopy: matte shielding, maintenance scars, hand-painted service numbers, and a row of dark windows meant for people who would not wake until a different sky existed.

Quiet Reed fired its tow bridle.

The cable launcher flashed white. Its clamp crossed the gap toward Everspring's forward truss, steering vanes making small corrections. Sena used Honest John's cold jets and moved into its path.

"Sena," Tev said, "that clamp outweighs your cockpit."

"It needs a conductive target."

She rolled the tug to present an external cargo rail. The clamp's magnetic jaws found Honest John and closed. Impact snapped her against the harness. Pain flooded the bound arm. The tug spun twelve degrees before its attitude jets caught.

The tow cable tightened between Honest John and Quiet Reed.

"Attached," Sena said. "Not to the seedship."

She blew the cargo rail's explosive bolts. The rail left Honest John's hull with the clamp still gripping it. Cable recoil pulled the useless assembly back toward Quiet Reed, forcing the cutter to vent thrust and avoid its own line.

Everspring ignited.

A seedship did not leap. Its drive built force through a structure carrying eighteen thousand four hundred sleepers and ecosystems packed for another world. The hull flexed along kilometers of truss. Acceleration rose by careful tenths. Suspension telemetry stayed green, then amber in two older banks.

Honest John lit sixty percent reactor and followed.

The first push drove Sena into the web. Her injured shoulder tried to separate from her body. She kept her right wrist locked to the yoke and flew from pressure through her palm. No stars crossed the canopy. Only instrument marks showed speed accumulating in the dark.

"Vane temperature climbing," Tev said. "Cold stop holding."

"Time."

"Thirty-four minutes to lubricant boil at present load. Thirty if you enter the plume."

Linden's Edge moved on the seedship's aft quarter. Its bridle launcher tracked the drive-control truss. Sena could not cross behind Everspring without taking exhaust scatter. She pushed above the seedship's plane, rolled Honest John nose-down, and used three seconds of full thrust to make the cutter recalculate collision risk.

The radiator alarm lit before Tev spoke.

"You exceeded the hinge load."

"I noticed."

"Vane angle lost four degrees. Reduce reactor."

Sena cut to fifty-two percent. Honest John fell half a kilometer behind. The cutter continued closing. Its tow clamp opened.

Okoro sent the seedship's local navigation plot. Sena let her controller, a quiet bridge pilot named Mere, place a safe branch on Honest John's board. It curved outside the drive plume and gave up distance to keep the tug cool.

"Take it," Mere said.

Sena wanted the shorter line. The shorter line let her cross the cutter's nose and force another abort. It also passed through exhaust scatter. Jori would have taken it. Sena's memory of Jori always offered speed first and consequences later.

She accepted Mere's branch.

Honest John descended beneath the seedship. Heat load eased by three percent. Linden's Edge fired from too far out. Its clamp reached the aft truss with little steering reserve, struck armor, and failed to seat. The cable reeled back.

"Good plot," Sena told Mere.

"You flew it."

"Keep sending them."

Calder transmitted on the open command band. "Everspring, cut drive. Pilot Vale, break escort and hold for recovery. No weapon has fired. Do not force escalation."

Okoro answered, "No weapon needed to fire. Your order already did the killing."

"You have crossed the quarantine line under thrust. I will convene captains for pursuit authority."

"Convene them behind us."

The seedship continued its burn.

At minute thirteen, the forward coolant trunk alarm sounded. The seed mirror package had absorbed more drive heat than its storage blankets could radiate. Thermal cameras showed a pale line spreading across one lattice panel.

"Mirror temperature above annealing limit," the seedship engineer said. "We need to dump memory coolant."

"Dumping costs the settlement archive reserve," Okoro replied.

"Not dumping costs the lattice."

Sena watched the pale line divide. A mirror built to send knowledge across fifty-three years carried tolerances measured in fractions of a degree. It had spent the launch folded against a seedship hull, waiting to open under Serein's gravity. Now military geometry and escape heat had reached it before any future message could.

The first fracture registered as a dry pulse through the escort telemetry. Then three neighboring cells lost phase lock.

"Seed mirror sector seven cracked," the engineer said. "Coolant venting now."

Silver vapor streamed from Everspring's forward package and crystallized behind it. The vent lowered lattice temperature, but every kilogram left the settlement reserve forever.

Honest John's radiator answered with its own alarm. The reset bearing had begun shedding ceramic grains into the race. Tev's diagnostic trace jumped in steps.

"Twenty minutes are gone," he said. "You have nine before the vane locks."

"Seedship burn ends in six."

"Then you get three for mistakes."

Behind them, Quiet Reed recovered its cable. Linden's Edge rolled its hot side away and opened radiators. Neither cutter could match the next increment without cooling. Farther back, defense ships altered formation one by one. Drive points appeared on Sena's plot like sparks struck from cold metal.

Calder's flag signal changed from quarantine enforcement to pursuit preparation.

Sena held Mere's next safe branch. Her hand shook against the yoke, whether from acceleration or the stimulant she could not tell. The seedship's burn clock fell through ninety seconds. Suspension banks stayed amber but stable.

At cutoff, Everspring carried six new meters per second toward Serein. Six looked small on a tactical display. Applied to its mass and the remaining corridor, it moved every tow solution out of reach.

Honest John shut down to cooling power. The radiator vane stopped one degree short of its commanded angle. Sena marked it failed-open enough to live.

The seedship coasted ahead, coolant crystals bright on the black behind its cracked mirror package. Sena matched its damaged side with the tug and accepted another plot from Mere.

One by one, the fleet's pursuit drives lit in their wake. The distance numbers began changing, and none of them promised escape.

Chapter 8 — The Orbit of Nobody

The security officer removed Iven's wrist seal only after cartography locked every command surface.

Their main table woke in observation mode. Routes crossed it in dull gray, stripped of the colors that indicated edit authority. Everspring moved toward Serein on the curve created by its six-meter-per-second escape burn. Pursuit ships spread behind it according to drive tolerance, heat capacity, and the willingness of their captains to obey before the captains' council had voted.

Admiral Calder stood at the far edge of the table. Commander Pell occupied Iven's usual station with a command key inserted. Tev Arin waited beside the thermal console carrying a paper binder against his chest.

"Chief Mora," Calder said, "your suspension remains active. You will evaluate one maneuver. You will not transmit, annotate the fleet product, or open the isolated packet."

"Understood."

"Say what happens if we catch the seedship."

Pell restored color to a corridor on the display. Two cutters would overtake Everspring above the outer edge of Nox's debris fan. A third ship would place low-energy laser fire on the seedship's drive-control relays if it refused tow. Once disabled, the cutters would attach fore and aft, rotate the long hull, then force it into a braking corridor behind the convoy.

The route looked clean. No debris clusters crossed it. The tow pair shared load within six percent. The final curve ended outside the Serein commitment gate with enough time for a captains' review.

Iven read the assumptions instead of the line. "You used defense-cutter heat limits."

"The cutters execute the burn," Pell said.

"They tow the seedship through it."

"The seedship's drive stays cold."

"Its systems do not."

Calder pointed toward Tev. "That objection brought him here. Prove it or clear the route."

Tev laid the binder on Iven's table. Metal corners had worn through its black cover. A loop of faded green cord held it shut. No modern maintenance record needed paper, but Everspring expected to wake after decades with failed storage, incompatible terminals, and no depot. Seedships carried instructions that could survive a dead battery.

"This came off the commissioning copy in Aster Vale's thermal archive," Tev said. "Okoro's crew sent revisions before quarantine closed their engineering channel."

Pell looked at the binder. "You had live contact after the annex release?"

"Before the boundary went active. The revision packet arrived as scheduled maintenance traffic."

"And your later contact with Vale?"

Tev met Calder's gaze. "You can hold that inquiry after the radiator inquiry."

Calder did not accept the deflection. She also did not spend time on it. "Open the relevant system."

Tev untied the cord. Foldout diagrams spread across the table, too large for the magnetic surface. Iven caught one edge before spin ventilation lifted it. The sheets showed Everspring's four heat-rejection wings, suspension coolant trunks, seed-mirror storage loop, and emergency sink tanks distributed through the central spine.

Ink covered the margins. Some notes carried engineering values. Others carried names.

LINA SEFT, BANK 12 PUMP TREE.

OMAR ILEN, WING 3 MANUAL PURGE.

DAVI AND REN KO, MIRROR COOLANT CROSSFEED.

Dozens more continued down every fold. The handwriting changed from line to line. Some letters leaned hard enough to score the paper. One name had a small leaf drawn beside it.

Pell touched Lina Seft's entry. "Maintenance staff?"

"Future maintenance staff," Tev said. "Sleepers. Each person trained on a system before entering suspension. They signed the hard copy beside the station they expect to service after waking. If a valve has no assigned sleeper, the ship carries an instructional model instead."

"Why use names rather than bank numbers?" Calder asked.

"Because bank numbers change when medical rebalances suspension loads. People keep their names."

Tev turned to the forward cooling tree. "These handwritten maintenance names represent sleepers who know specific failure routes. They are not decorative. Lina can bypass the bank twelve pump tree from memory. Omar knows which purge valve sticks below seventy kelvin. The Ko siblings can separate the mirror loop without losing the habitat archive. None of them can do that while frozen."

Iven followed the lines. The abstract manifest on Calder's annex had become a repair crew sleeping inside its own machine. The map offered no symbol for that relation.

"Show us the braking load," they said.

Tev pinned three diagrams and connected his diagnostic wafer. Pell allowed read-only access to the thermal console. The simulation began with Everspring's current damage: seed mirror sector seven fractured, forward coolant reserve reduced by the vent, two older suspension banks warm from the escape burn, and a radiator wing carrying a deployment fault logged six months earlier.

"That fault passed inspection," Pell said.

"For independent flight. Tow rotation loads the wing sideways."

He ran Calder's corridor.

The model rotated the seedship between two cutter vectors. At eleven degrees from its present attitude, the forward mirror loop absorbed reflected drive heat from the lead cutter. At seventeen, debris shielding blocked one radiator wing's view of cold space. At twenty-three, towing torque flexed the central spine enough to close a coolant check valve near bank twelve.

Amber warnings filled the table.

"Reduce tow rate," Calder said.

Pell halved rotation. The seedship remained longer in the cutters' exhaust geometry. Mirror coolant crossed annealing limit again.

"Move the lead cutter outward."

That reduced reflected heat and increased cable angle. The aft bridle then loaded the damaged radiator truss beyond commissioning tolerance.

Tev pulled a grease pencil from the binder cord and marked values directly on a paper overlay. "Digital model assumes both wing-three hinge motors answer. One failed its cold test during launch. The crew left it deployed because they had dock support. Escape vibration may have shifted it. Use one motor."

Pell changed the value. The wing stalled at sixty-one percent.

Red spread from the suspension loop into three banks. The simulation killed its own tow burn after predicting coolant vapor in bank twelve.

Calder leaned over the model. "Kill the mirror package."

"Storage loop cannot detach while folded," Tev said.

"Vent it."

"They already spent reserve during the escape. Full vent saves the mirror and starves two suspension banks of emergency coolant."

"Then sacrifice the mirror."

Tev changed the priority. The fractured lattice heated until local supports softened. Its storage frame warped into the forward coolant trunk and blocked flow to the central spine. Predicted failures cascaded more slowly but reached the same banks.

Iven watched Lina Seft's name beside the first red pump tree.

"How many survive?" Calder asked.

"Thermal models do not count deaths," Tev said.

"The suspension model does."

Pell opened casualty estimates. Ranges appeared, each wider than command preferred. At best, bank twelve lost several dozen sleepers to warming injuries. At median conditions, coolant loss propagated through three banks. The worst branch exceeded two thousand before cutters could stabilize the spine.

Tev did not accept the first run. He opened the maintenance-response layer and set awake crew strength to Everspring's present roster. The model assigned a general engineer to bank twelve, then directed that engineer to isolate the failed check valve through a service tunnel behind the suspension shell.

"That tunnel stays sealed while the bank holds pressure," Tev said. "Opening it vents the service atmosphere across two compartments."

Pell checked the digital procedure. "Archive says accessible under emergency authorization."

"Archive assumes Lina Seft has woken and can close the hidden bypass before anyone opens the tunnel."

He unfolded Lina's sheet. Beside her name, a pencil sketch showed three valves, one crossed out, and a route entering from the opposite side of the pump tree. The digital archive had scanned the words but not the pressure marks in the drawing.

"Why did commissioning approve a procedure stored in one sleeper's memory?" Calder asked.

"It did not. The approved procedure works after normal thaw, when the tree sits at habitat pressure. Lina found a cold-state shortcut during training and added it by hand. Seedships keep human knowledge because fifty years of storage always outlives part of the approved manual."

Pell entered the shortcut. Casualties fell in one branch, then rose again because no awake crew member could identify the crossed-out valve from outside the sealed tunnel.

Tev moved to Omar Ilen's purge note. Omar's penciled instruction prevented a radiator line from hammering when the ship rotated. Without him, the model chose an automated purge that consumed twice the expected coolant. Tev tried the Ko crossfeed next. Its training record required two people at separated manual wheels, turning by voice count after the central controller went dark.

"Can the awake crew follow these pages?" Iven asked.

"Some. Not during forced rotation with cutters pulling at both ends. Every manual exception assumes the hull can hold attitude while people work."

The handwritten names did more than represent lives. They marked missing actions inside the thermal model, tasks assigned to people command had reduced to cargo. Calder's corridor demanded their knowledge while keeping them unable to use it.

Tev reran the tow with every undocumented shortcut removed. Red reached bank twelve forty seconds earlier.

"Those ranges assume no weapon damage," Iven said.

Pell added Calder's low-energy drive-relay strike. Scatter heated the forward hull. One relay failed open rather than closed, forcing local crew to cut power through an external access point while under tow. Casualty estimates widened again.

Calder looked toward Tev. "Can Everspring survive any forced braking route before the commitment gate?"

"Yes," he said.

Pell's attention snapped to him. "You just proved this one kills them."

"You asked any route. They can brake under their own drive, with radiators facing cold space, after the cracked mirror loop has been isolated. That takes repair time and cooperation."

"How much time?"

"At least fourteen hours for isolation if the awake crew accepts losing half the settlement archive reserve. Longer if the Ko crossfeed procedure requires access behind the folded package."

"The Ko sleepers cannot perform it," Calder said.

"Their handwritten instructions can. Maybe. The awake crew will work from those pages."

Calder moved the pursuit clock beside the thermal model. Fourteen hours pushed the intercept near the commitment gate. If Everspring refused to brake after repairs, the fleet would have less room to compel it.

"You have proved my planned corridor unacceptable," she said. "You have not given me a route that enforces the warning."

Tev closed the casualty pane. "Machines do not owe you a moral option."

The security officer shifted near the door. Calder studied Tev for a second, then returned to Iven.

"Map an intercept that keeps the seedship in independent thermal attitude," she said. "No tow rotation. No reflected drive plume. Preserve a laser line to the control relays."

Iven looked at the gray controls. "You suspended my access."

"Pell will enter your work."

"Then it becomes his map."

"Does that make it less accurate?"

"It makes responsibility harder to see."

Calder's patience thinned. "You admitted concealing the annex less than two hours ago. I will not restore command access because you object to muddy attribution. Give Pell the route."

Iven began with the seedship's true state vector. Its escape burn had shifted it above the convoy's old survey plane. The fastest pursuit line crossed the edge of the mineral-noise region extending from Lumen's orbital wake. Chapterless survey products still marked the interference as geological weather. Sensor returns there carried timing errors, false edges, and phase delays that changed with microwave storms.

Pell traced the direct line. "We can tolerate that noise at fleet separation."

"The tow cutters can," Iven said. "Your laser line cannot."

They built a cone from Sorn's low-energy strike plan. Firing through the interference enlarged aim uncertainty at the drive-control truss. A miss could touch the seed mirror, suspension shielding, or empty space. The cone looked bad enough to stop a careful captain, but not bad enough to force a detour.

Calder saw the same thing. "Use independent optical tracking inside the storm edge."

"Lumen has no visible light source. You would track hull occultation against Nox lensing."

"Then use convoy beacons."

"Latency and geometry leave a blind interval."

"How long?"

"Twenty-seven seconds."

"Acceptable."

Iven knew twenty-seven seconds did not buy enough. Everspring needed repair time. Tev's fourteen hours lay beyond any honest uncertainty the map could create.

They opened the raw survey archive from the day before. Access restrictions permitted observation, not editing. Lumen's weather traces crawled across the display in pale bands. One trace had answered a passive sweep with a phase echo displaced from every mineral model. Iven had set it aside for later study. Now it crossed the pursuit corridor at an angle no tactical map displayed.

"What does that return do to ranging?" Calder asked.

"Unknown."

"That word does not map a fleet."

"It maps this region better than a number would."

Pell enlarged the trace. "Can it spoof a hull return?"

"It can repeat energy at a shifted phase. We have not tested whether the shift remains stable under an active ping."

"Then mark a sensor exclusion and route the ships outside it," Calder said.

The order created an opening. An exclusion zone carried a boundary. Boundaries depended on confidence thresholds chosen by a cartographer.

Iven constructed the zone from the raw trace. The honest contour reached forty thousand kilometers at low confidence. They extended it by applying the storm's maximum observed phase shift to every unsampled sector. The resulting shape swallowed the direct pursuit line and pushed Calder's ships onto a high arc.

Pell read the metadata. "You used maximum propagation."

"We have no basis for a mean."

"Survey practice uses bounded median when a phenomenon lacks hostile intent."

"We do not know its intent."

Calder looked sharply at them. "Geological weather has no intent."

Iven kept their face still. "Then bounded median would be appropriate after an active test. Until then, maximum propagation protects the firing line from false phase."

Every sentence remained defensible. Together they made a route Iven did not believe. The storm might disrupt a laser solution, but no data justified the full exclusion contour. The high arc would cost pursuit ships forty-six minutes and extra reaction mass. It would not give Everspring fourteen hours. It might give the seedship enough time to finish mirror isolation before the closest cutter regained a line.

Calder sent the proposed route to weapons analysis. "Run it."

Pell held his hand above the transfer control. "This product requires a cartographer's uncertainty seal. I can enter it, but I cannot certify the survey interpretation."

"Mora certifies under witness restriction," Calder said.

The table projected a one-use seal prompt. Iven's name appeared with a red border. They could approve an advisory layer, not a command route. Pell would still need to adopt it into the fleet map.

Tev gathered his papers. Lina Seft's name disappeared under the folded corner, then returned when he adjusted the sheet. He had proved that a clean line through empty space could kill people through heat alone. Iven had answered with an unclean line intended to preserve them.

Their mother's voice came back without sound: build honest maps instead.

An honest map of present authority would show Calder's weapons, Everspring's refusal, and Iven's restricted hand poised above a false contour. It would show no route without ownership. It would not absolve the hand.

"Chief Mora," Calder said. "Seal or reject."

Iven pressed their palm to the prompt.

They changed one field before sealing. SURVEY EXCLUSION, PROVISIONAL became NAVIGATION HAZARD, ACTIVE. The first label advised. The second forced automated route planners to avoid the zone until a command cartographer cleared it.

Their one-use seal accepted the change.

Pell noticed. "Active hazard requires confirmed effect on navigation."

"The phase displacement affects ranging."

"We have not confirmed magnitude."

"Then reject my certification."

Calder examined the delay estimate. Forty-six minutes. Pursuit ships would remain outside the uncertain region, preserve laser geometry, and spend more fuel. No tactical argument favored the direct line unless command accepted blind fire near sleeper banks.

"Adopt it," she said.

Pell did not move. "Admiral."

"We asked for a route that protects the seedship's thermal attitude and preserves a controlled firing line. This route does both. Adopt it."

He entered his command key. The gray advisory contour turned amber. A route packet left cartography and propagated through the pursuit net.

Ship acknowledgments arrived in order of distance. Quiet Reed accepted first. Linden's Edge recalculated and reported fuel cost. Two farther cutters requested clarification, received Calder's command seal, and accepted. The flag plot bent into the high arc.

Iven watched the false route become operational fact. Drive vectors changed by fractions of a degree. Over distance, those fractions opened thousands of kilometers.

Calder removed the one-use seal from the table. "Return Chief Mora to restricted status. Tev, leave the binder."

"No," Tev said. "Digital copies are in the thermal file. The paper returns to archive custody."

"It supports a command decision."

"It belongs to the people whose names sit inside it. You can requisition a copy."

Calder looked at the worn cover, then nodded. "Take it."

The security officer restored Iven's wrist seal. Editing controls faded back to gray. On the main table, pursuit ships continued their accepted detour while Everspring held its real course toward Serein.

Pell closed the raw survey archive but left the fleet product visible. "If the storm does not affect them, every captain will know this map overstated the hazard."

"Yes," Iven said.

"You confessed to one concealment this morning."

"I remember."

Tev retied the green cord across the handwritten names. The paper edges kept the creases of hands that expected to wake beside these machines. Calder said nothing more. She had the route she requested, and Iven had bought forty-six minutes with a line no empty space had earned.

The final pursuit acknowledgment arrived from Resolute Vigil. ROUTE ACCEPTED. EXECUTION ACTIVE.

Beyond the locked table, ships began turning toward an orbit that existed only because Iven had drawn it.

Chapter 9 — The Map Without Descendants

Quiet Reed fired the survey ping before Iven reached the cartography door.

The pulse crossed the Serein plot as a thin white line. It left the cutter's forward array, spread through the region Iven had marked hazardous, and struck Lumen's mineral-weather wake twenty-three seconds later. The table predicted a weak return after another twenty-three. If the return stayed within ordinary phase limits, Pell could clear part of the exclusion zone and recover eleven minutes of pursuit time.

Iven stood inside the doorway with their wrist seal still active. The security officer waited behind them. Commander Pell had left for Calder's captains' council, but his command key remained in the table under remote lock. Observation surfaces stayed gray.

"Am I confined to quarters or cartography?" Iven asked.

The officer checked her instruction slate. "Cartography counts as your duty confinement until Commander Pell releases you. No transmissions. No edits."

"The table is receiving a live test of my map."

"You may observe."

Iven entered. The door sealed, placing the officer on the corridor side. Somebody had cleared Tev's paper diagrams, but one grease-pencil shaving remained caught in the table seam. The accepted high route glowed amber above it. Four pursuit ships followed that route. Everspring held its own line toward Serein, gaining seconds whenever a defense ship spent thrust on the detour.

The ping timer reached forty-six seconds.

Nothing returned.

At fifty-one seconds, tactical filters reported MINERAL SCATTER, BELOW NAVIGATION THRESHOLD. The active hazard contour began contracting automatically. Iven watched its outer boundary withdraw twelve thousand kilometers.

Then the raw receiver pane filled with weather.

Pale bands rose from Lumen's wake at six frequencies. They did not arrive as one reflection. Each frequency carried a different phase delay, as if the moon's silicate storms had caught pieces of the pulse in separate layers and released them after unequal travel. The tactical filter averaged them into noise. The hazard boundary contracted another two thousand kilometers.

Iven touched the raw pane. The wrist seal blocked them.

"Observation magnification," they said.

The table enlarged the bands without granting edit control. The first carried Quiet Reed's outgoing pulse in reverse frequency order. The second carried only its even-indexed segments. The third resembled the passive sweep Iven had noticed the day before, but its low-frequency edge had shifted by exactly one sampling interval.

A geological echo could sort frequencies. Crystal layers did it whenever a wave crossed uneven pressure. An echo could drop odd segments through destructive interference. It could retain charge and release it after a delay.

It could not have heard yesterday's passive sweep inside today's pulse.

Iven requested the old raw file. Observation authority opened it beside the live return. They aligned both traces. A bright ridge in the new data matched the previous sweep down to receiver noise from Aster Vale's own array. The ridge could only come from recorded energy. Lumen's weather had retained the sweep, altered its interval, and included it in the answer to a new signal from another ship.

"Reclassify contraction pending contamination check," Iven told the table.

ACCESS DENIED.

The tactical product reduced the hazard again. Quiet Reed received permission to approach the former boundary.

Iven called the security officer through the door intercom. "I need Commander Pell."

"He marked council interruption emergency-only."

"A live survey result contradicts the automated classification."

"Does it endanger a ship?"

Iven looked at the high route. If they said yes, Pell would interrupt the council and open the raw return to weapons analysis. Every captain would see the region as an active threat while arguing whether to pursue Everspring. If they said no, Quiet Reed might enter a phenomenon nobody understood.

"Not at present distance," they said. "Ask him to call when available."

The officer entered the request without emergency priority.

Iven returned to the table. Their seal allowed analysis tools that did not alter records. They opened a correlation workspace and fed it the two traces. The first result reported an eighty-three percent match between yesterday's receiver noise and the bright ridge. Too low for proof. They removed known instrument drift. Match rose to ninety-one. They corrected for relative motion between Aster Vale, Quiet Reed, and Lumen. Ninety-six.

At ninety-six percent, contamination remained possible. The survey archive might have copied the old trace into a calibration channel. A software loop could have placed it beside the new return. Iven traced data custody from receiver to table. Different ships had collected the signals. Separate clocks stamped them. No shared buffer existed before fleet relay storage.

They needed a feature no faulty archive could invent.

The new response contained seven quiet gaps. Six divided the frequency bands. The seventh sat inside the copied ridge where yesterday's sweep had suffered a transmitter dropout. Iven enlarged that gap. Yesterday it had lasted eleven milliseconds. The response held it for twenty-two.

Lumen's weather had not merely replayed the signal. It had doubled an absence.

Iven built a simple transform: reverse the six live frequencies, remove odd pulse segments, shift the stored sweep by one interval, double its missing section. Applied to the outgoing ping and archived sweep together, the transform reproduced ninety-eight percent of the response.

No mineral model in the survey library produced that sequence.

They opened piezoelectric storm literature. Silicate canyons under tidal stress could store microwave charge. Layered crystals could sustain standing patterns for years. Thermal cycling could move those patterns between strata. Several papers proposed geological memory in the weak sense: past energy changed future response. None demonstrated ordered retrieval from two separate inputs.

The distinction mattered. A scar remembered impact by remaining a scar. A switch remembered state by occupying one of two positions. This weather retained signals, separated them, transformed their structure, and joined old input to new.

Iven labeled a private workspace NONRANDOM RETENTION TEST.

Their wrist seal allowed the label because private analysis carried no command effect.

Quiet Reed reached the first contracted boundary. Its captain requested clearance to fire a stronger ranging pulse. Automation approved.

"Cancel pending review," Iven said.

COMMAND AUTHORITY REQUIRED.

They tried Pell again. Council interruption remained emergency-only. The security officer opened the intercom.

"Chief, do you need me to stop the ship physically?"

"Can you?"

"No."

"Then no."

The second survey ping carried four times the first pulse energy. It used a coded sequence designed to distinguish hulls from debris: three short bursts, one long, two short, then a frequency climb. Every tactical array in the fleet knew the code.

The pulse entered Lumen's wake.

Iven started a new raw capture before the table's filter could average it. Observation mode permitted temporary calibration buffers. The buffer would erase itself after ten minutes unless attached to an authorized product.

The return began early.

A low-frequency band reached Quiet Reed before a simple reflection could have completed the round trip. The cutter's system rejected it as local interference. Another band arrived on time. A third came late. The tactical filter stacked all three and reported variable mineral depth.

Iven separated them by arrival time. The early band did not contain the second ping. It carried a transformed copy of the first. The on-time band carried the second ping with its long burst moved to the end. The late band held yesterday's passive sweep again, but this time the old transmitter gap appeared once at its original duration, once doubled, and once four times as long.

One absence had become a sequence.

Eleven. Twenty-two. Forty-four milliseconds.

The weather had taken the missing interval and extended it by powers of two.

Iven sat down. Cartography chairs locked to the deck, but their knees had stopped trusting the weak spin gravity.

Nonrandom did not mean conscious. A crystal oscillator produced ordered ratios without knowing them. A feedback loop could double a period. Repetition could arise from physics alone.

Yet this sequence selected the only feature shared by neither carrier frequency nor pulse code. It selected a break made by a human transmitter a day earlier. It preserved that break, then manipulated its duration when a new signal arrived.

Iven searched the old Serein surveys for the same spacing.

The archive reached back seventeen years. Early probes had logged microwave storms as calibration failures and compressed most raw data. Enough survived. A survey drone had emitted a seven-millisecond timing check. Its return contained fourteen and twenty-eight millisecond gaps, dismissed as packet loss. A later probe carried a nine-millisecond fault. Eighteen appeared in a ring-scatter record. Thirty-six appeared in an altimetry failure six hours later.

Different gaps. The same doubling relation.

The weather had been answering for years. Human filters had classified every answer according to the instrument that failed to understand it.

Iven built a table of source pulse, retained absence, transformed intervals, receiving location, and elapsed time. The pattern crossed separate storm cells and survived orbital seasons. No single crystal seam connected all observations. Retention moved through Lumen's weather bands.

They forced themselves to attack the result before naming it. A resonant cavity could double intervals if each discharge triggered a slower harmonic. Iven generated ten thousand layered-canyon models and allowed pressure, temperature, crystal orientation, and tidal stress to vary across every measured range. Hundreds produced one doubled gap. Nine produced two. None selected the transmitter dropout from three unrelated surveys while ignoring stronger pulse features.

Relay contamination offered a simpler explanation. Fleet software might stamp old timing faults into every file it touched. Iven rebuilt the chain without fleet relay data, using raw probe fragments recovered from maintenance checksums. The seven-millisecond source survived in a drone's local crystal log. Its fourteen-millisecond answer survived on a different recorder. Those devices had never shared code, storage, or clock correction.

They tested deliberate human fabrication next. Seventeen years of survey crews gave plenty of opportunity for a joke, a hidden calibration signature, or fraud intended to win research funding. Crew rosters showed no person common to all three missions. The earliest probe had died before the latest transmitter design existed.
