Its response still anticipated the same operation later signals received.

One alternative remained: selection by Iven. They had searched thousands of faults and might have chosen the few that fit doubling. To test that, they asked the archive for every preserved dropout above five milliseconds, not merely the examples already noticed. Forty-one source gaps appeared. Thirty-eight had later returns. Thirty-one displayed doubled duration within receiver tolerance. Four showed tripling mixed with doubling. Three produced no stable relation.

Chance probability fell below the survey service's threshold for nonrandom structure even after its strictest correction.

Iven saved the failed models beside the transform table. Proof required the routes that did not work. Without them, a clever pattern could become a map drawn to flatter its maker.

The analysis still could not cross the final distance from structured response to intention. It did something more limited and more difficult to dismiss. It showed persistent selection operating across separated mineral storms, preserving particular features of human signals and applying repeatable changes to them.

The map beneath their analysis showed Serein, Lumen, debris rings, and routes for human ships. It treated every orbit as empty unless a human hull occupied it. Survey law reserved inhabited status for biosignatures, artifacts, or intentional communication confirmed by independent teams.

They had no biology. They had no artifact distinct from the moon. Intent remained unproven.

They had memory distributed through geological weather, with ordered responses to signals and continuity across seventeen years.

It constituted the first evidence that the Serein system was inhabited.

Iven did not enter that sentence into the fleet product.

The temporary buffer timer fell through seven minutes. They needed to preserve proof before it erased itself. Command save required Pell's key. Public survey publication would send the raw returns to every ship, including weapons stations already treating the region as a hazard. Their inquiry status offered one other route.

Any witness under active Compact review could submit potentially exculpatory evidence into a write-once legal hold. The evidence became inaccessible to command until an advocate and inquiry officer verified scope. Iven had used that safeguard for their annex notes.

They opened the submission form.

RELATION TO INQUIRY, the form requested.

Iven could claim the response affected forecast provenance. That stretched truth. The pattern came from Lumen, not from the fifty-three-year return channel. It might illuminate why the final warning feared Everspring's arrival, but no evidence connected them yet.

They typed: SURVEY DATA BEARING ON SEREIN INSERTION RISK AND THE CONSEQUENCES OF DELAYED ANNEX DISCLOSURE.

True, but incomplete.

The form required their advocate's countersignature. Jo Venn answered on the third attempt, voice only.

"I am entering a captains' chamber in two minutes," she said. "What are you submitting?"

"Raw mineral-weather returns from the route Calder accepted. The automated filter is destroying information."

"Does the information help your defense?"

"It may show the Serein risk cannot be reduced to the warning."

"May?"

"I need independent review."

Venn heard the evasion. "Are you asking me to preserve evidence or help you hide it?"

The buffer timer showed five minutes.

"Preserve it," Iven said. "Restrict it from tactical distribution until a survey specialist can separate signal from contamination."

"Does Calder need it to keep a ship safe right now?"

"Quiet Reed has already crossed the outer contour. The returns have not shown destructive energy."

"That did not answer the question."

"No present evidence requires evasive action."

Venn stayed silent long enough for Iven to hear the captains' chamber doors opening on her end. "I will countersign preservation, not your scope description. Add that the data may indicate an unknown signal process."

Iven added the words.

Venn's key entered the form. The legal hold copied the raw first and second returns, source pings, custody clocks, old survey matches, and Iven's transform table. Hashes appeared beside each item. The write-once archive sealed before the temporary buffer reached four minutes.

A preservation receipt printed from the table's evidence slot on thin ceramic paper. Iven checked every hash against the receiver logs, then added the failed mineral models and the full dropout census. The hold now carried both the apparent answer and the strongest attempts to explain it without one. Two storage nodes accepted independent copies. Neither fed the tactical net. If one node failed during pursuit, the other would retain the phase records without compression.

Iven signed the receipt by hand. Their restricted credential could not alter the archive after sealing, which also meant they could not improve the claim later by deleting an inconvenient trace. The proof had left their control even while its interpretation remained private.

"Who knows what you think this is?" Venn asked.

"Only me."

"Keep it that way until review. You do not get another private disclosure decision merely because the last one had consequences you dislike."

"You just agreed to restrict it."

"I agreed to preserve uncertain evidence from tactical misuse. I did not agree that you alone decide what it means. I will appoint a reviewer after the council."

The channel ended.

Fleet automation finished processing the second return. Its summary entered the map: PIEZOELECTRIC MINERAL INTERFERENCE. STABLE UNDER ACTIVE RANGING. NO DIRECT HULL HAZARD CONFIRMED.

The statement cleared another section of Iven's exclusion zone. Pursuit routes shortened by eight minutes. Calder's forty-six-minute delay fell to thirty-eight.

Pell called from the captains' corridor. His image appeared on a side pane, breath quick from walking under spin.

"Report," he said.

Iven looked at the sealed legal hold indicator. Its contents no longer appeared on their table. Only the title remained, amended by Venn: UNKNOWN SIGNAL PROCESS, SURVEY REVIEW REQUIRED.

"The active test shows stable piezoelectric interference," Iven said. "No direct hull hazard. Automated contraction has reduced the zone."

"Do you agree with the contraction?"

"Not without specialist review. The phase behavior exceeds current mineral models."

Pell glanced offscreen. Voices from the council entry called for him. "Does it threaten navigation?"

"It corrupts ranging and retains input longer than predicted. The high route remains prudent until review."

"Retains input how?"

Iven chose each word. "Charge storage across layered storm cells. I placed raw returns in evidence hold because filters discarded phase structure. Venn countersigned."

"You used inquiry privilege on fleet survey data?"

"The raw data remains preserved. The tactical summary reached you."

"That privilege will not survive a second abuse."

"Then review it quickly."

Pell looked ready to ask more. The captains' chamber called his name again. "Send me the hold receipt. No interpretation outside counsel."

Iven transmitted the receipt. It contained hashes and scope, not evidence. Pell left for the council.

The fleet map continued treating Lumen as weather. Quiet Reed advanced inside the old contour. Linden's Edge followed on a cooler line. Far ahead, Everspring held course with its cracked seed mirror and eighteen thousand four hundred sleeping people.

Iven reopened the seventeen-year survey index in private observation mode. Without the sealed raw returns, the old records looked ordinary again: packet loss, delayed phase, mineral scatter, instrument fault. The labels covered the pattern like dust on etched glass.

They wrote the doubled intervals on a paper calibration strip because their wrist seal could not erase paper. Seven, fourteen, twenty-eight. Nine, eighteen, thirty-six. Eleven, twenty-two, forty-four. They folded the strip once and placed it under the grease-pencil shaving in the table seam.

Nothing on the fleet plot marked an inhabited system. No contact icon appeared beside Lumen. No alarm reached Calder's council. The moon remained a gray sphere crossed by amber weather bands.

A third return arrived after the ping sequence had ended. Tactical filters did not display it. The raw receiver showed one low pulse, then two, then four, each separated by the doubled gap from yesterday's failed sweep.

Iven watched until the receiver went quiet. The legal hold kept the proof. Beyond cartography's locked door, the fleet continued its pursuit without knowing what had answered.

Interlude I — The Absent Reply

Household Testimony 17: Yara Pell, medical ring C

My son woke hot enough to make the pillow damp. I put the thermometer against his neck, waited for the tone, and held the result under the kitchen light as if brighter light could make the number smaller.

On any other morning, the pediatric advisory would already have arrived. Our household monitor sent sleep pulse, air quality, school exposure, and the previous week's meals into the family forecast queue every night. Descendant medicine usually answered before breakfast. It would name the dose, tell me whether to wake him for water, and estimate how much fear the symptoms deserved.

During the first day without advice, the monitor displayed REQUEST PENDING until the words felt insulting. I called my sister. She asked what his breathing sounded like. No advisory had ever asked me that in her voice.

We counted breaths together. I gave him the standard dose printed on the bottle, not the revised dose that might have come from fifty-three years ahead. Then I sat beside him and checked the time every ten minutes.

By noon his fever had fallen. Relief came with anger attached. I had known how to read a bottle before I became a parent. I had let a future household turn that knowledge into something ceremonial, a thing I touched only after permission.

My son woke and asked for ice. I gave him two cubes in a blue cup. The monitor still said pending. He bit one cube in half and complained that I had chosen the wrong color.

Domestic Audio 4B: Esme Tal and Rian Olt, Caul-facing grow quarters

I came home with six bruised tomatoes because the nutrient rail had overfed bed nine again. Rian had noodles boiling. Our kitchen panel waited for the evening meal correction, which normally told us how much salt his kidneys would regret and which tomato carried the lowest mold probability.

The correction never came.

Rian asked me to choose. I said he had started cooking, so the failure belonged to him. He said I had brought the tomatoes, so causality had entered with my boots. We argued for three minutes and used all six.

Dinner tasted too sharp. We added salt anyway.

I had expected the silence to frighten me. Instead I kept noticing small permissions. Nobody from our line sent a warning that I would sleep badly after fermented tea. No labor forecast advised Rian to trade tomorrow's shift because bed nine would jam again. He could keep the shift, meet the jam, and curse it at the proper time.

That relief embarrassed us both. Descendant guidance kept the hydroponics ring alive through two blights. My mother survived a fungal exposure because a treatment warning arrived before her lungs failed. We owed actual meals and actual years to people not born when the danger started.

Still, Rian ate the burnt noodles from the bottom of the pot. He likes them. The household panel always treated burnt food as preventable loss.

We left the scorched pieces in his bowl and washed the pot after midnight.

Written Deposition: Havel and Min Sori, berth habitat Patient Current

I found the old compatibility letter behind the linen drawer. Min kept paper then. She said a household promise deserved something that could not update itself while you slept.

The letter recommended our marriage. It gave us a seventy-eight percent chance of remaining together for forty years, adjusted for work transfers, illness, and likely children. We were twenty-two. Seventy-eight felt like certainty because every other choice in our lives arrived with smaller numbers.

We have reached year thirty-nine.

When the regular advisories stopped, Min put the letter on the table and asked whether staying one more year meant obeying it. I told her the question arrived late. She said lateness did not make it false.

Our household has used descendant forecasts for arguments as often as for emergencies. We uploaded sleep records before deciding who snored. We requested outcome branches before moving berths. Once, after I kissed another man, Min asked the family service whether forgiveness produced better health than separation. The service recommended a six-month repair plan. We followed it. I never learned whether she forgave me or merely completed the plan.

That afternoon we made tea and read the compatibility letter aloud. Its margins held our younger handwriting. Beside the line predicting two children, Min had written, One, and enough. Beside likely bereavement in year thirty-one, I had written, Not if I can help it. The forecast missed both.

Min tore the percentage from the bottom. She left our signatures intact.

We have not decided about year forty. Tonight she put her cold feet against my calves, and I moved over without consulting anyone.

Clinic Voice Record: Nema Voss, Jalen Voss, prenatal room six

I held the screening consent in one hand and Jalen's thumb in the other. The clinic could tell us what the fetal cells contained. It could not tell us whether the treatment we chose would create a life our daughter later thanked us for.

That second answer used to come through the family forecast service. Not certainty, the counselor always said. A modeled message from possible descendants. The distinction sounded careful until we had to sign without it.

Our screen showed a moderate cardiac risk. Early intervention might lower surgical danger and raise the chance of premature delivery. Waiting protected the pregnancy and left less time before birth. Both paths carried children in the projections. We had expected one of those children to send advice back.

Jalen wanted to wait six hours in case the service resumed. I wanted to sign before fear learned new vocabulary. We traded positions twice. The counselor brought water and did not recommend either path.

I asked her what she would do. She said she could describe outcomes, not borrow our daughter. That sentence made me dislike her, which helped because dislike gave me something smaller than terror to hold.

We chose additional imaging before intervention. It delayed treatment by four hours and gave us better measurements. No descendant approved. No warning arrived. The technician pressed the scanner to my skin while Jalen counted the chambers on the display.

Four opened and closed. I could not tell whether that meant hope, danger, or only a heart doing its work.

Communal Log, edited by Sef Iri, Dockside Household 22

I called dinner because the household schedule did not. Eleven people came. Three stayed at work, one slept through the chime, and old Toma brought a chair from the corridor because our table never has enough.

Usually the domestic forecast sorted us into two meal times. It reduced conflict, balanced the galley load, and kept Sef from sitting beside Ina after their divorce. Without it, Sef and Ina arrived together. They argued over the last lentil cake, divided it badly, and spent the rest of the meal discussing a leaking wall valve.

The valve had leaked for nine days. Maintenance advice kept postponing repair because a descendant model predicted a cheaper seal compound next month. Toma put a cup beneath it. The cup filled every six hours. We had obeyed because waiting cost only a cup.

After dinner we opened the panel. Nobody in the household held plumbing certification. Ina found the manual. Sef found a wrench. I found the shutoff and refused to touch it because the label had faded.

Fear entered then, not grand fear, just the useful kind that keeps water inside pipes. We closed the panel and called morning maintenance. The floor kept taking one drop every eleven seconds.

Toma set the cup back under the valve. Sef moved his chair beside Ina so they could hear the drops without turning their heads. We left the table crowded, dishes stacked in the sink, and the faded shutoff exactly where it had been.

Chapter 10 — The Fleet Divides

The captains' chamber on Resolute Vigil smelled of recycled air and cold sweat. Calder had not shaved since the warning arrived, and the mirror over the basin had begun to fog at the edges where the lattice heat bled through the hull. Eighteen captains sat in the curved benches. Eighteen chairs, eighteen votes, eighteen different habits of conscience.

She had read the warning aloud at 0400 and watched the chamber divide before the words were finished. The warning named Everspring and her manifest, named Serein and its second moon, named the year of arrival and the verification seal. It did not name the eighteen thousand sleepers. It did not name the children who would wake to an inhabited world. It named the cargo as if the cargo carried the danger and not the people inside it.

Calder had not slept since. She had written three drafts of a council address and torn up each one. She had reread her brother's last letter from Hespar, the one that arrived three days after the council voted and two days after the southern continent stopped answering. She had read his handwriting until the words blurred. She had not cried. Crying would have to wait for a captain who could afford it.

She stood at the long table because her back hurt less standing. The vote-tally holo floated above the table in dull amber. Eight for pursuit already, eight against, two abstaining. The missing names belonged to captains whose ships had not yet docked. Three of those ships were escorting Everspring, which had refused tow six hours ago and was running on a track the cartographers had refused to predict.

"Admiral." Captain Besh of Watchful Coil spoke without standing. He had a long jaw and a quiet voice and a habit of folding his hands on the table as if prayer could substitute for arithmetic. "The math is plain. We can reach Serein in twelve days at maximum burn. Everspring in nineteen. We cannot catch her without consent or a weapon."

"We can," said Captain Imala of Linden's Edge, leaning forward. "We can bring her about with a focused strike on her radiator vanes. Lose one vane, lose three days of braking margin. The seedship falls back into the corridor."

"You would fire on eighteen thousand sleepers to delay their arrival," Calder said.

"I would fire on the radiator vanes," Imala said. "Not on the sleepers. Sleepers ride inside. They do not die from a vane strike."

Besh tilted his head. "They die from anything that prevents Everspring from waking them at a world that will support them. Wake them at Nox and they die from a different cause."

"The vote will not be unanimous," Captain Daren of Vesper Tide said. He was the youngest of the eighteen and the most patient. "We should know that before we begin. There are captains in this chamber who remember Hespar. There are captains who have forgotten it. The vote will split on that scar."

Calder let the name sit in the room. Hespar had been a planet's evacuation ordered by a forecast council that delayed for seventy-two hours while they verified the warning. By the time the order went out, two million citizens on the southern continent had no transports left. Her brother had been one of the council who delayed. He had not survived his own vote.

"This is not Hespar," Calder said.

"It is the same council," Daren said. "Same compact, same warning, same seventy-two-hour question. We can delay and verify, or we can move on verified warning. Hespar chose delay. Hespar died."

"Hespar had a verified warning," Calder said. "We have one too. The forecast that named Everspring arrived authenticated, sealed, and uncorrupted. Every signal-check our ciphers can run confirms it."

"Then why are we voting?" Besh asked. He unfolded his hands. "If the warning is verified and the danger is verified, the doctrine is clear. We act on verified warning before we understand it. That doctrine saved Pell, saved Brennan's Reach, saved six other worlds whose names I will not list because their survival is the only monument to obedience."

"And it failed at Hespar," Daren said.

"It failed because the council delayed." Besh's voice did not rise. He had learned long ago that captains who raised their voices lost votes. "Delay was the sin. Not obedience."

Calder watched the holo. Eight amber, eight azure, two grey. The amber were captains who believed in the warning. The azure believed the warning could be wrong, or that warning itself was the danger, or that pursuing a seedship full of sleepers was not what the doctrine meant. The grey were undecided, or had decided and would not say.

"Captain Marek," Calder said. "Your ship is the closest to the corridor. What do you see?"

Captain Marek of Quiet Reed sat in the second row with his hands on his knees. He had been a cabin boy on a freighter when Hespar fell. He had lost a sister and a niece and a sister-in-law and the habit of trusting councils.

"I see a fleet," Marek said. "Eighteen hulls. Eight hundred and forty-two officers. Twelve thousand crew. Three thousand maintenance staff. Eleven thousand passengers on the freighters. We have families aboard, Admiral. We have children who have never seen a planet. We have civilians who joined this fleet because they were promised the doctrine would protect them. Hespar did not protect them. Hespar killed them because a council paused to check a number."

"Vote your conscience, Captain," Calder said.

"I am voting my conscience." Marek looked at the holo. "I vote against pursuit. Not because the warning is wrong. Because obedience to a forecast council cost two million lives once, and I will not pay that price a second time on the same coin."

The holo updated. Eight amber, nine azure, one grey.

"Admiral." Imala again. "The vote will not hold. We need a motion."

Calder stepped back from the table. She had rehearsed this in her bunk for four hours and the rehearsal had not prepared her. Doctrine said verified warning first, understanding later. Conscience said a verified warning could still be wrong. Hespar said delay killed. Pell said delay had saved three million people who would otherwise have refused evacuation.

"I motion that we pursue," Calder said. "Not to fire. To intercept, hail, and demand that Everspring explain why the warning names her. We bring her about if she cannot. We fire only if she fires first."

"You are motioning the same thing Imala proposed," Besh said.

"I am motioning a different thing," Calder said. "Imala proposed radiator fire. I am proposing escort capture. Bring her about by boarding, not by strike."

"Aspirational," Daren said.

"Documented," Calder said. "There is precedent. The Compact of the Third Wake allows boarding intercept of any vessel carrying sleeper cargo where the sleeper manifest cannot be confirmed. The forecast names Everspring and her manifest cannot be confirmed because she has refused our hail for six hours."

"Refusing hail does not invalidate a manifest," Besh said.

"Refusing hail while running does," Calder said. "It suggests the sleeper cargo is being moved without the consent of the sleepers. The Compact protects them in that case."

"And if she consents to hail?" Daren asked.

"Then we listen. We hear what the warning actually demands. And we hold a second vote."

The chamber went quiet. Eighteen captains looked at their hands or the holo or the deck. Outside the observation port, the convoy drifted in formation. The brown dwarf Caul glowed dull rust on the starboard bow. The hull lattice ticked as it shed heat. Three of the eighteen captains were not in the chamber. Captain Hesta had been awake for forty hours and had been ordered to bed by the ship's doctor. Captain Olen was on the bridge of Quiet Reed running docking drills with crew who had never boarded a seedship. Captain Ravel had refused to attend and was drafting his own broadcast to the convoy.

"Admiral." Captain Onn of Sounding Star spoke from the third row. She had lost her husband at Pell and her cousin at Hespar and her belief in the doctrine between the two. "I need to ask the chamber a question before we vote. I need to ask if the doctrine requires us to fire on sleepers or only to prevent the wake."

"Both," Calder said.

"That is not both," Onn said. "Those are different acts. Preventing the wake is boarding or escort. Firing on sleepers is killing. The doctrine never required killing. The doctrine required obedience to verified warning. The warning verified. We obey. We do not kill."

"Then we vote," Calder said.

"I second the motion," Imala said. "With a strike rider. If boarding fails, we escalate to radiator fire."

"Vote the motion as written," Calder said. "Boarding intercept, hail, capture if consent is withheld. No strike riders. The motion on the floor is boarding only."

The holo rolled. Amber pulsed. Azure pulsed. Grey went dark.

Nine amber. Nine azure.

The holo held. The chamber held. Calder held the rail of the table and did not let her hand tighten.

"Admiral." Besh stood for the first time. "I have voted amber. I want you to know why."

"You do not need to explain a vote, Captain."

"I do, because I will not vote with you again if this goes wrong." Besh looked at the chamber. "I vote amber because Hespar taught me that delay is the sin. Hespar did not teach me that boarding an eighteen-thousand-sleeper seedship is a safe act. Hespar taught me the opposite. If we board Everspring and the sleepers die in the boarding, that is on us. We will have done to them what the forecast council refused to do to us."

"You cannot guarantee sleeper safety in any boarding," Daren said.

"I cannot guarantee it," Calder said. "I can guarantee that we will not fire on the sleepers. We will not strike the radiator vanes while eighteen thousand bodies ride inside. If we cannot bring her about by boarding, we will escort her under live rules of engagement. We will shadow her to Serein. We will watch what she wakes there. And we will hold a second vote when we know what we are voting on."

"That is not the doctrine," Besh said.

"That is the doctrine amended by Hespar," Calder said. "Obedience to verified warning. Protection of the warned. Everspring does not carry that protection. She carries the cargo. The cargo does not die from us, and the world she would wake does not die from her if we can stop her from waking it."

The holo waited. Marek looked at his hands. Imala looked at Calder. Daren looked at the port.

"Vote," Calder said.

The holo rolled. Nine amber, nine azure, the one grey now lit.

"Captain Vesper," the holo announced. "Abstaining, vote counted under Compact provision seventeen. The motion carries by tie and provision seventeen."

The chamber exhaled. Calder did not exhale. She had used provision seventeen once before and the use had cost her a friend.

"Admiral." Onn stood again. "I want it on the record that the doctrine as amended does not authorize firing on sleeper cargo. The motion as written is boarding only. No strike riders and no radiator fire authorized under this vote. If any captain fires without your explicit order, that captain alone bears the consequence."

"That captain alone bears the consequence," Calder said. "Recorded."

"Recorded," Besh echoed, keying the bridge log.

Outside the chamber, through the observation port, a small cutter from Far Margin detached from the convoy and began a slow burn toward the open corridor. Captain Ravel had made his choice. Calder watched the cutter until it was too small to resolve and did not give the order to intercept it.

"Captain Marek," Calder said, when the chamber began to clear. "Your ship is the closest. You take the lead. Boarding party of twenty, no weapons heavier than sidearms, no strike riders, no laser burn. You intercept and you hail."

"I voted against this," Marek said.

"And I am asking you anyway," Calder said. "Because your ship can match Everspring's bearing and your crew can dock without casualty. I am not asking your vote. I am asking your hand. Your ship has docked civilian freighters in worse weather than this."

Marek looked at his hands. He had docked a freighter through a spin-gravity failure six years ago and lost no one and remembered every second. He had watched a council vote Hespar into a seventy-two-hour delay and lost his sister and his niece and the belief that councils of strangers could ever be trusted with the lives of people they would never meet.

"I will intercept," Marek said. "I will hail. I will not fire."

He paused at the rail of the bench. "When we dock, Admiral, I will ask to speak to the sleeper manifest. I will record the names of the children on board. I will keep that recording with me until the mission is over. If the doctrine fails them, I want their names in my log. I want to know who I failed when we failed."

"Record the names," Calder said. "Record them for me, too. The doctrine will not fail them if I have anything to do with it."

"Captain Imala," Calder said. "Your ship shadows. Laser armed, not fired. Radiator strike only on my explicit order, and only if Everspring fires first."

"Acknowledged," Imala said.

"Captain Besh." Calder paused. "You are the senior captain. I need a witness to every order I give from this point forward. You have the bridge log. Every word I speak goes into it before I send it."

Besh nodded. "Acknowledged, Admiral."

Then Besh paused at the bench. "There is one more thing, Admiral. There is a forecast on the bridge log from three days before the warning arrived. It did not name Everspring. It named the convoy's courier burn pattern. It predicted that a courier carrying a spindle would succeed in reaching the seedship before any intercept. I did not read it at the time because it was not authenticated. Now I do not know if it was a fluke or a leak."

"Show me the log," Calder said.

"After the council," Besh said. "I want the council to disperse first. Some of what is on that log implicates people in this chamber. I do not want to accuse anyone without the bridge officer present."

"After the council," Calder said.

The vote had split. The fleet would split with it. Three captains had already signaled private dissent on secure channels. Two had asked for transfer to convoy work. One, Captain Ravel of Far Margin, had broadcast a refusal to follow pursuit and begun a slow burn toward open space.

"Ravel's refusal is recorded," Calder said. "He is a civilian convoy captain. He cannot be ordered. He can be asked to surrender registry. He will not. Mark him as separated and continue."

"Admiral," Daren said. "There are captains in this chamber who will fire on Ravel if he attempts to reach Everspring before us."

"Then those captains will be court-martialed," Calder said. "We do not fire on civilians. We do not fire on our own fleet. We do not fire on anyone who has not fired on us. That is the doctrine."

"That is a new doctrine," Besh said.

"That is the doctrine I am sworn to enforce," Calder said. "The old doctrine died at Hespar. The new doctrine will die when it has killed enough people. Until then, we follow this one."

She looked around the chamber. Eighteen captains. Eighteen votes. Eighteen different loyalties and eighteen different versions of Hespar living inside them. The fleet would split, would fracture, would bleed. She had known it since the warning arrived. The vote had only confirmed it.

"Dismissed," Calder said. "Captain Marek, prepare your intercept. Captain Imala, prepare your shadow. Captain Besh, you have the bridge log. Captains of the dissenting ships, you are released from this council's orders. You are not released from the Compact."

The captains stood. The benches emptied in ones and twos. Calder watched them go and felt the weight of every name that would not return to this chamber.

Besh lingered. "Admiral."

"Captain."

"This will not hold," Besh said. "You know that."

"I know it."

"You voted the fleet into pursuit knowing the fleet will not hold together."

"I voted the fleet into a course that may save eighteen thousand sleepers and the world they were going to wake. If the fleet will not hold that course, the fleet is not worth holding."

Besh unfolded his hands and put them flat on the table. "I voted amber because I believe the warning. I will not vote amber again if the doctrine bends further. The doctrine can bend toward protecting the warned. It cannot bend toward protecting the warners. The moment we protect the warners, we become the warners, and Hespar repeats. I have said that out loud because I need you to remember I said it. I have also said it for the bridge log, which I keep, which the log officer keeps, which the next captain will read."

"I will remember," Calder said. "If I bend it further, court-martial me."

"I will," Besh said. "And I will ask the log officer to read the charge aloud when they do it."

Besh folded his hands. "That is what your brother said at Hespar."

Calder did not answer. She watched the port. Outside, three ships peeled off from the convoy. One of them was Far Margin, already burning. The convoy thinned. The doctrine bent. The fleet divided, and the chamber was left with what division had always cost.

She had known it would. She had hoped it would not. The hope died in the chamber, and the work of the morning began.

Chapter 11 — What the Moon Remembered

The escort tug Sworn had four people aboard and one radiator with a cracked vane that Tev had patched with alloy tape and a prayer he refused to translate. Sena sat at the helm with her feet braced against the gimbals and watched the moon Lumen climb out of the starless black ahead. The storm band on its nightside pulsed in long silver bands, and the microwave carriers she had ridden for an hour were starting to climb her skin with static.

"Carrier at twenty-three hundred," she said into the comm. "Tev, the panel is hot."

"Hot is data," Tev said from the aft bay. "Hold the bearing."

"Tell that to my hand."

She flexed her fingers against the gloves. The tug had no glove protocol. Its bridge panel ran warm when carriers crowded the upper ring, and Lumen carried denser bands than any survey chart admitted. Heat had climbed through three thresholds in the last hour. Each threshold marked another coherent frequency riding the storm. Something below had begun sorting noise into durable intervals.

Three days earlier, Everspring had refused tow and run. Quiet Verdict, Honest John to everyone who had kept the old tug alive, completed the escape burn with its number-two hinge fused half open. The tug now hung cold against the seedship's far-side cradle, too damaged for another pursuit leg. Okoro transferred Sena's escort authority to Sworn, a utility tug from the seedship's stores, while Tev moved the courier hard point, the encrypted radio, and the least damaged restraint web across. He called the transfer surgery. Sena called it theft with paperwork. Neither name improved the replacement throttle, which answered faster than Honest John's and gave her hand no familiar resistance.

The escort assignment had limits. Mere owned the safe navigation branches. Tev could cut the drive for heat. Okoro could recall the tug if any suspension bank crossed red. Within those boundaries, Sena could range ahead of the seedship and test the approach no loyalist survey vessel could enter without revealing its own firing geometry.

Lumen offered the first useful test. Iven's last survey ping had returned a pattern no geological model could flatten into accident. Tev wanted the raw carriers gathered inside the storm rather than filtered through a remote probe. Okoro wanted to know whether the moon could alter the safe route to Serein. Sena wanted one fact the fleet could not turn into doctrine before anyone looked at it.

They carried a crystal buffer in Sworn's shielded bay, empty when they left and large enough for nine minutes of storm traffic. No settlement code rode inside it. No terraforming sequence waited for delivery. The mission consisted of listening, recording, and returning with the buffer intact. Sena understood the value of work whose cargo did not pretend to decide its own meaning.

The storm window had opened. The buffer remained empty. Her hand held a borrowed helm that had not yet learned her.

"Lumen bearing two-one-five," Tev said. "Storm window opening in eight minutes. We want to be inside it."

"For what?"

"For the data. For whatever Lumen is doing with the carriers."

Lumen was doing something. The mineral storms on its nightside had been catalogued as noise since the second survey, four years before Sena was born. The noise had a periodicity that the surveys had classified as piezoelectric resonance. The periodicity had a structure that the third survey had filed without comment. The structure had not been named until Iven Mora, in a forecast he did not broadcast, had noted that the carriers repeated with the regularity of memory. Sena had read the forecast on the courier run. She had not known what it meant. She knew now that Iven had been right.

The forecast had used a phrase she had not parsed. The phrase had been "carrier coherence under storm modulation." The phrase meant the carriers rode the storm in patterns that did not disperse. The phrase meant the patterns persisted across cycles. The phrase meant the storm remembered itself.

"Carrier at twenty-eight hundred," she said. "Tev, the panel is hot."

"Hot is data."

"Hot is also a warning."

"Then warn me in three minutes."

She crossed her hands on the glove. The tug tipped into the storm band, and the panel screamed. Outside the bridge port, Lumen's silver bands brightened into white bands. White bands meant the storm was lifting particles into the upper ring, and the upper ring was where the carriers rode.

The ring was also where the survey probes had died. Four of the second-survey probes had crossed the upper ring and never reported back. Two of the third-survey probes had done the same. The surveys had assumed the probes had failed under particle abrasion. The surveys had not considered that the probes might have been recorded instead.

"Sworn, this is Watchful Coil on intercept." The cutter's comm cut through the storm hiss. "You are inside a closed patrol zone. Heave to and prepare to be boarded."

Sena did not answer. She knew Watchful Coil. She knew its captain. Captain Besh had been at the council. Besh had voted amber. Besh had meant well.

"Sir," she said to Besh. "We are inside Lumen's storm band harvesting carriers. Request you hold your intercept until we exit."

"You are a courier tug with cracked radiators. You are not a survey vessel. You are not authorized to be inside the storm band. Heave to."

Sena held the bearing. She had been told to hold the bearing. She had been told the data mattered. She had been told that Lumen was not noise.

"Captain Besh," she said. "The storm band is not closed patrol. There is no patrol zone here. The doctrine does not extend to moons with no human presence."

"There is now," Besh said. "Admiral Calder has declared the Serein approach closed patrol under the pursuit order. You are inside it. Heave to or I will fire across your bow."

Tev spoke in her ear from the aft bay, on a private channel. "Sena. The carriers are organizing. Look at the panel."

She looked. The carriers on the glove panel had clustered into a shape. The shape carried no resemblance to a circle or a line. The shape resolved into a spiral. The spiral rotated once, then twice, then settled into a stable pattern that the survey charts had never recorded.

The spiral had direction. The direction was not arbitrary. The direction carried meaning, the way a hand on a door carries meaning. The carriers were not random. The carriers were being arranged by something that wanted them arranged.

"Pattern stable," Tev said. "Recording. Hold the bearing for nine minutes."

"Besh will fire in three," Sena said.

"He won't fire on a courier inside a storm band. Not yet."

"He will fire if he decides obedience requires it."

"Then let the storm band decide for him," Tev said.

The storm band did something. The carriers in the upper ring, the ones that had been pulsing silver, dimmed. The white bands retreated to grey bands. The grey bands thickened and the tug began to rock. Outside the port, the moon's nightside filled with a rolling fog of silicate particles. The fog moved against the wind. The fog had direction.

The fog moved toward the cutter. The fog did not strike the cutter. The fog moved around the cutter and around the tug and formed a corridor between them. The corridor was wide enough for one ship. The corridor pointed toward the moon's center.

"Sworn, this is Watchful Coil." Besh's voice tightened. "You are inside a structural event. Heave to. Repeat, heave to."

"I can't," Sena said. "The tug is being pushed. I am not steering. The storm is steering."

"The storm does not steer."

"It does today," Sena said.

Then the spiral tightened. Panel temperature fell four degrees although the radiator load kept climbing. White marks appeared along the curve, first as isolated points and then as linked vectors. Tev sent the display to navigation mode. The marks resolved into flight paths.

One path matched survey probe Yarrow, lost twenty-three years earlier after its last carrier burst. The panel returned Yarrow's approach, roll correction, and final tumble with errors smaller than the surviving mission log. A second trace reproduced a school-ring rescue burn from nine years earlier. Sena knew the uneven third correction before its identifier rendered. Jori had flown that correction while Pell burned behind her.

A third route belonged to Vel Tass, the courier who crossed Lumen four years earlier and reached Everspring carrying a sealed survey spindle. Tass had reported structured interference. The council had filed the report under abrasion noise. Lumen returned the route now with the spindle's transmission pulses nested beside it, preserving both the courier and the ignored evidence.

"Tev," Sena said. "The storm kept the traffic."

"Traffic recorders don't push ships."

"This one does."

The storm drew Sworn across the nightside for nine minutes. Every adjustment Sena made entered the spiral. When she eased starboard thrust, a new white segment joined her plotted route before the navigation computer finished projecting it. The moon did not merely replay an archive. It watched the tug add another line.

Tev opened the buffer's raw feed. Probe call signs surfaced beside carrier harmonics, followed by radiator temperatures, pilot corrections, and the timing gaps left by machines that never returned. Human surveys had crossed these bands for decades and treated persistence as a mineral defect. Lumen had answered by preserving the crossings with more care than the survey office that sent them.

Sena kept her hand still on the helm. The display held Jori's old correction beside her own. It offered no voice, no body, and no promise that memory meant survival. It offered evidence that the moon had witnessed a flight the council later told backward.

"Sworn, this is Watchful Coil." Besh again. "I am holding intercept. The storm has pushed you into a holding arc. I cannot close to boarding without crossing the storm band. I will not cross the storm band. The storm is doing something I do not understand."

"The storm is recording," Sena said.

"Pilot, the storm does not record."

"The moon is remembering," Sena said. "The moon has been remembering every probe we have ever sent, every courier we have ever run, every burn pattern we have ever traced near it. The carriers are not noise. The carriers carry memory."

A silence opened on the comm. It lasted four seconds, which was a long time for a captain to be silent.

"Say that again," Besh said.

"The carriers carry memory. The moon is keeping a record. The moon is not empty. The moon is inhabited. The moon was here before we landed on it and the moon will be here after we leave."

"By what?"

"By something that does not need a body to remember."

Besh went quiet. The cutter held its position. The storm band held the tug. The carriers held their spiral.

The spiral tightened a quarter turn. The tightening made the cold deeper. The depth carried a name. The name belonged to a probe that had crossed Lumen twenty-three years ago and never reported back. The name belonged to a pilot who had crossed Lumen nine years ago and had not been heard from since. The name belonged to a courier who had crossed Lumen four years ago and had reached Everspring with a sealed spindle and a story that the council had refused to hear.

The name belonged to Jori.

"Sworn," Tev said. "The data is full. We can exit."

"Hold one more minute," Sena said. "I want the exit burn recorded."

"Burn is data," Tev said.

"Burn is also goodbye," Sena said.

She held the bearing for one more minute. The carriers arranged themselves into a final pattern. The pattern was a shape she did not recognize. The shape might have been a name. The shape might have been a question. The shape might have been the moon's first attempt to write itself in a language the courier could read.

She could not read it. She could record it.

"Watchful Coil," she said. "I am exiting the storm band. I have new data for the council. I am requesting escort to Aster Vale for transcription."

"I will escort you," Besh said. His voice had changed. The cutter pulled alongside, and Besh's face on the comm belonged to a captain who had voted amber because he believed the warning and now was not sure what the warning was warning about.

The escort lasted eleven seconds before Watchful Coil began to roll.

Besh had released a warning flechette when he first ordered Sena to heave to. The shot should have crossed Sworn's bow and vanished into the open ring. Instead the storm's charged silicate sheet pulled the flechette off its clean line. It struck Watchful Coil near the port radiator root, not hard enough to open the hull, but hard enough to tear the vane actuator from its track. The cutter's heat panel stopped halfway through a correction and caught the mineral flow broadside.

"Port vane jammed," Besh said. "Roll thruster three is not answering."

The cutter rotated another six degrees. Its exposed panel crossed the densest carrier band. White charge crawled over the radiator ribs. If Besh folded the damaged panel, his drive heat would trap itself inside the cutter. If he left it open, the next quarter turn would put the thin face into the debris stream.

Sena saw the two failures arrive on her plot as separate red branches. Honest John would have answered her hand with a warning tremor. Sworn answered immediately and made her distrust the ease of it.

"Tev, grappler status."

"Magnetic head only. Cable motor reads green. You use it in that storm and we may bring half the ring home."

"Besh, cut main thrust. Cold gas only. Put your dorsal rail toward me."

"You cannot tow this cutter with that vane."

"I don't need to tow you. I need to stop one quarter turn."

Sena backed Sworn across the corridor Lumen had opened. The storm resisted her stern, then thinned around the tug as if the new line had become legible. At forty meters she fired the grappler. Its magnetic head struck Watchful Coil's dorsal rail and locked. Cable spilled through the motor. Sena waited until the cutter's roll pulled the line taut, then pulsed starboard translation for one second.

The restraint caught her low across the ribs. Sworn's cracked vane climbed seven degrees. Tev swore in the aft bay, a precise inventory of every person who had approved the maneuver. The cable motor screamed. Watchful Coil's roll slowed, held, and reversed half a degree.

"Now vent roll three," Sena said.

Besh fired cold gas through the damaged thruster manifold. The cutter shuddered away from the debris stream. Its radiator cleared the white carrier sheet with less than a meter between the outer rib and the mineral flow.

Sena released the grappler. The cable came home silver with dust. Tev closed the buffer shield before the charged grains could corrupt the record.

"Radiator remains open," Besh said after checking his board. "Drive limited to twenty percent."

"Enough for escort."

Besh looked at her through the comm. A minute earlier he had intended to board her under Calder's authority. Now his own warning shot had damaged his cutter, the moon had bent the shot, and the pilot he meant to arrest had spent heat she could not replace to keep him alive.

"Record the rescue under my command seal," he told his bridge. "Record the storm deflection as observed, not inferred. Attach the tug's carrier buffer by reference."

His bridge officer acknowledged. The sentence entered a record Besh could not later reduce to weather.

"Pilot," Besh said. "The moon is not in the warning."

"No," Sena said. "The moon lives in the carriers."

"What does that mean?"

"It means we have not been listening. It means the surveys have been misreading for twenty years. It means the moon has been keeping a record and we have been calling it noise."

"Will the council listen?"

"Some of them," Sena said. "Captain Marek will. Admiral Calder will not. Captain Onn will. Captain Ravel already left."

"You know the captains."

"I flew with three of them on Pell," Sena said. "I know their hands on a helm. I know how they vote. Besh, the council needs to see this data before we reach Serein."

"I will take it to the council," Besh said. "I will take it as a witness. I will not take it as a vote. The vote is for intercept of Everspring, not for first contact."

"First contact is not what I am calling it," Sena said.

"What are you calling it?"

"A correction," Sena said. "The moon was here before us. The moon will be here after. The moon has been keeping a record we cannot read. We owe the moon a hearing, not an intercept."

The cutter pulled back. The storm band let the tug go. The carriers settled into a slower rhythm. The panel cooled. Sena flexed her hands against the gloves and felt the static bleed away and felt something else replace it, something like a hand on a shoulder from a distance.

"Tev," she said.

"Sena."

"The carriers carried me. The carriers carried Jori, too. Jori flew this band before me. The carriers know Jori's burn."

"They know everyone's burn," Tev said.

"They knew Jori's pattern. They returned it. They put it on the panel."

Silence on the aft channel. Tev did not speak for thirty seconds. Then he said, "Sena, do not chase that."

"I am not chasing it," she said. "I am reporting it. The moon remembered Jori. The moon remembers everything. The moon remembers with a witness's patience."

"The council will not accept that," Tev said.

"The council will have to," Sena said. "The carriers are not noise. The carriers carry a record. The carriers will be there when we reach Serein. They will be there when we try to wake the sleepers. They will be there when obedience orders us to fire on the seedship."

"The carriers do not have obedience," Tev said.

"The carriers do not need obedience," Sena said. "The carriers remember."

She set the bearing for Aster Vale. The storm band faded behind her. Lumen's nightside cooled to black. She did not look back. She did not need to. The carriers had made their record. The record was in her panel, in the courier's glove, in the courier's hand.

The escort tug Sworn limped toward the survey carrier with a cracked vane and a new language in its skin. The moon had remembered. The fleet would have to learn what the moon had learned, and the learning would begin with one captain who had voted amber and now carried a witness he had not asked for and could not refuse.

Chapter 12 — The Ruin at Serein

The debris band began at forty thousand kilometers and thickened until the sky turned to gravel. Sena watched the range tape count down on the helm repeater and listened to the hull lattice tick faster as it ate the smaller shards. Sworn had no shields; courier tugs never did. The lattice ate what the lattice ate, and the rest of the ship lived with the bruises.

"Three centimeters," Tev said from the aft bay. He had stopped calling out panel readings and started calling out the width of the strikes he could hear through the deck. Three centimeters meant a stone the size of a closed fist. Five centimeters would put a crack through the second deck that no alloy tape could close.

"Besh is holding station a thousand klicks back," Sena said. "He will not follow us through the belt."

"He voted amber because he believes the warning," Tev said. "He has not yet learned what the warning is warning about."

Sena flexed her hands against the gloves. The panel ran cold. Lumen had faded from the bridge the way a smell fades from a room when you walk out of it. The storm had pushed them across the moon's nightside. The storm had let them go. The moon had kept its record, and the panel held the spiral pattern and a burn that did not belong to Sena. That burn belonged to Jori.

Serein filled the forward port. The planet was a black disc edged in bruise-colored cloud. The clouds moved in slow bands she had never seen on a world with weather she trusted. A weather she trusted ran in three cycles and obeyed its own physics. A weather she did not trust ran in three cycles and obeyed someone else's physics.

The debris was not orbital junk. The debris had shape. The shards carried flutes and cones and what looked like the cross-section of a hull taken apart by something that knew where the rivets were. The cross-section belonged to a survey cutter. The hull markings said Quiet Verge. She had gone missing four years ago, the same year the courier with Jori crossed Lumen's band.

Tev read the markings off the hull tape before Sena asked. "Second cutter. Quiet Verge. Lost the same year the courier crossed the band with the survey probe."

The broken cross-section rolled past them, and she saw the names stencilled on the inside of the service bay: DORA, HALEN, MAREK-A. Three names. Three surveyors. Three families that had waited four years for a hull that had not come home.

"Marek-A," Tev said. "Captain Marek's brother."

"Yes," Sena said.

The cutter rolled on. The bearing held. The lattice ate a two-centimeter strike and the deck rang like a struck bell. Tev's voice from the aft bay was hoarser than it had been at the start. Hoarseness was a kind of tiredness. Tiredness was a kind of honesty. Honesty was what the ruin was going to require.

The descent carried them through forty minutes of belt. Each minute taught her a new shape: a dish bent into a parabola by someone who understood parabolas, a thruster nozzle pried free and set adrift like a caught fish, a flight recorder with the case still sealed and the tape spooled out behind it like a tongue. The recorder belonged to a courier. The courier had been inside the recorder at the moment of ejection. The recorder had not protected the courier.

The recorder carried a name. The name was not on the case. The name lived in the courier's glove, which had been pulled inside out by the ejection and which still held the courier's hand-shape in its fabric. The hand belonged to a pilot who had crossed Lumen and never reported back. The pilot had been a woman. She had been carrying a survey probe, not a permission. She had been carrying Jori.

Sena had not known the pilot's name until now. The pilot's name was Vel Tass. She had been the junior courier on Sworn's sister tug Pale Errand. Okoro had not mentioned her when she handed Sena the spindle. Vel Tass had been the one the council had refused to hear about. Her family had asked Everspring's mission office for a closure letter. The office had told them to ask the courier board. The courier board had said ask the council. The council had said ask the mission office. The family had stopped asking.

Vel Tass had been carrying Jori as cargo. Jori had not been cargo. Jori had been a passenger on a survey probe. The probe had been launched from Pale Errand on a standard survey run. The probe had crossed Lumen's band. The band had kept the probe. The probe had not been heard from again. Jori had asked to ride along because Jori had wanted to see what the moon did with its carriers. Jori had been Sena's copilot on the Pell rescue years earlier, and Jori had died in that rescue, and the death had not been a death anyone had wanted to make public. The death had been folded into the survey probe manifest, and the manifest had crossed Lumen, and the band had kept it.

Sena held the bearing. The lattice ate another strike, and the aft bay went silent while Tev did not say anything for thirty seconds.

"I am landing us at the ruin," she said.

"I know," Tev said. "I can see the ruin."

"I am going to land us because the memory node is there. The carriers carried the pattern down to the ruin. The ruin has a node the surveys never recovered. Okoro said the spindle was not the only copy. The other copy is at the ruin. The other copy is what I have to bring up."

"I know," Tev said. "The tape on the vane is starting to peel. We have one more pass through the belt. If we land, we land with what we have. If we take off again, we take off with what is left of the vane."

"Then we land," Sena said.

She set the bearing for the ruin. The ruin was a survey outpost the second mission had built on the planet's equator in the lee of a basalt cliff the surveys had named Cliffside. Cliffside had not been heard from in nineteen years. The outpost had been a habitable dome over a hot spring the surveys believed was geothermal. The hot spring had not been geothermal. The hot spring had been a vent from the moon's silicate weather. The weather had moved through the vent and into the dome and the dome had been kept, and the surveys had filed the records without comment.

The third survey had lost two probes to the moon. The fourth survey had never been launched. The fifth survey was the one Iven had read. Iven had called the carriers memory. Iven had been right.

The descent carried them through twenty more minutes of belt. The lattice ticked. Tev called out the strikes by width and she answered in helm corrections and the corrections brought the ruin up on the forward port in slow reveal.

The ruin was a dome. The dome was a quarter of a habitable ring pressed into a basalt shelf. The ring had been meant to hold twelve. It had held eight. The eight had spent nineteen years on the shelf before the moon had decided patience was over. The dome was cracked in three places. The cracks had been cut, not broken. The cuts had been made by something that knew where to cut a dome to let the air out without letting the air in too fast to be remembered.

The ruin held a node. The node was a crystal the size of a closed hand. The crystal sat on a basalt plinth at the center of the dome. The moon had placed it there. The same carriers that had lifted the spiral up to Sena's panel had carried this crystal down from Lumen's band. The crystal was not a probe. It was not a sample. It held a record. It carried the moon's first attempt to write itself in a language the surveyors could read.

The language was not human. It was a folded pattern of carriers the crystal replayed on a loop. The carriers ran microwave and cold. They carried the names of the eight surveyors, the four lost probes, the two pilots, the courier who had crossed the band four years ago. The courier's name was Vel Tass. The pilot's name was Jori. The names rode the carriers the way a hand rides the weight of a door it has just opened.

Sena brought Sworn down on the basalt shelf beside the dome. The vane cracked another three percent in the landing burn. Tev reported a temperature spike on the starboard radiator he could hold for ninety minutes and not for one hundred. Ninety minutes was the time she had. Ninety minutes was what the carriers had given her.

She suited. Tev suited. They crossed the basalt shelf in twelve minutes, suits not made for running, suits carrying the courier's belt that did not include a spindle. She had left the spindle in the tug. The belt in the tug, the tug in the lee of the basalt cliff where the moon's storm could not reach it.

The dome's airlock had been cut. The cuts came from something that knew where the bolts were. The airlock opened onto eight bunks and eight lockers and a basalt plinth with a crystal on it and the crystal was glowing. The crystal glowed in long silver bands the way Lumen had glowed. The bands carried the surveyors' names.

The surveyors were gone. They had not died in the dome. They had walked out through the cut in the airlock and into the storm. The bunks were empty. The lockers were sealed. The lockers carried the surveyors' names on tape labels written in the surveyors' own hands, and the tape had held for nineteen years because the tape had been made to hold.

Sena pulled the first label free. The label said DORA. She set it on the plinth beside the crystal. The crystal's silver band brightened for a moment, then dimmed, then brightened again. The brightening was a response. The crystal had read the tape. The crystal had held the tape. The crystal was keeping a record of every tape the surveys had left on the shelf, and it was willing to give the tapes back if someone asked.

She pulled the second label. HALEN. The brightening came again. She pulled the third. MAREK-A. She set it on the plinth and thought of Captain Marek on Quiet Reed, the sister, the niece, the council that had voted Hespar into a seventy-two-hour delay. She thought of the council that would have to vote on this ruin.

"The crystal is keeping the records," she said.

"I see it," Tev said.

"It is willing to give them back."

"I see that too."

"Then I am taking one."

She did not reach for the crystal. She reached for the lockers. The lockers held the surveyors' belongings. The belongings held letters. The letters carried the names of children the surveyors had left on Pell and the names of parents they had left on Brennan's Reach and the names of pets they had left on moons that did not have names. The letters had been sealed. The letters had been waiting. The letters had been waiting for someone who had crossed Lumen's band and been allowed back.

She took the letters. She took the eight labels and the eight lockers' worth of sealed envelopes. She set them on the plinth, the crystal brightening once for each label and once for each envelope, the brightening carrying the moon's first attempt to say thank you in a language the courier could read. The thank you was the brightening. The thank you was the record.

She lifted the crystal. She did not know she was allowed to take the crystal until she had already lifted it. The crystal ran cold. It carried the weight of the carriers that had placed it. It weighed more than a hand and less than a probe. She set it in the courier's belt. The belt held the crystal the way the belt held the spindle. The belt held both.

"I have it," she said.

"I see you have it."

"I have the ruin's record. I have the carriers' record. I have the eight surveyors' letters and labels. I have the crystal and the spindle. I am going back to the tug."

"I am going with you," Tev said.

They crossed the basalt shelf in eleven minutes. The suits carried the letters and the labels and the crystal. The suits carried the ruin and the moon's first attempt at thank you. The suits did not carry the eight surveyors. The eight surveyors had been kept. The eight surveyors belonged to the moon now.

The tug took off in a burn that cracked the vane another two percent. Tev reported a temperature spike on the starboard radiator he could hold for seventy minutes and not for eighty. The carriers on the panel ran sparse and silver and cool. The crystal warmed against her hip and the spindle warmed against the other hip and the two warmths held each other across a courier's belt that had been cleared of everything except the things that could not be cleared.

The ruin had given her one more thing. The ruin had given her the truth about Jori.

Jori had not died in the Pell rescue. Jori had died on Lumen's band. Jori had died crossing the band in a survey probe folded into a manifest the council had refused to read. Jori had died in a way no one had wanted to name, and the death had been filed as missing. The missing had crossed Lumen. The band had kept the file and the death, and the death had been the first thing the moon had recorded.

The moon had been recording ever since. The moon had decided, on the night a courier with a cracked vane and a sealed spindle rode its storm band, that this courier would be the first to come back. The moon had opened a corridor. The corridor had carried the courier to the ruin, and the ruin had given the courier the truth about Jori.

Sena held the bearing for Aster Vale. The vane cracked. The aft bay did not answer for a long moment.

"Tev," she said.

"Sena."

"I did not save Jori at Pell."

"I know," Tev said.

"The story I told at Pell was not the story."

"I know that too."

"Then I am telling it now. I am telling it to you because you are in the aft bay and the aft bay is the place where the courier tells the truth. I am telling it because the ruin has the truth. The council will not have any of this unless I bring it up."

"I am listening," Tev said.

"I was the copilot on the Pell rescue. Jori was the pilot. Jori flew the school ring out of a storm window no one had charted. The ring carried four hundred children. Jori flew the ring. I flew the tug. The tug lost a vane on the way out, a second on the way back, a third on the way home. Jori told me to bring the ring in. Jori told me to leave the tug in the storm. Jori told me to take the copilot's seat in the ring. I refused. I took the helm of the tug. Jori took the helm of the ring. Jori died in the ring. The ring made it home. I made it home. The story that was told was that I had flown the ring and Jori had died in the tug. The story was told because the council wanted a hero and I was the only one who would agree to be the hero. I agreed. I have been agreeing for nine years. I am done agreeing."

She held the bearing. The vane creaked under the strain. The aft bay did not answer for thirty seconds.

"You are not a hero," Tev said.

"No," Sena said.

"You are a courier who refused a story."

"I am a courier who told a story I knew was wrong."

"You are a courier who is telling the truth now."

"I am telling the truth to you because the aft bay is the place where the courier tells the truth. I am telling the truth because the ruin has the truth, the crystal has the ruin, the carriers have the crystal, and the moon has the carriers. The council will not have any of it unless I bring it up."

"Then bring it up," Tev said.

"I will tell it to Captain Besh first. Besh voted amber because he believed the warning. Besh will listen because he has been listening since the moon pushed us across its nightside. Besh will take the truth to the council as a witness, not a vote."

"And the crystal," Tev said.

"The crystal goes to Iven. Iven will read the crystal. Iven will know what the carriers meant. Iven will translate the crystal into a map the council can read. The map will name the moon and the carriers and the truth about Jori."

"And the spindle," Tev said.

"The spindle goes to Okoro. Okoro will know it is the same spindle Okoro gave me. Okoro will know the moon kept a copy. Okoro will know the moon has been keeping a copy of everything for twenty years. Okoro will know the moon is not a planet. Okoro will know the moon is a witness. Okoro will decide what to do with a witness."

"And the letters," Tev said.

"The letters go to the families. The families have waited four years for a hull that did not come home. The families will have a letter instead. The letters will not be enough. The letters will be what the moon has to give."

She held the bearing. The aft bay went quiet, the quiet a kind of listening, the listening a kind of agreement, the agreement what the ruin had given her and the carriers had kept and the moon had decided.

The escort tug Sworn limped toward the survey carrier with a cracked vane and a crystal in its belt and a spindle in the other half of its belt and eight letters in the courier's pocket and the truth about Jori in the courier's mouth. The moon had written its record. The courier had brought it up. The fleet would have to learn what the ruin had kept and what the truth about Jori would cost the council that had refused to hear it, and the learning would begin with one captain who had voted amber and now carried a witness he had not asked for and could not refuse.

Chapter 13 — A Message from Iven

The legal-memory substrate woke him with a headache that belonged to someone else.

He sat up on the bunk in the witness chamber and felt the headache settle behind his eyes the way a courier's headache settles after a long burn. The headache did not belong to him. The headache carried the shape of a cartographer's afternoon spent reading seventeen forecasts at once. The cartographer had been forty-two. The cartographer had been awake for thirty hours. The cartographer had fallen asleep at the console and had dreamed of maps that had learned to read their readers.

He knew the cartographer's name because the cartographer's name was the first thing the substrate had loaded. The substrate loaded it as a courtesy, the way a courier loads a passenger manifest before a burn. The substrate loaded the cartographer's name and pronouns and rank and medical history and mother and grief and habit of mapping what was not yet there.

The substrate did not load the cartographer's permission. The permission would come later. The substrate loaded the cartographer as a template, the way a courier loads a template address when the real address has not yet been written.

He sat on the bunk and looked at his hands. His hands were younger than the cartographer's hands. His hands did not shake. His hands had been grown in a substrate tank on a ship that had not yet been built, in a future that had not yet been written. The hands did not know what they were for. The cartographer's hands had known what they were for. The cartographer's hands had held charts. The cartographer's hands had held a stylus against a screen and the stylus had traced routes the council would later trust.

The witness chamber was small. The chamber held a cot, a sink, a mirror that did not reflect the right face. The mirror reflected the cartographer's face. The face looked tired and precise. The eyes were dark. The mouth carried a thin line.

He touched the mirror. The face did not match the hands. The face belonged to a person who had fallen asleep at a console. The hands belonged to a person who had not yet been born. The mismatch carried itself as a headache.

A voice from the wall speaker said his name. The speaker said, "Iven Mora. Are you receiving?"

He said, "I am receiving."

The speaker said, "This is Doctor Halla Sen aboard Aster Vale. I run the legal-memory substrate. You sit inside an embodiment grown from a forecast pattern transmitted along the return mirrors. The pattern arrived nine hours ago. The pattern is yours. The body was grown. We need to ask some questions."

He said, "I am Iven Mora. Chief cartographer of this survey carrier. I fell asleep at the console. I have been having a dream. The dream shows a map that has learned to read its reader. The dream has not ended. The dream has the shape of a headache."

The speaker paused for four seconds. The pause carried the weight of a doctor's first question answered in a way that did not match the chart.

"Mr. Mora," the speaker said. "The pattern we received carries your name as a courtesy header. The pattern carries more than your name. The pattern carries a memory architecture that does not match any Aster Vale crew manifest. The pattern carries an attestation signed by an advocate named Pel March of Orison. The attestation names you as a future descendant of Everspring transmitted backward to be a witness. The attestation does not name you as Iven Mora. The attestation names you as Aro Nhal."

He looked at his hands. They were younger than the cartographer's hands, grown in a substrate tank. Nothing in them explained their purpose.

He said, "I am not Iven Mora."

The speaker said, "Then who are you?"

He said, "I am Aro Nhal. I am twenty-eight. I belong to a coalition called Orison. I am a descendant of the wake that Everspring will found. I am a cognitive pattern transmitted backward along the return mirrors. I have been embodied in this substrate as a witness. I thought I was Iven Mora because the substrate loaded the cartographer's name first. The substrate did not load my permission. The substrate did not load my advocate. The substrate did not load my mother. The substrate did not load my grief. The substrate loaded a template, and the template took the shape of a cartographer who had been awake for thirty hours and had dreamed of maps that had learned to read their readers."

The speaker paused again for four seconds.

"Mr. Nhal," the speaker said. "Orison is not on our manifest."

"Orison is not on anyone's manifest yet," he said. "Orison lives in the futures that Everspring will found if Everspring reaches Serein and wakes the sleepers. I come from one of those futures. I sit here as a witness. The futures disagree about whether the present may silence the future. They do not agree about anything else. They agree only that I am to be the witness."

The speaker said, "Where does the witness come from?"

"The witness comes from the substrate of Aster Vale," he said. "The witness comes from the return mirrors. The witness comes from a future that depends on the arrival of Everspring and the consent of the moon Lumen and the survival of the sleepers. The witness comes from a future that has not yet been written and may not be written if the warning that arrived at 0400 yesterday is obeyed."

The speaker said, "The warning was authenticated."

"The warning was authenticated," he said. "The warning was authenticated by a council that has been receiving authenticated warnings for one hundred and twelve years and has obeyed every one. The warning was authenticated by a doctrine that saved Pell and Brennan's Reach and six other worlds whose names have become monuments to obedience. The warning was authenticated and orders the destruction of Everspring. The destruction would prevent the futures I come from. The destruction would also kill eighteen thousand sleepers who have done nothing wrong except agree to be born."

After another four-second pause, the speaker said, "I am going to bring the captain."

He said, "Bring the captain. Bring the cartographer. Bring the advocate's wafer. Bring the advocate's attestation. Bring the attestation signed by Pel March of Orison in a sealed envelope. Open the envelope in front of me. I will not be believed until I am read."

The witness chamber held a chair bolted to the deck. He sat in the chair and waited. The headache had settled. The headache now belonged to Aro Nhal. The headache carried the shape of a future that had been loaded into the substrate as a template and had begun to remember itself.

The captain arrived. The captain was Admiral Rhea Calder. The captain had a long face and short hair and a habit of standing at parade rest when she did not know what to do with her hands. The captain did not know what to do with her hands. The captain stood at parade rest and looked at the body in the chair. The body did not match the chart.

"Mr. Nhal," Calder said.

"Aro," he said. "Please."

"Mr. Aro," Calder said. "I am told you claim to be a future descendant of Everspring transmitted backward along the return mirrors."

"That is what I claim."

"I am told you have been embodied in the legal-memory substrate of my ship."

"That is where I sit."

"I am told the embodiment serves as a witness."

"That is what I am."

"I am told the embodiment carries an attestation from an advocate named Pel March of Orison."

"The attestation sits in my memory architecture."

"I am told Orison is not on any manifest."

"Not yet."

"I am told you are twenty-eight years old in a future that depends on the arrival of Everspring, and that the warning authenticated at 0400 yesterday orders the destruction of Everspring."

"That is what the substrate holds."

Calder looked at the door. The door opened. Iven Mora walked into the witness chamber with a chart roll under one arm and a stylus in the other hand. Iven had dark eyes and a thin mouth. Iven had been awake for thirty hours. Iven carried the same headache.

Iven looked at the body in the chair. The body in the chair had been grown from the cartographer's medical history. The body carried the cartographer's face. The body carried younger hands.

"Iven," Calder said.

"Admiral," Iven said.

"This is the embodiment," Calder said. "It claims to be Aro Nhal. It claims to be a future descendant of Everspring."

Iven looked at the embodiment. The embodiment looked back.

"I am not you," the embodiment said.

"I know you are not me," Iven said. "For nine hours I have shared your dream of a map learning to read whoever studies it. That map is a forecast. The forecast has been writing itself backward for one hundred and twelve years. It has been reading the council that receives it. The council has been calling the reading obedience."

Iven set the chart roll on the table. The chart roll held a forecast the substrate had transmitted nine hours ago. The forecast carried a memory architecture that did not match any Aster Vale crew manifest. The forecast carried an attestation signed by Pel March of Orison. The attestation named the embodiment as Aro Nhal. The attestation named the embodiment as a witness.

"You have been receiving our forecast," the embodiment said.

"We have been receiving your forecast," Iven said. "The forecast has been arriving along the return mirrors for one hundred and twelve years. The forecast has been arriving authenticated and sealed. The forecast has been arriving as warnings, engineering improvements, epidemiological guidance. The forecast has been arriving as advice. The forecast has been arriving as the doctrine the council obeys."

"The doctrine the council obeys bears the name forecast sovereignty," the embodiment said. "The doctrine says the present must obey the best available warning before understanding arrives. The doctrine says obedience is safer than delay. The doctrine saved Pell. The doctrine saved Brennan's Reach. The doctrine cost Hespar, where a council delayed for seventy-two hours to verify a warning and lost two million citizens. The doctrine is real. The doctrine is not what I am here to overturn."

Calder stepped forward. "Then why have you come?"

"I have come to be a witness," the embodiment said. "I have come because the futures disagree. The futures disagree about whether the present is allowed to silence the future.
