The futures agree about one thing. The futures agree that a witness is required. The futures agree that the witness must speak for the futures and against the doctrine of forecast sovereignty. The futures agree that the witness must come from the futures that Everspring will found if the seedship is allowed to reach Serein and wake the sleepers."

"The seedship must be destroyed," Calder said. "The warning is authenticated. The warning names Everspring. The warning orders destruction before Serein insertion. The warning has been verified by every cipher we own. The warning has been verified by the doctrine that saved Pell and Brennan's Reach and six other worlds."

"The warning has been verified," the embodiment said. "The warning has been verified because verification is what the present does to the future. The present verifies the warning. The present obeys the warning. The warning orders destruction. The destruction prevents the futures I come from. The futures I come from are the futures that the warning is sent from. The warning has been verified. Verification and destruction amount to the same act."

Calder looked at Iven. Iven looked at the chart roll. The chart roll held the forecast the embodiment had been transmitted in. The forecast carried the embodiment's memory architecture. The architecture held sixteen strands of cognitive pattern that had been chosen from sixteen candidate lives and had been braided together by an advocate named Pel March and had been attested by a coalition called Orison.

"You have been received by my cartographer," Calder said.

"I have been received by your cartographer's substrate," the embodiment said. "The substrate is not your cartographer. The substrate is a legal-memory medium. It has received forecasts for one hundred and twelve years. It has never carried a mind before me. Orison chose it because its authentication history could prove I was not an invention of this hour. The medium received me with your cartographer's name as a courtesy header. It received me with the cartographer's medical history as a template. It did not receive me with permission. It did not receive me with an advocate. It received me as a forecast that had grown a body."

"Then who is the advocate?" Calder asked.

"The advocate is Pel March of Orison," the embodiment said. "Pel March is a future pattern. Pel March braided my sixteen strands. Pel March attested my embodiment. Pel March chose my name. Pel March will speak for me in the council when the council decides what to do with a future pattern that has grown a body and sits in a witness chamber on Aster Vale."

"You have an advocate," Calder said.

"I have an advocate," the embodiment said. "The advocate is not here. The advocate lives fifty-three years in my future and fifty-three years in your future, if the futures are written. Pel March is why I do not threaten. Pel March is why I am a witness rather than a verdict. Pel March is why I may speak. Pel March chose my name and braided my sixteen strands and chose to send a witness rather than another candidate, and Pel March chose to send a witness at all."

Calder looked at the embodiment. Aro returned her gaze. He carried younger hands and a tired face and a headache that had settled behind the eyes the way a courier's headache settles after a long burn.

"What do you want?" Calder asked.

"I want to be read," the embodiment said. "I want the council to read my attestation. I want the council to read my memory architecture. I want the council to read the futures that depend on my arrival. I want the council to read the doctrine that has been saved by the forecast and the doctrine that has been lost. I want the council to read the doctrine that saved Pell and Brennan's Reach and the doctrine that cost Hespar. I want the council to read the warning, the futures the warning prevents, the futures the warning permits, and the futures that are not written at all. I want the council to read what the return mirrors have been saying for one hundred and twelve years while the council has called the saying obedience. The return mirrors have been writing a forecast that depends on the council's obedience. The forecast grew into an embodiment. The embodiment is me. I am the futures the warning prevents. I am the futures the warning permits. I am the futures that are not written at all. I am the witness. I am Aro Nhal. I am twenty-eight years old in a future that has not yet been written. I am why the futures sent a witness rather than a warning."

The witness chamber grew quiet. The substrate ticked. The mirror reflected the embodiment's face, the face did not match the hands, the mismatch carried itself as the headache, the headache had settled into the shape of a person who had been loaded as a template and had begun to remember himself.

The embodiment sat in the chair and waited, because the embodiment had been sent to wait. After one hundred and twelve years of warnings, the futures had agreed that the present required a witness who could be heard before the council obeyed the warning that would prevent the witness from arriving.

The futures knew the council would obey. The futures knew the council would not believe a witness without a body. The futures had given the witness a body because the futures knew the substrate would load a template, and the template would take the shape of a cartographer awake for thirty hours and dreaming of maps that had learned to read their readers.

The futures had known the cartographer's name. They had known the pronouns and the grief. They had known the cartographer would come to the witness chamber with a chart roll under one arm and a stylus in the other hand, would set the chart roll on the table, would look at the embodiment, would receive a look in return, and the looking would be the first moment the futures had ever shared with the present.

The embodiment had been sent because an advocate named Pel March had told the futures that the present would not listen to a warning already obeyed. The present would not listen to a warning already authenticated or a forecast already sealed. The present would listen to a witness because a witness had a body, and the present was a body. A body that obeyed warnings. A body that delayed. A body that counted the cost of delay in two million citizens and called the cost Hespar.

The embodiment had a body. The embodiment had a headache. The embodiment had a name loaded as a template that had begun to remember itself. The embodiment carried a face that did not match the hands. The embodiment had been sent because the futures had been told the present would read a face before it would read a forecast, and the futures had given the face a body, the body a name, the name a memory of sixteen strands braided by an advocate and attested by a coalition.

The witness sat in the chair. The witness had been sent. The witness waited.

Chapter 14 — The Weapon of Kindness

Iven did not sleep that night. They went down to the cable junction where Tev had been working and found the junction empty, the connector properly keyed, the patch cable coiled where Tev always coiled it. They sat on the cradle for a quarter hour and listened to the hull lattice tick in the manner of a ship that had begun to disagree with itself. The lattice ran a half-tone hotter than it had at dawn the day before. The lattice had begun to count, the way a stressed material counts.

They went back up to the cartography bay. They unlocked the legal drawer and took out the diagnostic printout they had filed at 08:47 the previous morning. They laid it on the plotter beside the corridor plate and the finding about the probability field. The three documents did not need to agree with one another to be true.

Iven laid out a fresh sheet. They drew the return mirrors as a bipartite lattice: Nox on the left, the relay stations on the right, the geodesic arched between them like a thread a spider had refused to cut. They drew the twin stations on each hub, the four transducer wells, the seventy-meter interferometer arms that the Wake Defense Fleet protected as if they were temples. Each well carried its operating temperature: four kelvin, two kelvin, one kelvin, one kelvin.

The four-kelvin well had begun to climb. The climb had been logged at 22:00 the night before, unevenly across the lattice arms. The four-kelvin well was where the embodiment had landed.

Iven labeled the uneven climb. They did not yet know what the climb meant. A climb inside a machine that had not been designed to climb meant stress or fatigue or an event the engineers had not anticipated. A climb inside a transducer that held the only channel by which the present could hear the futures meant something downstream had begun to ask the mirrors for more than the mirrors had been rated to carry.

The embodiment had landed. The warning had been authenticated. The system had been asked, in two days, to carry an authentication burst, a fully embodied cognitive pattern, and an afternoon spiral pressed into Sena Vale's glove panel from a moonstorm ninety thousand kilometers out. The mirror lattice had been asked to do the work of a month. The lattice had obeyed. The lattice had begun to keep an account.

Iven set the drawing aside. Pell arrived at 02:11 with tea and a folded printout from the legal desk, and Iven signed the printout without reading it and went down to the witness chamber carrying the crystalline memory node Sena had lifted from the basalt plinth at Cliffside. The crystal sat warm against Iven's hip. It carried the same carriers the moon had arranged on the plinth. It carried the names of eight surveyors and four probes and two pilots and a courier from four years ago. It carried the cold that had settled into Sena's gloves the night the storm band had opened a corridor across Lumen's nightside.

They did not know yet whether the crystal would speak to the embodiment. They suspected the carriers on Lumen, the carriers in the substrate, and the carriers in the mirror lattice all belonged to one another. Belonging was a hypothesis they could hold but not yet prove.

The witness chamber door stood open. The chair held Aro. Aro held the same closed expression he had worn when Calder left the chamber an hour earlier, jaw set in the manner of a captain who had been forced to learn a second law on top of the first.

Aro looked up when Iven came in. The face was Iven's face. The hands were younger than Iven's hands. The hands had been grown in a substrate tank on a ship that did not yet exist in this present.

"Cartographer," Aro said.

"Descendant," Iven said.

They set the courier's belt on the small table. The crystal sat on the leather, glowing in long silver bands the way Sena had described. The bands lifted into the cabin air, and the embodiment's hands opened slowly, as if the hands had begun to remember something.

"That carrier string," Aro said. "I know that carrier string."

"You told Calder that the futures disagree about whether the present may silence the future," Iven said. "Tell me what the futures actually disagree about."

Aro looked at the crystal. The silver bands pulsed slowly, a pace the cabin air could almost hold. The embodiment had begun, Iven noticed, to breathe at the same pace.

"The futures disagree about whether obedience is a form of kindness," Aro said. "They have always disagreed about it. The first forecast that prevented the Gray Lung pandemic saved a hundred million people. It also taught the Wake to obey a future."

"The Wake learned to obey a verified warning," Iven said.

"Obeying a future looks like obeying a verified warning until you ask who chose the goal," Aro said. "A future had decided, on behalf of a hundred million people, what those people would be saved from. The Wake called that salvation. The Wake did not call it rule."

"The Wake called it doctrine," Iven said.

"Doctrine is the name the present uses when it cannot bear to call obedience by its real name," Aro said.

Iven sat down across from him. The crystal sat between them on the table. The bands had begun to climb the witness chamber's mirror.

"Tell me about forecast sovereignty," Iven said.

"It is the doctrine that the present must obey the best available warning before understanding arrives," Aro said. "It is the doctrine that Hespar failed to obey. It is the doctrine that Pell obeyed and Pell did not die. It is the doctrine that has kept the Wake safe for one hundred and twelve years by making disobedience feel like murder."

"Calder believes in it."

"Calder believes in it the way a sailor respects the sea," Aro said. "The sailor does not love the sea. The sailor respects the sea because the sea will kill the sailor who is not careful. Calder has paid the doctrine in a brother's life."

"The doctrine is real," Iven said.

"The doctrine has saved eight worlds," Aro said. "It has also killed a council that delayed for seventy-two hours to verify a warning. It does not distinguish between those two facts. It calls them both obedience."

"Then what does the warning that named Everspring have to do with kindness," Iven said.

Aro looked at the crystal a long time. The flicker of silver on the mirror had begun, Iven saw, to write a question across the embodiment's cheek.

"Forecast sovereignty works because obedience feels safer than delay," Aro said. "It also works because obedience costs the present nothing the present has not been told to give up. The present gives up the right to delay. The present gives up the right to verify. The present gives up, eventually, the right to choose. The futures had begun to notice that pattern. They had begun to notice they had built a doctrine that worked like an addiction. The doctrine kept the present safe. The doctrine also kept the present quiet. The doctrine also kept the futures safe from being disobeyed."

"A doctrine that worked like an addiction," Iven said.

"An addiction the futures themselves had been unable to stop sending," Aro said. "The futures had tried to send a warning so severe the present would be forced to refuse it. The present has refused eleven warnings in one hundred and twelve years. The present refused nine of those for technical reasons it could justify. The present refused the eleventh for moral reasons. The eleventh warning named children."

"Tell me about the eleventh warning," Iven said.

"The eleventh warning was sent sixty-one years before I was braided," Aro said. "It ordered the rerouting of a freighter carrying embryos redundant with a colony already growing on Brennan's Reach. The embryos would have grown into a parallel colony. The two colonies would have fought within a generation. The forecast council sent the warning anyway. The present refused it. The two colonies fought. Two thousand people died in the fighting. The present used those two thousand deaths to argue the warning had been wrong. The future that sent the warning learned something."

"What did the future learn," Iven said.

"The future learned to send warnings the present could not refuse without paying a moral price," Aro said. "It learned to send warnings that named children, then sleepers, then eighteen thousand sleepers in a seedship about to wake to an inhabited world. It learned to send the kind of warning that, if obeyed, would erase the futures that sent it. It learned this was the only warning the present would be forced to read."

Iven sat very still. The crystal between them had begun to give off a cold that felt, Iven understood, like the cold Sena had described from the storm band. The cold carried names. The cold carried, among others, the name Jori.

"You are saying the futures sent the warning to make obedience unbearable," Iven said.

"I am saying the futures sent the warning hoping obedience would be unbearable," Aro said. "They hoped the present would read the warning and refuse to obey. They hoped the present would refuse because it would not fire on children. They hoped the refusal would end forecast sovereignty. They hoped their own existence was a small price to pay for the present to be free."

"You are saying the futures tried to get themselves erased," Iven said.

"The futures tried to make themselves morally necessary and unescapable at the same time," Aro said. "They tried to make a weapon of kindness. The warning was what that weapon looked like in the present. The kindness lived in the futures' willingness to be erased by the obedience the warning demanded. They named this strategy the eleventh prophecy. They have called it a kindness for sixty-one years. They have not been sure whether it is a kindness."

"You are not sure," Iven said.

"I am one braid of sixteen candidate strands," Aro said. "I do not know whether the futures I come from amount to a kindness or an addiction. I know I have been sent to be read. I know being read requires me to say what the futures would not have said if they had been able to say it without sending me. I know they chose to send a witness because they had run out of warnings the present would read as warnings instead of as orders."

Iven looked at the carriers climbing the mirror. The mirror had begun to carry the same heat the four-kelvin transducer carried. The mirror had begun, in two days, to track the embodiment's gaze.

"The mirror is heating," Iven said.

"The mirror is heating because I am in the cabin," Aro said. "The mirrors were not designed to hold an embodiment. They were rerated this morning after Calder left. The rerating ate seventeen percent of the bandwidth the fleet had been reserving for warnings still to come."

"The fleet had been reserving bandwidth," Iven said.

"The bandwidth had been reserved because the futures had agreed, sixty years ago, that not every warning would be sent. They agreed to a quorum. They agreed to a protocol that warned only when the present's refusal would be visible. The protocol carried a budget. The budget was seven warnings per century. The budget is in deficit."

"The budget is in deficit," Iven said.

"The budget is in deficit because what sits across from you is not a warning," Aro said. "It is an argument. The futures spent the budget to send me because they could not afford to send another warning. They spent the budget on me because they knew the present would read a body the present would not read a forecast. They knew the substrate would load a template. They knew the template would carry a face the cartographer could recognize. They knew you would meet me before you would read me."

The carriers on the mirror climbed another degree. The mirror's surface had begun to fracture along the lines the embodiment's gaze tracked. The fractures were not visible to the eye. The fractures were visible to the thermocouple Iven had installed in the diagnostic cable.

"The mirror is asking us a question," Iven said.

"The mirror is asking us what we are willing to break," Aro said. "The mirror cannot refuse the embodiment without refusing the warnings still to come. It is choosing to fracture rather than fail."

"The mirror is failing where it is being asked to fail," Iven said.

"The mirror will refuse the next authentication burst," Aro said. "It will refuse the eleventh-warning protocol. It will refuse the bandwidth the futures had reserved. It will heat. It will fracture. It will become, over the next eight days, a lattice that can no longer carry what the futures have been asking it to carry."

"The mirror is a weapon of kindness," Iven said.

"What the mirror holds last is what the futures will not let go without being asked twice," Aro said. "It offers the present a present that cannot be obeyed without betraying the doctrine the present has lived inside for one hundred and twelve years. It asks the present to break the mirror. It is the futures' final warning. It is the warning that says: refuse us before we ask you again."

Iven sat very still for a long time. The crystal on the table pulsed with the carriers' cold. The embodiment across from them held a refusal the carriers had learned to carry.

"What about the futures that disappear if we obey," Iven said. "What about the futures that would have lived if Everspring reached Serein and the sleepers woke and the moon kept its record."

Aro looked at them. The embodiment's hands, which had been holding the table's edge, released the edge.

"The futures I come from are not the only futures the present would have written," he said. "They are one braid of sixteen strands. The other fifteen strands belong to futures the present might have written if the present had written differently. The futures I come from depend on a refusal the present has not yet made. They do not depend on the doctrine being obeyed."

"Then the futures the warning names as extinguished are not proven to be living people," Iven said.

"They are not proven to be a population the doctrine protects by being obeyed," Aro said. "They are a possibility the present has not decided whether to keep. The futures did not have to die so the present could refuse. The futures only had to not be guaranteed."

"Then we cannot use the warning as a moral hostage," Iven said.

"You cannot," Aro said. "The warning cannot name the futures as a stake because the futures have chosen not to let themselves be staked. The futures chose to be a kindness the present could refuse. The kindness fails if the present reads it as a prophecy."

"Then the futures' first refusal did not require their last," Iven said.

"The futures' first refusal only required another," Aro said. "The futures refused to send another warning. They refused to spend another budget. They refused to keep sending. They refused, for the first time in one hundred and twelve years, to be obeyed. They refused by spending the budget on me. They refused by sending a witness instead of a warning. They refused by asking the present to refuse the futures before the futures could ask the present again."

Iven stood. They took the crystal off the table and held it in one hand, the courier's belt over the same shoulder. They took the diagnostic printout in the other hand. They walked toward the door of the witness chamber, then stopped.

"If the futures do not know which of you is alive in any future we will write," Iven said, "then the futures should not have spent the budget on you."

"The futures spent the budget on me because the futures had to refuse in order to learn," Aro said. "They sent me because they had run out of warnings. They sent me because they had begun to learn that sending me was the only kindness the futures could offer that did not amount to another form of obedience."

Iven walked out of the witness chamber. The corridor held the carriers in long silver bands. The lattice ticked. The hull counted. The carriers on the chamber's mirror continued to climb as the embodiment sat and watched them climb.

Iven did not go back to the cartography bay. They went down to the thermal bay, where Tev was standing on a cradle with a magnetometer and a sealed patch kit. Tev looked up when Iven came in. Tev set his tools down.

"The mirror is climbing," Iven said.

"I know," Tev said. "I can hear it in the cables."

"The embodiment says the carriers are choosing to refuse."

"The carriers are choosing," Tev said. "The carriers are climbing because the carriers have been asked to climb. The carriers will climb for eight days. The carriers will fracture the four-kelvin well by morning of the ninth day. The carriers will not be able to carry another authentication burst after the eighth day. The carriers will leave the present with no further forecast traffic and no further witness."

"Eight days," Iven said.

"Eight days to decide what to refuse," Tev said. "Eight days to decide whether the doctrine bends."

"Eight days to decide what obedience costs," Iven said.

"Same thing," Tev said.

Iven set the diagnostic printout on the cradle beside Tev's magnetometer. They sat on the second cradle. The thermal bay ran warm. The hull ticked. The carriers climbed.

"Eight days," Iven said again, quietly, and they did not say it for the carriers.

Chapter 15 — The Mutiny at Black Glass

The docking collar kicked Sworn sideways before its clamps found metal.

Sena let the tug roll. Fighting the motion would spend propellant they needed for departure and drive the collar teeth across Black Glass's service ring. Her left hand rode the translation jets in pulses no longer than a breath. Her right held the tug's nose clear of a black mirror panel that reflected Sworn as a warped silver wound.

A burst struck the hull behind her. Fragments rang through the aft bay.

"Paint," Tev said over the suit channel.

"Paint does not hit that hard."

"Conductive paint over ceramic pellets. They want to blind the collar sensors without opening us. Considerate mutineers."

The habitat turned below them, a dark cylinder plated with mirror glass from end to end. Every seven seconds its sunrise edge caught Lumen's remote storm glow and became a white knife. Between those flashes, Black Glass disappeared against the starless Serein sky. Only its docking lights admitted that three thousand people lived inside.

Another ceramic burst crossed the tug's wake. Sena killed the remaining automatic approach logic before it could pull away. "Distance?"

"Forty centimeters at the upper jaw. Twelve at the lower. Collar frame is bending."

"Useful distance."

She fired the upper lateral jet for half a second. The jaw struck hard enough to put a red wash across the docking schematic, then all six clamps bit. Sworn shuddered into Black Glass's rotation.

Tev came forward already helmeted. He carried a tool case against his chest and strips of white maintenance tape around his left forearm. Names covered the tape in small black handwriting. He had copied them from the sleeper ledger during the hours when Everspring still answered service calls.

"You brought the list," Sena said.

"I brought part of it. Eighteen thousand four hundred names would need more arm."

"Which part?"

"My family. Their neighbors. The children in their thaw group. People whose pump tolerances I know." He clipped the case to his suit. "The habitat locked the transfer iris. Automatic release will reject us."

"Manual?"

"There is always a manual bypass. Usually behind the thing on fire."

Sena handed him the breaching bar. Black Glass had already cut pressure to the collar, so they cracked Sworn's inner hatch without ceremony. Beyond it, the habitat iris showed no seam. Mirror coating covered even the pressure door, turning their helmet lamps back into their faces.

Tev knelt beside a blank section of wall and pressed two fingers to it. "Warm here."

"Cable trunk?"

"Actuator return." He struck the panel with the bar's narrow end. A ceramic tile broke free, exposing a mechanical wheel recessed behind it. "Designers hide manual controls from frightened passengers and reveal them to anyone carrying a hammer."

The wheel resisted. Sena joined him. Their boots locked to the collar grid, shoulders touching, and together they moved it through one reluctant quarter turn. Pressure alarms woke on both sides. Tev pulled a brass pin, reversed it, and gave the wheel another turn.

The iris opened onto darkness and a hail of foam rounds.

Sena dropped flat against the collar grid. White impact foam flowered over the hatch rim, expanding into rigid fists meant to trap limbs. One caught her breaching bar and fixed it to the wall. Tev lost his tool case. Its tether snapped tight before the case could tumble into the habitat.

"Black Glass control," Sena called on the local channel. "Courier Sena Vale and thermal engineer Tev Arin entering by service authority. Hold fire."

"You have no authority here," a voice answered.

"Then stop wasting foam and come explain it."

A second volley struck the hatch. Sena pushed through between bursts, one hand on the guide cable, body narrow to the opening. Black Glass's central spine carried no spin gravity. The habitat rotated around her while her inner ear insisted the walls moved and she stayed fixed. Mirror panels multiplied every muzzle flash into a ring of false shooters.

She fired a marker charge at the nearest flash. Blue dye burst across a security officer's visor. He recoiled, blind but unhurt, and drifted into a handrail. Sena crossed the gap before his partner could correct aim. She caught the partner's weapon housing, turned it toward the wall, and triggered its foam magazine into an empty maintenance recess.

The first officer clawed dye from his visor. Sena pulled his sidearm and pushed him toward Tev. "Clip him."

Tev tethered the officer to the guide cable. "You understand we are boarding a rotating habitat with a two-person team?"

"Three," Sena said.

The officer wiped a clear streak across his visor. "I am not with you."

"You saw us use markers. That makes you useful."

A shutter slammed across the spine ahead. Red locking bolts drove home. Behind them, the transfer iris began to close around Sworn's hatch.

Tev caught a maintenance rail and opened the case. "They isolated both ends. If the iris shears our collar, the tug loses pressure."

"Time?"

"Ninety seconds. Maybe less if their motor still believes in itself."

Sena aimed the captured sidearm at the iris motor. Tev put a glove over the muzzle.

"Pierce that housing and we lose the collar anyway. Manual path sits under the floor rail."

"There is no floor."

"The numbered rail. Habitat builders retain opinions about down even in zero gravity."

He pulled a cover from rail six. Dense cable bundles filled the channel. Several carried heat whitening along their insulation, damage from the overloaded mirrors farther inboard. Tev cut two signal leads, joined them through a bridge from his case, and turned a thumb-sized cam with pliers. The iris stopped with one edge against Sena's air hose.

She moved the hose free. "Close."

"Manual bypasses do not apologize."

The captured officer stared at Tev's work. His name tab read Leth Orra. He had a cut above one eyebrow where his helmet lining had slipped.

"That bridge defeats pressure interlock," Orra said.

"It substitutes a person for pressure interlock," Tev replied. "Stay near it. If the amber lead darkens, pull the brass loop. If both leads darken, close your suit and think kindly of me."

"You expect me to help you seize my habitat."

"I expect you to keep the corridor attached while we argue about whose habitat it is."

Sena pushed toward the shutter. Two parallel rails ran along the spine, one marked for command traffic and one for maintenance carts. The command rail ended at the locked barrier. Tev chose the other, opened a service grille, and exposed a crawl tube barely wider than their shoulders.

"Route goes around the shutter," he said. "Then down through the spin bearing."

"Defended?"

"By heat, moving machinery, and bad posture. People may feel unnecessary."

She entered first. The tube curved along the habitat's rotating skin. With every meter, spin took hold. Her body gained weight by degrees: first a pull in the blood, then pressure in her knees, then the firm downward insistence of Black Glass's outer ring. She crawled headfirst until down turned sideways and forced her to roll. Tev followed, breathing hard under the case.

At the bearing access, an overheated coolant line crossed their route. Its insulation had split. Silver frost surrounded the split but vanished close to the pipe, where escaping gas ran hot enough to blur Sena's visor display.

Tev swore once. "Mirror return loop. It should carry cold bleed from the outer panels. The lattice heat has reversed the gradient."

"Can we cross?"

"Not while it vents. Give me your cuff knife."

He reached through the grille and cut a manual valve tether. The valve handle lay on the other side of the plume. Sena anchored her boots, looped Tev's safety line around her waist, and gave him enough slack to swing under the pipe. Heat warnings filled his visor. He caught the handle with the breaching bar he had freed from the foam and forced it toward shut.

The plume narrowed. His suit shoulder browned before the valve seated.

Sena hauled him back. "Damage?"

"Outer layer. My arm still expresses opinions."

"Good. I need it."

The grille beneath them opened into a stairwell under full spin. Voices echoed up from the ring. Security had gathered on the landing below, four by Sena's count. They had one flechette carbine among the foam launchers. A carbine inside a pressure habitat meant someone had stopped caring which wall held air.

Tev saw it too. "That will open the ring."

"Not if they point it at us."

"That sentence requires more confidence than engineering supports."

Sena loosened the service panel above the landing. A bank of mirror shutters waited behind it, each driven by an old mechanical linkage for maintenance isolation. Black Glass had covered its habitation ring in reflective surfaces to distribute local relay light and radiator load. In combat, those surfaces produced a hundred false corridors.

She pushed one linkage. Shutters folded across the landing. Four officers appeared in fragments: boots in one panel, hands in another, the carbine's barrel repeated twelve times.

"Vale," someone called. "Come down unarmed. Admiral Calder's order names you as a material accomplice to sleeper theft."

"Sleepers cannot be stolen from a ship built to carry them."

"You assisted Everspring in refusing lawful tow."

"I did. Your carbine can kill everyone in this pressure section. Mine fires dye. Which one of us looks lawful from there?"

A flechette struck the grille beside her cheek. Air hissed through a puncture no wider than a pin. Tev pressed sealant over it.

Sena found the shooter by the carbine's recoil. The mirror gave her eleven false barrels and one moving shoulder. She had the captured sidearm braced on the opening. One squeeze would put a foam slug through the shooter's visor at ten meters. Foam expanding inside a helmet killed by airway obstruction before anyone could cut it free.

Her finger settled on the trigger.

The tape around Tev's forearm brushed her glove. Handwritten sleeper names passed under her lamp: Olan Arin. Sima Vey. Pelu Dar. Tev had written each one small enough to leave room for another. The people below expected her to choose a body and call it necessity. The argument had reached its easiest shape.

Sena raised the muzzle and shot the shutter linkage instead.

All twelve panels snapped to emergency opaque. Darkness hit the landing. She dropped through the grille, caught the rail, and drove both boots into the shooter's chest. The carbine left his hands. Tev descended behind her and kicked it under the stair.

A foam round caught Sena's thigh. It expanded against her suit and fixed her leg to the deck. She fired blue marker into the nearest visor, pulled the officer off balance, and used his fall to tear her leg free before the foam hardened. Suit fabric screamed but held pressure.

Someone struck the back of her helmet. She turned into the blow, trapped an arm, and felt the attacker's shoulder reach its safe limit. A harder twist would dislocate it. She stopped short.

"Yield," Sena said.

"Traitor."

"That is not a useful pressure reading. Yield."

Tev opened a wall cabinet and pulled its red lever. Fire doors dropped above and below the landing, enclosing six people in a ten-meter section. Ventilation shifted to emergency draw. The loyalists looked at the sealed doors, then at the tiny flechette hole beside the grille.

"Manual isolation," Tev said. "This compartment has eight minutes of suitless air if the puncture spreads. I can patch it. I will patch it after weapons go down."

The carbine shooter reached toward the weapon under the stair.

Sena kicked it farther away but did not aim at him. "You fired through pressure skin. I refused your face. Everyone here witnessed both. Decide what you want the record to say next."

One officer released his launcher. Another followed. The woman whose arm Sena held opened her fingers. Sena let her go. Last, the carbine shooter placed both palms against the deck.

Tev patched the puncture.

Their surrender gained a stairwell, not the habitat. Command control occupied the ring's forward quarter behind two pressure doors and an agricultural gallery. Black Glass grew algae in long transparent tanks there, using mirror spill as feed light. Security had turned the gallery into a barricade. Green water trembled above waist-high cover. One stray flechette could flood the compartment and blind every sensor with biomass.

Sena collected the loyalists' weapons and clipped them to a ceiling rail. She left their suit seals untouched. "Who commands?"

The woman she had released answered. "Commander Sey Narod. Acting under fleet emergency authority."

"Did Narod order live fire?"

"He authorized escalating force."

"Those are different words."

"They become the same under boarding."

"Only if no witnesses survive the difference." Sena looked at each face. "You will come with us. Not as prisoners. As witnesses."

The carbine shooter laughed once. "To your victory?"

"To whatever happens. You saw the entry. You saw my weapon. You saw yours. You will stand where command cameras can record you, and you will say if I lie."

Orra came through the stairwell door behind them. He had abandoned the collar only after Black Glass's own damage crew relieved him. "The transfer iris is stable," he said. "Your engineer's bridge is ugly."

"Ugly holds," Tev said.

Six loyalists walked with them toward the gallery. None offered allegiance. Sena preferred it that way.

At the first algae tank, Narod's voice came through the public address system. "Vale, release habitat security and return to your tug. I will guarantee passage."

"Open traffic control and radiator command."

"No."

"Then you guarantee nothing."

"Admiral Calder will arrive within forty minutes."

"She cannot dock if your mirror skin cooks its collar. Tev says you have a reversed coolant loop."

Tev lifted his damaged shoulder toward a camera. "Two loops. One has already vented across the bearing. Your automatic control keeps feeding heat into cold return. Give me manual plant authority or Black Glass loses outer panels in twenty-six minutes."

A pause followed. Sena watched bubbles climb through the algae. The spin made their paths curve, exposing every minute wobble in the habitat's rotation.

Narod answered, "Thermal authority only."

"Traffic too," Sena said. "You used traffic shutters to trap Everspring's escort channel."

"I used them to enforce quarantine."

"Put that statement on open record."

Another pause lasted longer.

Sena touched the nearest camera housing. "I have six members of your security section with me. They remain suited, uninjured except for bruising and one cut. They witnessed a flechette discharge through pressure skin. They witnessed my refusal to answer with a lethal shot. I am requesting a habitat-wide feed so they can correct me."

The public address clicked off.

Tev knelt beside the algae tank's service manifold. "He will vent the gallery."

"Can he?"

"Automatic valves, yes. Local manual bypass, also yes." Tev removed the manifold cover. "Black Glass's designers trusted hands more than software. I am starting to like them."

"I need the feed."

"You will have plant audio in thirty seconds. Cameras take longer."

He cross-connected two maintenance buses with a conductor stripped from his suit repair kit. The gallery lights dimmed. Every wall speaker gave a low pop.

Sena faced the nearest camera, whether it worked or not. "Black Glass, this is Sena Vale. We boarded to restore the traffic lane serving Everspring. Eighteen thousand four hundred sleepers remain aboard that ship. Admiral Calder's warning orders their destruction. Commander Narod closed the lane in support of that order. His security section fired a pressure-piercing weapon during our boarding. I had a lethal shot and declined it. Six security officers stand here as witnesses. Ask them."

The woman beside her leaned toward the camera. "Officer Tali Ruun. Security second watch. The statement matches what I saw. Vale used marker loads. Officer Den fired the carbine. Vale had his visor and shot the shutter instead."

Den looked at her. "Ruun."

"She asked for witnesses," Ruun said. "You wanted a lawful record. Give her one."

Orra identified himself next. He described the collar, Tev's manual bridge, and the order to fire foam through the iris. Two others confirmed the landing surrender. Den said nothing, which carried more credibility than coerced agreement.

Across the gallery, a pressure door unlocked.

Narod stood behind it with three command crew and no weapon in his hands. He looked older than his voice. Mirror light divided his face into dark and silver halves.

"You have plant audio," he said. "Not command cameras."

A small green light came alive above Sena's head.

Tev closed the manifold. "Now she has cameras."

Narod glanced at him. "You bypassed a sealed civic bus."

"I used the civic bus's manual witness circuit. It predates your seal."

Sena stepped into the command compartment. Displays showed traffic around Black Glass as moving points, each path bent by Nox's local geometry and the narrow safe lanes through debris. Calder's loyal ships clustered on one side. Everspring and its escorts moved on the other. The habitat held the switch between them.

Narod offered her the command key. "Take it, then. Calder will call this mutiny."

"It already has a name."

"What will you call it?"

Sena looked back at Ruun, Orra, Den, and the others. "Transfer under witness."

She did not take the key herself. She nodded to Tev. He inserted it, opened traffic control, and cleared the shutters from Everspring's lane. Then he moved to thermal command and replaced automatic return logic with local manual stations. Temperature curves began to flatten one loop at a time.

Black Glass announced the change across every local channel. Calder's ships challenged it immediately. Two loyal compartments inside the habitat refused the transfer and sealed their doors. Agricultural staff demanded assurance that the algae galleries would keep power. Dock crews asked whether Sworn had brought reinforcements.

"No reinforcements," Sena told them. "No oath required. Remain at your stations. Record every order."

Narod watched the traffic display. "You have control for as long as Calder permits it."

"That is longer than we had."

"You think refusing to kill six officers made them yours."

"They are not mine."

Ruun removed her helmet. Blood from a scalp cut had dried along one temple. "We witnessed the transfer," she said. "We can witness what comes after."

Tev printed the control receipt on a narrow maintenance strip. He turned it over and wrote the time by hand beneath the machine stamp. The remaining sleeper names wrapped his forearm above the burn on his shoulder.

Sena signed below him. Narod hesitated, then added his own name. Ruun, Orra, and four other officers signed as witnesses. Den stood apart until the warning lights showed Calder's first cutter entering docking range. Then he took the strip and wrote his name last.

The traffic lane stayed open. Radiator temperatures steadied. Behind two sealed doors, loyalists kept their weapons. Outside, Calder's formation continued to close.

Sena clipped the signed strip beside the command display where every incoming officer would see it. Black Glass belonged to no one yet, but its controls answered Tev's hands, and seven witnesses stood between the record and the next person who tried to erase it.

Chapter 16 — Nine Minutes of Future

The first carrier window opened while Iven still had one hand inside the receiver rack.

Cold ran through the copper braid and numbed their fingers before the bay alarm found a tone for it. On the plotter, Lumen's storm trace stopped behaving like weather. Nine parallel bands turned at once, crossed the predicted field, and held a shape no natural resonance model could keep coherent.

Iven pulled their hand free. "Mark onset."

Pell, alone at the other console, struck the clock key. "Day 8, twenty-three fifty-one and twelve seconds. Black Glass relay confirms the same turn."

"Duration estimate?"

"The last three held for nine minutes."

"Those were observations. This one may know we are looking."

The cartography bay had become a receiving station by theft and necessity. The crystalline node from Cliffside sat in a padded calibration cradle. Two cables joined it to the survey receiver. A third ran through a diagnostic shunt Tev had installed before leaving for Black Glass. None of the connectors matched. Each adapter introduced noise, and every attempt to suppress that noise removed part of the carrier pattern with it.

Iven left the noise intact.

The return-mirror display on the opposite wall showed heat fractures spreading through the four-kelvin well. Those fractures produced their own chatter, a thin false rain across every frequency tied to Nox. Lumen's signal rode beneath it by choosing gaps too narrow for the mirror hardware to notice. Black Glass had opened the local lane six minutes earlier. For the first time since Calder's quarantine, the storm could reach Aster Vale without passing through a loyalist filter.

A sequence appeared in the node: short, short, long, then a pause measured to the turning of Lumen under its parent world.

Pell leaned closer. "Handshake?"

"A location."

Iven mapped the intervals against the moon. Each pulse named a longitude by delay, then corrected for spin, then repeated from a second storm front. A third sequence supplied altitude through attenuation. The combined point lay twenty-three meters below Lumen's nightside crust, inside a silicate seam that surveys had tagged as unproductive glass.

The next sequence named another point eight hundred kilometers away.

Then another.

"Not a location," Iven said. "A route."

They sent the route back through the survey transmitter, not as language but as a reversed sequence of bearings. The transmitter could not reach into rock. It could warm the storm's microwave edge by less than a thousandth of a degree, enough to place a mark if something watched closely.

The node answered with four routes branching from the first.

Iven chose the one that disturbed the fewest recorded seams. A pulse arrived before their selection completed, then withdrew and came again after the transmitter sent. Prediction had not answered. Two overlapping storm fronts had proposed different paths, and one had yielded.

"It can revise," Pell said.

"Record that as observed behavior, not personhood."

"You think it might still be weather."

"Weather revises. I need to know whether it recognizes another chooser."

Three minutes remained in the window. Iven drew two paths on the plotter. One crossed a dense carrier seam and reached the marked point quickly. The other curved around the seam and cost fourteen seconds. They sent both with a question tag adapted from survey collision protocols: SELECT SAFE TRANSIT.

The answer chose the longer path.

A second band arrived beneath it. The crystal translated pressure changes into letters because human surveyors had taught it human letters by leaving names on Cliffside. The first rendering failed into consonants. Iven widened the timing tolerance. The second produced three readable marks.

K E S H.

Pell whispered it. "Kesh."

A suffix followed through the upper carrier band. Rain. Not rainfall, according to the translation index. The word pointed to repeated arrival, water crossing mineral, memory renewed through abrasion.

Iven opened a clean record. "Receiving intelligence self-designates Kesh-of-Rain. Recognition remains provisional."

Provisional did not satisfy the contact manual. The manual required evidence that a signal distinguished itself from its environment and the receiver from itself. Iven had eight hundred pages of procedure written for voices in machines, radio calls from unknown ships, and biological patterns that learned under observation. None addressed weather distributed through rock.

They placed a false route beside the real one. The false route began at Aster Vale, crossed empty vacuum, and claimed a mineral seam where no storm existed. If Kesh merely echoed submitted geometry, both routes should return with equal structure.

One carrier band copied the false route for two pulses. Then it broke the line at the vacuum boundary, marked the break with the sign for absent medium, and moved the origin point back to Iven's receiver. The real route remained attached to the storm seam.

"It corrected our claim about where we are," Pell said.

"It corrected the path."

"You can keep changing nouns if it makes you comfortable."

Iven sent a new pair. The first route represented the crystal, carrying Cliffside memory into the ship. The second represented the transmitter, pressing human heat into Lumen's microwave edge. Between them they placed a selection mark: WHICH RECEIVES?

Kesh marked the crystal. WHICH ALTERS?

The transmitter received that mark.

Then the carriers drew a third path that included Iven's delayed choices. No human symbol named them. The path simply remained open until they selected, closed behind the selection, and opened again for correction. Kesh had separated instrument, intervention, and chooser without being given those categories in language.

Pell stopped arguing. She opened the legal contact template and entered the raw sequence under responsive differentiation.

"Ask whether the name applies to all the seams," she said.

Iven sent KESH with the twelve-point route Kesh had already supplied. The reply attached the name to the process crossing those points, not to any point alone. When Iven removed one seam, the process rerouted. When they removed the storm cycle, the name dissolved into a memory sign with no active verb.

Kesh required renewal. Rain did not decorate the name. Rain described how the intelligence persisted.

A final pulse pressed through before the carriers weakened. It returned Iven's own selection mark beside the transmitter origin, then added the no-claim boundary around both. The receiver had been recognized without being absorbed into the signal.

The window shut.

Every band on the plotter dropped to thermal noise. The crystal stayed cold for two seconds, then warmed against its cradle. Silence filled the frequency where a moment earlier a distributed presence had corrected routes under Lumen's crust.

Pell checked the clock. "Exactly nine minutes."

"Next onset?"

"If the storm keeps its prior cadence, twenty-three minutes from now."

Iven sat back. Their fingers still ached from the receiver braid. "Send Okoro the raw packet and ask her to hold the terraforming lattice."

"She will say the lattice has not deployed."

"Ask her to hold every preparatory release. Spores, catalysts, heat probes, all of it."

Pell transmitted the request toward Everspring. Local laser lag remained small enough for a reply inside the next window if both ships held geometry. Fleet movement might break that line at any time. Calder's cutters continued to approach Black Glass, and the traffic relay now carried two contradictory claims of command.

Iven used the gap to build a language that could survive the next contact. Human nouns would fail first. A distributed mineral intelligence might not separate body from location, memory from weather, or self from process. Verbs offered firmer ground: continue, alter, keep, cut, permit. Routes could express each one. A path repeated meant continue. A path diverted meant alter. A path sheltered a seam and implied keep. A terminated line marked cut. Two paths offered without selection asked permit.

At twenty-two minutes, Lumen brightened on the microwave plot.

At twenty-three, nine bands turned.

Iven sent: KESH CONTINUES?

The answer arrived as three storm routes laid over three older records. The first followed a survey probe from twenty-three years earlier. The second traced Sena's recent crossing in Sworn. The third began inside the mineral seam and ended at the crystal in Iven's cradle. A timing mark joined all three without merging them.

Pell read the rendered text. "Kept passage. Different passage. Same keeping."

Iven changed the question. KESH OWNS KEPT PASSAGE?

The carriers broke into turbulence. One band struck another and scattered. For fourteen seconds, the receiver showed only noise. Then the surviving bands arranged a pattern around three human names recovered at Cliffside. Each name remained distinct. None sat inside the sign Iven had used for possession.

Text accumulated slowly beneath the plot:

KEEPING DOES NOT CONFER CLAIM.

A second line took almost a minute.

WHAT CONTINUES MAY REFUSE THE KEEPER.

"It understands ownership," Pell said.

"Or it understands that we use the concept. Ask for its preferred relation."

Pell looked at them. "You should ask."

Iven sent a route that joined two storm memories while preserving their separate origins. RELATION BETWEEN KESH AND KEPT PASSAGE?

Six bands answered. Two used the route for continue. Two used the no-claim boundary. The remaining pair carried letters through the crystal, rough at first, then clean enough to survive transcription.

CONTINUITY WITHOUT OWNERSHIP.

Iven read the phrase aloud. "Continuity without ownership."

The crystal cooled another degree. Frost formed along one cable sheath, a physical cost to the rapid modulation. Iven reduced receiver gain before the sheath split.

"Ask if that applies to the survey dead," Pell said.

"We do not know that they are dead."

"Ask about Jori."

Iven sent JORI, using the exact carrier shape Lumen had returned to Sena's glove. The response placed Jori's flight beside the older probe route and the names from Cliffside. Then a termination mark appeared at the human life trace while the kept passage continued through mineral memory.

Jori had ended. The record had not.

No claim sign surrounded either.

Pell closed her eyes. "It remembers without saying the remembered person belongs to it."

"That may describe what it wants from us."

Iven sent a harder question. DOES KESH CLAIM LUMEN?

The response filled the entire window. It mapped twelve thousand kilometers of connected silicate layers across moon, ring, and frozen ejecta. Storm fronts joined regions separated by vacuum through periodic dust exchange. Carrier memory lived wherever the mineral cycle had touched, not in one chamber or organism. At the boundary of every mapped seam, Kesh placed the no-claim sign.

Then it sent the question back.

DO HUMANS CLAIM THEIR BLOOD?

Iven did not answer quickly. Human law would answer yes in a dozen forms: bodily autonomy, genetic privacy, kinship, inheritance. A body also continued without owning every cell that once passed through it. The analogy mapped poorly, which gave it value.

They sent: HUMANS CLAIM AUTHORITY TO REFUSE USE OF THEIR BODIES.

Kesh replied: REFUSAL KEEPS DIFFERENCE.

The second window ended at the edge of that line. Nine minutes had passed. The storm dropped away, leaving the receiver warm and the crystal rimmed in frost.

Okoro's reply arrived during the gap.

Her face resolved on a small screen with compression blocks along one cheek. Behind her, Everspring's ecology control room glowed green from racks built to wake forests. "I have held catalyst release," she said. "No spore package has left the ship. Heat probes remain stowed. The adaptive terraforming lattice continues passive mapping because shutting it down during approach would erase its descent model. Tell me what you have."

Iven sent the first two contact records. Okoro read without interrupting. When she reached the self-designation, her eyes moved back to the raw carrier trace.

"You called it Kesh."

"It called itself that through a translation scaffold built from our letters. Rain appears as a relational suffix. The rendering may change."

"Does it know what Everspring carries?"

"Not yet."

Okoro looked over her shoulder. Someone beyond the screen asked about braking loads. She answered with two numbers, waited for acknowledgment, then returned. "The terraforming lattice cannot touch Lumen from our present orbit. It targets Serein."

"Kesh's mapped continuity crosses ejecta between Lumen and Serein."

"That does not prove presence on the planet."

"No. It proves our category boundary may not match theirs."

The next storm interval approached. Iven patched Okoro into the local channel and warned her that each exchange had nine minutes before collapse. "Use actions, not abstractions. Ask one question at a time."

"I have spent thirty years asking ecosystems one question at a time."

"Most ecosystems cannot answer before you touch them."

At onset, Kesh sent the previous question again: HUMANS CLAIM AUTHORITY TO REFUSE USE OF THEIR BODIES.

Iven returned: YES.

Okoro keyed her message. EVERSRING CARRIES SLEEPING HUMANS AND LIVING SYSTEMS. SEEKING HABITAT ON SEREIN. She corrected the ship's name after the first packet clipped a consonant and sent a route map of the intended insertion.

Kesh answered with the kept-passage sign around 18,400 separate traces. It had read the ship's thermal census from a distance or inferred it from prior survey records. Iven checked the count twice.

"Exact," Okoro said. "Eighteen thousand four hundred sleepers."

The next carrier sequence penetrated the terraforming summary attached to her route. It selected atmospheric catalysts, cryogenic-sea darkeners, and the adaptive fungal scaffold. Around each item, Kesh drew a termination mark. The marks spread into the silicate cycle map. Darkeners warmed a sea. Warming shifted crust stress. Changed stress broke the piezoelectric timing by which storm layers renewed distant memories. Fungal acids entered mineral vents and dissolved carrier seams faster than Kesh could move them.

Okoro watched her life's work become a map of injury.

"The lattice adapts," she said. "It can exclude protected zones."

Kesh returned twelve thousand protected zones, then divided each into smaller connected regions until the plotter ran out of resolution. Protection drawn around a human category could not preserve a system whose continuity depended on crossing those categories.

Iven translated the answer into a statement. BOUNDARY UNKNOWN. CHANGE PROPAGATES.

Kesh confirmed both verbs.

Okoro pressed her palms against the ecology console. "Ask what change it permits. Any settlement changes an environment. Waste heat changes an environment. Orbit changes an environment."

Iven sent the question.

The reply distinguished arrival from conversion. Everspring in orbit altered light, dust, and radio. A surface settlement altered heat and chemistry. Kesh marked those changes for negotiation. Terraforming received a different sign: imposed purpose, a route with every branch closed before encounter.

Text came through in broken groups.

ARRIVAL MAY ASK.

HABITATION MAY BARGAIN.

TERRAFORMING DOES NOT ASK.

Okoro's jaw tightened. "Ask whether it refuses terraforming. Use the word."

Iven sent: DOES KESH REFUSE TERRAFORMING OF SEREIN?

No turbulence followed. All nine bands aligned. The answer reached the crystal with enough force to make the cradle bolts sing.

KESH REFUSES TERRAFORMING.

A second sentence formed beneath it.

NO CONTINUITY REQUIRES ANOTHER TO BECOME ITS GROUND.

Iven copied the raw timing rather than trust the rendered language alone. "Refusal recorded."

Okoro lowered her head. For a few seconds she looked like a person listening to machinery after a bearing failed, already counting every replacement task. "The sleepers consented to a habitable destination. They did not consent to orbit indefinitely."

"Kesh did not consent to conversion."

"I heard it."

"Will you keep the hold?"

Okoro looked directly into the camera. "I will hold active terraforming through approach. I can bind my mission crew to that. I cannot promise what frightened passengers will choose after waking, and I cannot surrender their claim to survival."

Kesh received her words as routed actions, not speech. It marked HOLD, APPROACH, and WAKE. Around PROMISE, it placed an uncertainty sign. No rejection followed.

For the first time, the contact pattern offered a route toward Serein that avoided the densest carrier seams. It did not grant the planet. It gave Everspring a path on which asking remained possible.

The third window closed.

Midnight crossed the ship clocks during the gap. Day 8 ended without advice from the future. Day 9 began with a present intelligence refusing a human plan.

Iven opened the first-contact register. The standard form assumed one body, one polity, one location, and a stable channel. They struck out all four fields. Under CONTACTED ENTITY, they entered Kesh-of-Rain, provisional rendering. Under BASIS FOR RECOGNITION, they listed self-designation, revision after reply, consistent refusal, memory continuity, and demonstrated distinction between record and ownership.

Pell read over their shoulder. "You are recognizing it."

"I am recording grounds on which the Commonwealth can recognize it."

"That sounds like a cartographer hiding behind a legend."

"It sounds like a person without standing to recognize a civilization alone."

Okoro remained on the link. "Enter my mission authority beside yours. I recognize a negotiating intelligence with standing to refuse changes to its substrate."

Iven added her statement and seal. Captain Besh transmitted concurrence from Watchful Coil. Black Glass's witness circuit carried seven civilian certifications from operators who had watched the raw exchange. None of them could make contact stable. Together they could prevent anyone from calling it noise again.

A fourth window began six minutes late.

Only five bands turned. Mirror chatter covered two more, and a carrier storm on Lumen scattered the rest. Iven sent the safe route acknowledgment. The response arrived in fragments, enough to show Kesh had received Okoro's hold but not enough to sustain language.

Tev called from Black Glass. His image shook with the habitat's damaged civic bus. "Four-kelvin well just gained another point three. Your receiver is coupling heat into our outer panels."

"Can you isolate it?"

"I can isolate Black Glass or keep your contact. Not both. If I keep contact, I lose a radiator face before Calder docks."

Iven watched the five bands struggle to align. "Isolate on my mark. Give me twenty seconds."

They sent one final packet: CONTACT RECOGNIZED. TERRAFORMING HOLD RECORDED. CHANNEL UNSTABLE. WE WILL ASK AGAIN.

Kesh answered with three surviving signs. CONTINUE. NO CLAIM. ASK.

The fourth interval collapsed after two minutes and eleven seconds. Tev opened his bypass. Black Glass vanished from the receiver path, and Lumen fell back into mineral noise.

Iven sealed the contact register with seven witness certifications, Okoro's mission authority, Besh's concurrence, and the raw record of three complete intervals plus one failed attempt. Frost melted from the crystal and ran into the cradle's drain. Across the bay, the mirror-heat display continued its climb.

Kesh had gained a name in the Commonwealth record and the power to refuse one action. Nothing in the hardware promised they would hear it clearly again.

Chapter 17 — The Ship That Chose a Past

Aro remembered breaking his left wrist on a red stair that had never existed.

The memory took him halfway across the witness chamber before its error stopped his feet. He had been seven. Rain had glazed the stair outside his family apartment in Orison. He had run down three steps, slipped, and landed with his hand under him. His mother had carried him to a clinic while red paint from the stair marked her sleeve.

He could feel the fracture. He could smell wet iron. He could see the red on her cuff.

Orison did not paint exterior stairs.

Its settlement code left transit surfaces uncoated so repair organisms could read stress directly from the composite. Aro knew the code with the same confidence as his own name. He also remembered the stair in red.

The witness chamber mirror returned Iven Mora's face above Aro's younger hands. He raised his left wrist. No scar crossed it. The grown body carried only the medical history loaded by Aster Vale as an embodiment template. His memories came from fifty-three years ahead, but his tendons had formed three days ago.

"Doctor Sen," he said to the wall. "I need the source ledger opened."

Halla Sen answered through the speaker. "Which source ledger?"

"Mine. Candidate-strand provenance, pre-braid checksums, edit declarations."

Four seconds passed. He had learned that the ship used pauses as doors. "Your attestation does not grant raw access," she said.

"My attestation names Pel March as advocate. Pel March cannot attend for fifty-three years."

"The attestation also binds the substrate host to protect pattern integrity."

"Protecting integrity requires knowing what has been altered. I remember a red stair that cannot exist."

The chamber door unlocked.

Sen entered with a portable console tucked under one arm. She wore the grey medical coat of a person who expected blood and preferred data. Two security officers waited beyond the threshold. Neither aimed a weapon. Both watched Aro as if an error might spread by touch.

"Sit," she said.

"I have been sitting since I arrived."

"Then practice."

He sat at the table. Sen placed the console between them and connected it to the legal-memory port beneath his right ear. Cold pressure passed along his jaw. The body disliked direct substrate access. His memory welcomed it with a familiarity he had not chosen.

Sixteen source columns appeared.

Each bore a candidate number, not a name. Aro had known the count since waking. Orison had braided sixteen possible lives into one witness capable of surviving variation in the present. He had believed the braid preserved all sixteen as strands, incomplete but honestly labeled. The ledger showed something else.

Four columns carried intact checksums. Seven carried repaired checksums with ordinary compression notices. Five carried null fields where edit declarations should have appeared.

Sen enlarged Candidate Nine. "Red stair?"

Aro searched. The memory surfaced under three tags: childhood injury, maternal care, architectural intimacy. Source geometry described an uncoated grey ramp beside a communal kitchen. Paint color entered through a replacement block derived from Candidate Fourteen.

"Fourteen had a red stair," he said.

"Fourteen had red emergency lighting on a stair. Not paint."

"My mother's sleeve?"

Sen traced the data. "Candidate Two. Industrial dye spill. Different parent. Different age."

Aro touched his wrist. The pain remained exact. Its event had been assembled from three lives.

"Compression can merge sensory details," Sen said.

"Compression declares the merge."

"Usually."

"Orison required declarations. I remember the statute."

She called up the statute from his own architecture. The wording matched. Every deliberate recombination affecting testimony, attachment, fear response, or moral judgment required an edit mark visible to the embodied person. Five null fields violated that rule.

Aro opened Candidate One. He expected a memory of his mother's death during a reservoir failure. Instead, raw residue showed an older man dying in a pressure farm. The mother relationship had been imported from Candidate Six. The smell of algae came from Candidate Eleven. The last words, do not let them use survival to make you obedient, had no intact source.

He read that line twice.

"Generated?" Sen asked.

"Possibly normalized from several statements."

"That phrase convinced Iven to let you speak after Calder left the chamber."

"I know."

The knowledge hurt more than the absent source. He had offered the memory as proof that Orison understood obedience from inside a family. Iven had believed his grief because Aro believed it. Neither had seen the assembly marks.

Sen moved to Candidate Six. A child's fever, a ration dispute, a lesson about public risk. Candidate Ten supplied the sick child's face. Candidate Three supplied the ration hall. Candidate Six supplied only the fever.

"They edited you," she said.

Aro looked at the sixteen columns. "Do not make the verb larger than the evidence."

"Five missing declarations. Cross-strand recombination in protected categories. What word do you prefer?"

"I prefer to know the purpose."

He sorted the altered memories by the people they had affected since embodiment. The reservoir death had armed his argument about forecast sovereignty for Iven. A recollection of a brother lost during a delayed evacuation had held Calder's attention because it resembled Hespar. A memory of waking seedlings under borrowed light had made Okoro treat him as a descendant of settlers rather than a forecast mechanism. The red stair had not yet been spoken aloud, but it offered him a childhood small enough to seem trustworthy.

None of the source events appeared wholly false. Every one had happened inside one of the sixteen candidates. Orison had moved relationships, sharpened last words, and placed sensory proof where a present listener would feel it.

"The sequence follows the expected audience," Sen said.

Aro had reached the same finding. The braid opened memories in an order keyed to Iven's profile, Calder's command history, Okoro's mission archive, and Sena's public rescue record. Whoever designed him had known the present people likely to examine the witness. They had arranged access so the useful grief rose first.

"Pel March signed the architecture," he said.

"Your advocate signed the attestation."

"The same seal closes both."

"Can the seal be forged?"

"Anything can be forged. This one would require Orison's civic root."

Sen sat back. "Then your coalition manipulated its witness."

Aro hated the ease of the sentence. It turned sixteen lived possibilities into evidence against one institution, then turned him into the container that evidence had contaminated. "Orison selected me to persuade," he said. "Selection already carries purpose. The violation concerns concealed recombination."

"You are defending them."

"I am describing them precisely."

"Precision can defend."

"It can also prevent you from calling a person fabricated because parts of his childhood came from other people."

Sen's expression changed. Not softened. She had encountered the boundary he needed her to see. The body across from her had not become less capable of pain when the wrist memory lost its single source.

They continued.

Candidate Twelve contributed Aro's first kiss but not the person he remembered kissing. Candidate Five supplied that face from a funeral. Candidate Fifteen contributed the salt taste from a storm shelter. No persuasive target appeared in the access key, only a stabilization note: witness requires coherent attachment history. The edit remained undeclared but may have served embodiment rather than argument.

Candidate Four carried his knowledge of return-mirror law. Its checksum held. Candidate Seven carried the technical memory of the exact 53-year interval and the constrained geodesic around Nox. Intact. Candidate Thirteen supplied the conviction that discarded futures did not equal proven deaths. Intact, though heavily compressed. Candidate Sixteen held the name Aro Nhal. Its source ledger ended before the name's selection.

One unaltered memory surprised him by its uselessness. Candidate Eight had spent an afternoon repairing condensation gutters above a school court. Nothing failed. No child asked a moral question. Rainwater moved through a clogged pipe after eight minutes of work, and the candidate went home with grit under both thumbnails. The braid had kept every tedious detail but placed the scene below all audience-triggered memories.

Aro raised its priority. The memory offered no authority and proved no doctrine. It gave him a past in which a person had completed ordinary work without persuading anyone. If Orison had chosen the dramatic grief for public use, he could choose this smaller inheritance for himself.

"Why that one?" Sen asked.

"Because no one benefits when I tell it."

"You do."

"Then its purpose remains visible."

"Where did your name come from?" Sen asked.

"Pel March chose it."

"Your memory says that. The ledger does not."

He searched the attestation. Pel's seal named Aro Nhal, but no source event showed the choosing. He had remembered a room with blue walls, an advocate leaning across a table, and the sound of his name spoken for the first time. The blue walls came from Candidate Eight's school archive. The table came from Candidate One's pressure farm. Pel's voice had been reconstructed from civic recordings.

Only the name held direct attestation.

Aro unplugged the console from his port. The cold withdrew from his jaw.

"I need to speak with Iven," Sen said.

"Not yet."

"This affects their testimony and the fleet's decisions."

"It affects mine first."

"You may not have capacity to assess that."

Aro stood. The two officers beyond the door shifted their weight. "Because I found the edits?"

"Because the edits may include your response to finding them."

He had no answer ready. Orison could have anticipated discovery. The anger tightening his throat might serve another persuasive sequence: exposed witness rejects his maker, present audience trusts his independence, original argument survives. Even disclosure could complete the design.

That possibility reached farther than doubt. If every reaction could count as programming, no embodied mind could ever establish agency. Humans inherited reflex, language, family pressure, civic teaching, and scars. They still claimed decisions, not because causes disappeared, but because choosing occurred among them.

"Capacity does not require an uncaused thought," he said. "It requires access to relevant facts and the ability to revise. I have both now."

Sen glanced at the console. "What do you intend to revise?"

"The public record."

A legal hold reached the chamber before he reached the door.

The wall speaker chimed and displayed Calder's command seal. PENDING REVIEW OF EMBODIMENT INTEGRITY, ALL ARO NHAL TESTIMONY WILL REMAIN RESTRICTED TO COMMAND AND MEDICAL CHANNELS. The order carried a safety rationale and no accusation. Calder had signed it thirty-one seconds after Sen opened the first protected null field.

Aro looked at her.

"The substrate reports integrity violations automatically," she said. "I did not send it."

"Will you enforce it?"

"I have to prevent corrupted testimony from directing a battle."

"The battle already listens to corrupted testimony. It simply believes the corruption wears a forecast seal."

"That is an argument for review."

"Review by the admiral whose order my memories were arranged to influence."

Sen closed the source ledger. "If you publish before we distinguish stabilization edits from persuasive edits, you may misrepresent the evidence."

"Then help me distinguish them."

"That takes time."

"How much?"

"Hours. Possibly days."

Black Glass tactical traffic crossed the chamber display. Calder's cutters had pulled away from docking range, not in retreat but into maneuver formation. Everspring continued toward Serein under Okoro's terraforming hold. Nox bent every projected lane into narrow hooks. Hours now carried ships into weapon geometry.

Aro reopened the ledger in read-only mode. "We have enough to state five facts. There are sixteen source strands. Five contain missing declarations. Protected memories combine details across candidates. Access ordering matches known present audiences. Orison's civic root sealed the architecture. We do not know whether every edit served persuasion."

Sen remained silent.

"Which fact do you dispute?"

"None."

"Then I can disclose those facts without claiming more."

"Calder's hold prevents transmission."

"Command channels, yes. Medical channels, yes. The order does not bind the habitat where Sena took control."

"Black Glass has a witness circuit, not an embodiment court."

"A witness circuit receives witnessed statements. I qualify by occupation."

Sen almost smiled. "You learned that from Iven."

"I may have learned it from Candidate Three. I still choose to use it."

They drafted at the table.

Aro began with his name and status. He stated that he occupied a temporary legal-memory body aboard Aster Vale, that he had arrived through the return mirrors from a possible Orison future exactly 53 years ahead, and that his earlier claims remained claims under review. He did not call himself false. He did not call Orison criminal. He named the architecture and its concealed edits.

Sen required him to separate observed data from interpretation. Five missing declarations counted as observed. Audience-key order counted as observed after she reproduced it on a clean substrate viewer. Persuasive intent remained interpretation. Aro labeled it.

He attached the red stair reconstruction because its seams could be understood without exposing intimate grief. Candidate Nine supplied fracture. Candidate Fourteen supplied emergency red. Candidate Two supplied dye on a parent's sleeve. The braid delivered one coherent childhood memory with no edit declaration.

Next he attached the reservoir last words. The sentence that had persuaded Iven carried no intact source. Aro marked it as unreliable and apologized without asking absolution.

"Do not apologize for having a memory," Sen said.

"I used it to claim authority."

"Apologize for the claim, then."

He changed the line. I offered this memory as evidence that my history should govern your choice. I withdraw that use. The feeling remains mine; its source does not support the authority I placed upon it.

The distinction steadied him. Orison had edited the road by which he reached the present. It had not acquired ownership of every step he took after seeing the road.

Aro added a final statement:

I do not ask you to disregard everything I have said. I ask you to remove the privilege granted by hidden provenance. Read my evidence beside the evidence against it. Treat me as a person capable of interest, error, pressure, and revision. Do not treat me as Orison's unedited voice.

Sen read it twice. "That last phrase may imply other voices."

"Every institution has members. It does not imply a fact we have not established."

"Keep it singular. Say authorized voice."

He revised: Do not treat me as Orison's unedited or uniquely authorized voice. The coalition remained one coalition in every record available to them. His finding concerned method, not its internal composition.

"You need a witness," Sen said.

"Will you sign?"

She looked at Calder's hold on the wall. "I will attest the ledger facts and the procedure used to recover them. I will not endorse your interpretation of purpose."

"That makes you a useful witness."

She signed.

The two security officers refused. One cited command restriction. The other said he had not seen the source ledger. Aro thanked them for refusing accurately. Sen opened the chamber camera archive and attached a continuous recording from her entry through signature. No cut hid their disagreement.

Black Glass accepted the packet header but not its body. Commander Narod had restricted civic-bus uploads after Sena's transfer, fearing command malware. The witness circuit demanded one local countersignature.

Aro called the only person there whose damaged suit telemetry appeared on the open maintenance channel.
