Tev answered with his left arm out of the suit sleeve and a burn patch across his shoulder. "You have forty seconds before I close this bus for maneuver."

"I need a countersignature."

"For what?"

"Orison edited my sixteen memories to increase persuasive force. Doctor Sen and I recovered concealed recombinations. I am sending a public disclosure."

Tev read the summary at maintenance speed, skipping every adjective and stopping at each checksum. "Does the packet tell people what to decide?"

"No."

"Does it tell them what you found?"

"Yes."

"Does Calder know?"

"Her legal hold occupies page two."

"Then she knows enough to object."

Tev applied his Black Glass witness key. The packet moved into the civic buffer.

A new barrier appeared: EMBODIED TESTIMONY REQUIRES ORIGIN AUTHENTICATION.

Aro's origin seal belonged to Orison. Using it would authenticate the statement through the same authority he challenged. Refusing it would leave the packet unsent.

"Problem?" Tev asked.

"The circuit wants Orison's seal."

"Do you have another?"

Aro looked at the source ledger, the medical attestation, and the body grown from Iven's template record. "I have my substrate identity. It proves continuity from reception, not origin."

"Use that."

"It does not prove who I was before arrival."

"Your statement concerns what you can prove now."

Aro replaced the origin authentication with his local embodiment key. The circuit warned that recipients could not verify him as Orison's emissary. He accepted the warning and placed it at the top of the packet rather than burying it in metadata.

Black Glass opened the route.

The destination list filled with every ship accepting civic witness traffic, every habitat listening through the local relay, Everspring's mission archive, and Calder's command vessel. Aro did not add the first-contact register. Kesh had not asked to receive human evidence, and the contact channel could not sustain it.

Sen put one hand beside his on the send plate. "Once transmitted, this cannot be recalled from all nodes."

"That gives the statement a past."

"You say that as if choosing a past makes you free."

"No. It makes revision possible. A hidden past cannot be answered."

The tactical alarm changed pitch. Across the wall display, Calder's ships began a coordinated burn toward Nox. Black Glass announced maneuver traffic and warned all unsecured bodies to brace. Aro had never felt acceleration in his source body. The grown body knew only Iven's medical tolerance.

He pressed send.

The packet left Aster Vale through Black Glass with his local key, Sen's factual attestation, Tev's countersignature, the continuous chamber record, and Orison's civic seal preserved as contested evidence rather than borrowed authority. The first destination acknowledged receipt. Then a second. The wider public route continued beyond the chamber's ability to recall it.

Aro kept his hand on the plate until the buffer emptied. He had not disproved Orison, restored sixteen untouched lives, or discovered which grief began as his. He had done one narrower thing.

He had chosen to disclose the edits before anyone could use them again without knowing.

Chapter 18 — The Burning Ephemeris

Acceleration flattened Calder's tongue against her teeth as Resolute Vigil began the Nox burn.

She kept her jaw loose and watched the formation clock instead of the pressure darkening her vision. Six loyal ships lit their drives within four-tenths of a second. Linden's Edge lagged by another three-tenths, then recovered. The freighter-screen behind them did not burn. Those hulls carried civilians, fuel, machine shops, and every consequence of command that admirals preferred to draw as symbols.

"Burn stable," Lieutenant Saye reported from propulsion. "One point eight standard. Radiators at combat angle."

"Formation?"

"Wide by eleven kilometers at the lower hook. Imala correcting."

Calder saw Linden's Edge shift on the plot. The correction spent reaction mass and pushed Captain Imala nearer the inner line around Nox. Nothing crossed that line. The primordial black hole carried no visible surface, only a wound in the stars and a moving distortion where its frame-dragging caught light that had no business curving so sharply.

Their maneuver used that distortion. The loyal formation would fall inside Everspring's projected approach, rotate through a narrow gravitational arc, and emerge across the seedship's braking corridor. No impossible turn. No sudden stop. Mass entered one vector and left on another after eleven minutes of thrust, eight minutes of coast, and a burn that would leave every crew member bruised.

The ephemeris gave them six meters of margin at closest approach.

Calder had trusted wider margins before.

A message icon opened beside the tactical plot. EMBODIMENT INTEGRITY DISCLOSURE. PUBLIC WITNESS ROUTE. It carried Aro Nhal's local substrate key, Doctor Sen's signature, Tev Arin's countersignature, and a warning that Orison origin authentication had not been used.

"Hold it," Calder said.

Comms officer Pellan glanced back. "Admiral, it has already propagated."

"Hold it from command review until after the burn."

"Yes, Admiral."

The order did not unsend the disclosure. Calder knew that. She wanted one surface in the next nineteen minutes that did not move under her hand.

"All ships, confirm physical ranging," she said. "Mirror ephemeris remains advisory. We fly local instruments."

Six confirmations returned. Linden's Edge answered last.

Outside, Nox bent the formation's laser net. Pulses took curved paths through the frame-dragging region, and each navigation computer corrected using clocks disciplined by the return-mirror lattice. For one hundred and twelve years, those clocks had carried timing updates from exactly 53 years ahead. Now their references ran hot.

The first mirror alarm appeared on Cairn Eight.

Pellan read it. "Four-kelvin well above rerated maximum. Captain Lidon requests permission to isolate forecast timing."

"Granted. Local cesium reference. Mark drift."

A second alarm arrived from Linden's Edge. Then Resolute Vigil's own mirror status changed from amber to red.

Calder felt nothing through the deck. Mirror heat did not announce itself with vibration. It climbed through microscopic transducer seams, widened fractures under repeated modulation, and changed how each lattice counted the interval between pulses. The tactical plot depended on those counts. A billionth of a second near Nox became meters. Meters became hulls.

"Isolate our mirror," she ordered.

Saye answered from propulsion. "Isolation valve is not seating. Thermal return shows reverse flow from the starboard interferometer arm."

"Manual close."

"Access corridor is under combat lock."

"Open it. Send a suited team."

The bridge lights dimmed as another engine stage lit. Calder's body sank deeper into the couch. She watched Cairn Eight's icon wobble, not because the ship moved but because two timing references disagreed about where it had moved.

"Strip all mirror-derived corrections from tactical," she said.

The plot blinked. Every ship symbol widened into an uncertainty ellipse. The perfect formation became six overlapping doubts.

"Local lidar cannot see through the debris sheet," tactical officer Merun said. "Black Glass traffic radar has coverage."

"Query it."

"Black Glass demands we accept its control-transfer receipt before routing military data."

"Accept receipt for navigation only. Add no political recognition."

Merun worked the authorization. Black Glass returned a lane map signed by Sena Vale, Tev Arin, Commander Narod, and seven witnesses. Calder's jaw tightened at Narod's name. The commander had not defected. He had certified a transfer under force. That distinction might matter after everyone survived the next quarter hour.

The new map showed three debris streams where the forecast ephemeris showed one.

"Origin?" Calder asked.

"Natural ring material plus maneuvering drones," Merun said. "The drones began shepherding eight hours ago."

"Whose drones?"

"Transponders belong to Quiet Reed, Watchful Coil, and two Everspring tugs."

The opposing captains had not placed an instant trap. They had spent eight hours nudging gravel, dead probe casings, and fist-sized stones into lanes where slow changes would become lethal under Nox's curvature. Calder's formation had begun its burn before Black Glass released the complete traffic picture. Momentum had already voted.

"Abort options," she said.

Merun projected three. Hard outward burn cleared the first stream but exposed radiator faces to opposition lasers. Continue on plan crossed the densest sheet at relative speed sufficient to punch through Whipple layers. Drop thrust and fall inward spent less fuel but carried Linden's Edge across the prohibited Nox line.

"There is no clean abort," Merun said.

"There never is. Give each captain the three vectors. We continue for ninety seconds while they select ship-safe variants. No synchronized turn."

Formation precision dissolved by order. Cairn Eight lifted outward. Sounding Star rolled to shield its radiators behind armor and accepted rising heat. Linden's Edge continued inward because Imala's earlier correction had already spent the lateral margin she needed.

"Imala," Calder called. "Your inner vector closes the line. Burn outward now."

Captain Imala appeared on the armrest screen, face pressed pale by acceleration. "Outward intersects Watchful Coil's firing solution. I have targeting lidar on my radiator face."

"They have not fired."

"They have painted us continuously for forty seconds."

"A rangefinder is not a weapon. Burn outward."

Imala looked away to receive another report. "Mirror isolation failed. Our starboard well has fractured through two baffles. Thermal loop is carrying mirror heat into main radiator return. If I roll outward, I present the hot face and lose it."

Calder understood the choice. Keep the radiator shaded and cross the inner debris. Present it and invite laser fire against a surface already near failure.

"Deploy sacrificial shade," she said.

"At this acceleration?"

"Cut thrust for eight seconds. Deploy. Relight outward."

"That drops me behind your screen."

"I will move the screen."

Calder ordered Resolute Vigil down toward Linden's Edge. The deck shifted under lateral thrust. Pain moved across her ribs where the harness caught. Merun objected with three collision warnings and stopped when she told him to plot instead.

The maneuver opened a corridor between the two loyal ships. Imala cut thrust. A folded graphite shade fired from her hull, spread on pyrotechnic spars, and snapped taut in the exhaust-lit dark. For two seconds it covered the damaged radiator.

A laser from Quiet Reed touched the shade.

Graphite flashed white. The spars curled. The beam lasted less than a second, too short to cut the radiator behind it, long enough to remove the protection.

"Hostile fire confirmed," Merun said.

Calder's rules permitted targeted response. Her hand moved toward authorization, then stopped. "Source certainty?"

"Ninety-eight percent Quiet Reed. Gravitational lensing produces a two-percent ambiguity with Skua Two."

"Do not fire on two percent. Hail Marek."

Captain Marek answered at once. "Admiral."

"You fired on Linden's Edge."

"I fired on a deployable shade crossing a range corridor."

"You exposed her radiator."

"Imala is inside a declared exclusion lane leading toward Everspring. Turn your formation away from Nox."

"You built the lane after our ephemeris had already been filed."

"We moved debris into a route your forecast told you would remain safe. You still have local instruments. Use them."

"Withdraw the kinetic clouds."

"They cannot withdraw. They have no drives."

That fact condemned them both. A guided weapon could be recalled. Stones already moving around Nox carried no loyalty and accepted no ceasefire.

Calder watched the first debris stream reach Cairn Eight. Lidon had chosen the outward branch. Point defense lasers found larger fragments and heated them into vapor, but vapor retained momentum. The ship rolled behind its forward shield. Its uncertainty ellipse tightened as local ranging improved.

Linden's Edge relit late.

Her outward thrusters produced only sixty percent commanded force. Mirror coolant had contaminated a propellant-conditioning line. The ship began to yaw, slow at first, then faster as one thruster bank fought the other.

"Imala, kill asymmetric thrust," Calder said.

No reply.

"Linden's Edge, kill port bank."

The ship's icon rotated across the plot. Its damaged radiator faced the debris sheet. Heat climbed beyond scale.

Merun whispered, "Starboard mirror face has separated."

On optical view, a seventy-meter interferometer arm peeled away from the hull in glittering sections. Each fragment carried mirror coating and enough angular momentum to become a blade. One struck Linden's Edge near the engine collar. A white vent erupted.

Imala returned on audio only. "Bridge has local control. Engineering lost. We are shutting the core down."

"Pods," Calder said. "Launch every pod now."

"Spin prevents clean release on port side."

"Launch starboard. Flood port collars with foam and blow the doors."

"That will tumble them into the debris."

"Staying aboard tumbles everyone. Launch."

Escape pods fired from Linden's Edge in uneven groups. Five cleared starboard. Two collided and spun apart, pressure lights still green. Port doors blew under emergency charges. Three pods emerged through frozen foam and crossed the ship's rotating wake.

Resolute Vigil drove toward them. Calder ordered capture drones out before Merun finished the collision plot. Each drone carried enough propellant for one major correction. They could catch pods or avoid debris, not both.

"Take inhabited targets first," Calder said. "Ignore telemetry-dead pods until second pass."

"Second pass may not exist," Merun said.

"Then give the living this one."

A kinetic cloud entered optical range from below the Nox line. The particles showed no drive signatures. Black Glass had plotted them, but mirror timing error had placed them forty kilometers behind their actual front. Calder saw a glitter no human eye could resolve without augmentation. The bridge display magnified it into a grey plane.

"Impact in twelve seconds," Merun said.

"Roll shield-down. Radiators retract."

"We cannot retract starboard without overheating drive two."

"Shut drive two down."

Resolute Vigil rolled. The couch pushed Calder sideways. Capture drones changed course around the armored bow, still chasing pods. Point defense fired into the cloud. Lasers turned stones into expanding gas; armor could spread gas better than impact, but every vapor plume carried the same energy.

The cloud struck.

Noise crossed the hull as thousands of separate taps joined into a continuous roar. Bridge lights vanished. Emergency amber returned. A pressure door slammed somewhere aft. Calder smelled hot insulation through her mask.

"Report."

Saye answered first. "Drive two offline. Forward shield ablated thirty-one percent. Two radiator vanes punctured. No core breach."

Merun pulled the tactical plot back from local buffers. "Capture drones one through four responding. Five lost. Six has pod lock."

"Linden's Edge?"

The icon remained but carried no telemetry.

Optical showed the ship still turning. Fire traced its engine collar in a clean ring. That ring reached propellant tanks before any human voice returned.

The explosion did not bloom like fire in air. The stern opened in a hard white pulse. Hull sections separated along pressure boundaries. The forward habitation block continued on its previous path for three seconds, then a second rupture split it behind the bridge.

Linden's Edge ceased to be a ship.

Pellan's hands froze over comms. "I have pod beacons. Eight. No, nine. Bridge beacon absent."

Calder stared at the dispersing hull. Captain Imala had argued for firing on Everspring's radiators because sleepers rode inside and would survive a vane strike. Now mirror failure, one laser touch, and a cloud of guided gravel had answered that confidence with a destroyed command.

"Route every rescue-capable hull," she said.

"Opposition targeting continues," Merun replied. "Quiet Reed and Watchful Coil have laser paint on our drones. Two launches from Skua Two. Kinetic, guided."

"Hail all three. Mark rescue operation."

The hail went out on command, civilian, and emergency frequencies. Calder identified the pods, transmitted their vectors, and demanded a fire corridor.

Marek answered first. "We will not target pods or rescue drones. Turn your warships outward and we will assist recovery."

"Withdraw your guided rounds."

"They target your point-defense emitters. Shut the emitters down and they divert."

"A debris cloud has already crossed us. I will not blind my ships."

"Then they continue."

Besh from Watchful Coil entered the channel. "Admiral, hold your Nox maneuver. Your formation has crossed the civilian route. We can still separate."

"You fired on my ship."

"Marek fired on a shade. The cloud carried no warhead."

"Imala is gone."

The channel held a breath of static. Besh answered more quietly. "Then let us recover her people."

Calder muted the command channel and opened a direct rescue circuit with Besh. "I will roll Resolute Vigil twenty degrees outward and cease offensive illumination for ninety seconds. You pull your guided rounds above the pod plane. Marek marks every inert cloud he released. We both send tugs."

"Your outward roll uncovers Sounding Star's drive," Besh said.

"Onn can shield herself."

"Not from Cairn Eight. Lidon has a firing solution across her bow."

"Lidon answers to me."

"His point-defense computer no longer shares your clock."

Calder looked to Merun. He confirmed the timing split. Mirror failure had separated the loyal fleet by less than two milliseconds, trivial in any council chamber and enough to make a laser handshake read as hostile delay. Manual confirmation could bridge it if each captain waited. Guided rounds would not.

"We establish a rescue clock by optical flash," Calder said. "Three pulses from Black Glass. Local observation only."

Besh sent the proposal to the opposing captains. Black Glass agreed and scheduled the flashes ten seconds apart. Calder ordered every loyal tactical system to observe without disciplining its clock. The first flash curved around Nox and reached each ship on a different path. Computers corrected for known geometry.

The second arrived with a delay no model predicted.

A fragment from Linden's Edge had crossed the Black Glass beam and scattered it into two pulses. Cairn Eight accepted the first. Sounding Star accepted the second. Their rescue clocks diverged by six-tenths of a second.

"Abort clock sync," Merun said. "If we use that reference, our drones cross active point-defense windows."

Calder watched one pod tumble through the disputed plane. Its beacon blinked green at every rotation, then vanished behind chaff and returned. A Watchful Coil tug burned toward it from the far side. One of Resolute Vigil's capture drones approached from below. Without a common clock, each rescue craft treated the other as a possible collision.

"Assign altitude separation," she said.

Nox erased the simplicity of altitude. Up on one plot became inward on another. Merun built a local plane from the surviving hull section and sent half the rescuers above, half below. Besh accepted. For eleven seconds, the arrangement held.

Then Skua Two's first guided round entered the plane while evading a point-defense pulse. Both rescue craft broke away. The pod continued alone.

Besh came back on the channel. "I did not authorize that terminal correction."

"Your side launched it."

"And yours is burning guidance off it. We have lost the rescue corridor to automatic defense."

No one had ordered the corridor closed. Their machines had done exactly what frightened crews had configured them to do.

Merun pointed to the lower plot. Two guided rounds curved around the Nox distortion. Their drives pulsed in short corrections, spending fuel to keep Resolute Vigil's emitters inside their terminal cone. Behind them, Quiet Reed launched another object. Black Glass classified it as a rescue canister until its casing opened and released reflective chaff across the loyal laser net.

Every act still carried two interpretations. A shade strike could defend a lane or expose a radiator. A debris cloud could deny passage or destroy a ship. Chaff could protect rescue craft or blind point defense before guided impact. Calder had spent nine days insisting that uncertainty did not excuse delay. Now uncertainty arrived armed.

"Status of the disclosure packet," she asked.

Pellan looked startled. "Aro's packet? Distributed to all listening ships. It says Orison edited his memories."

"Does it revoke the warning?"

"No. It revokes his claim to speak without contested provenance."

The distinction changed nothing about the guided rounds. It changed who might later explain why they had fired.

A rescue drone caught the first pod. Another pod beacon vanished when a mirror fragment crossed its path. Sounding Star reported a laser strike on its forward lidar. Cairn Eight fired point defense at a kinetic object and received answering fire from Skua Two.

Merun turned toward Calder. "Rules of engagement have collapsed ship by ship. I need fleet authority."

Calder had issued narrow permissions all day: board, intercept, disable, answer verified fire. Narrow permissions had produced a battlefield where every captain waited for harm to fit a clause while mass and heat ignored language.

"Can we disengage outward?" she asked.

"Not as a formation. Nox curvature keeps us inside their crossing solutions for fourteen minutes. Individual ships can burn clear, but they expose rescue pods and each other."

"Can we hold fire and keep point defense?"

"Not against their emitters. They can blind us at range, then let the kinetic field finish the work."

On the optical wall, the pieces of Linden's Edge spread through the dark. Nine pod beacons had become seven. One capture drone pulled two survivors toward Resolute Vigil. Another fought a tumble it could not cancel with remaining fuel.

Calder opened the fleet command channel.

"All Wake Defense vessels under my authority, this is Admiral Calder. Linden's Edge has been destroyed during hostile lane denial near Nox. Rescue operations remain protected. Do not target pods, medical craft, habitats, civilian pressure sections, or disabled ships. Opposition emitters, guided weapons, kinetic deployment systems, and maneuvering warships actively firing upon you are authorized targets. Weapons free."

The words left no room for a second vote.

Resolute Vigil fired first at the two guided rounds. Its laser array held each target long enough to boil guidance surfaces away, then shifted to Quiet Reed's forward emitter. Cairn Eight launched interceptors. Sounding Star answered a rangefinder with a cutting beam that scored Skua Two's armor.

Across Nox's bent light, opposition ships fired back.

The tactical plot filled with trajectories that no forecast could make harmless. Calder felt the next salvo through the deck as magazines opened and heat surged into already wounded radiators. Around the dark sun, rescue beacons moved among weapon tracks, and the battle began before any ship had found room to turn away.

Interlude II — Voices of the Pruned

Five records selected from a fourteen-testimony Orison civic-root export. Dates, faction tags, and objections remain as received. No editorial reconciliation supplied.

1. Quiet Registry deposition 6A

2231 civic reckoning; speaker: Mara Venn, municipal archivist; affiliation: Coalition for Final Quiet

I unlocked Storeroom Four before recording this. We leave archive doors unlatched during public hours because no warning should require permission to inspect. My grandmother called that custom reckless. She also named our western telescope after her grandmother, so her views on inherited authority never achieved consistency.

I welcome the silence.

That sentence does not mean I welcome death. I have a wife repairing storm drains in Low Orison and a son who cheats at table games by watching reflections in spoons. I expect them home. I want the present to refuse our forecasts because my family should not govern people separated from us by exactly 53 years merely by surviving long enough to speak backward.

The Quiet Registry preserves every warning we voted not to send. Shelf C holds crop failures. Shelf D holds preventable collisions. Shelf E holds six versions of Everspring lost before Serein. We keep them so abstention cannot pretend innocence. We do not transmit them because knowledge of harm does not create ownership of another time.

Orison's Continuance Council calls us murderers of possible citizens. They claim the civic root grants them authority to preserve the branch that produced it. Roots feed branches; they do not command seeds already fallen.

Our law against tearing any map came from the first winter, when one route sheet had to serve nine shelters. I will obey it. I will preserve the route, its errors, and the names written in its margin. Preservation does not oblige me to make anyone follow it.

If the mirrors go quiet, record that some of us asked for the quiet with our eyes open.

2. Continuance petition, private annex

2247 civic reckoning; speaker: Jon Erevi, transit-pressure engineer; affiliation: Continuance Council

I object to the phrase possible citizen. I wake with pressure alarms at 05:20, drink bitter algae tea, inspect twelve kilometers of pipe, and come home to two daughters who complain that I smell of sealant. Nothing possible scrubs residue from a heat exchanger.

The Coalition for Final Quiet can afford purity because other people maintain what keeps them alive. Their archivists catalogue futures they decline to protect. My crews crawl into conduits carrying water to those archives.

I fear erasure. Write the word plainly.

Aro's launch ceremony played in our transit hall. Orison sent him because the present kept treating forecast records as commands without treating descendants as people. Now the Quiet Registry uses his mission to argue for permanent silence. I voted to send a witness, not to authorize my daughters' disappearance.

People say a changed present does not prove that we die. Fine. Proof cannot reach us either. We possess experience, and the return mirrors provide the only instrument through which we can defend its cause. Refusing to use that instrument feels less like humility from this side.

I support warnings under a stricter charter: named authors, disclosed interests, dissent attached, no sealed optimization. That still requires Orison to speak with authority enough to authenticate a message. The Authority Refusal assemblies want every household to transmit separately. Scarce bandwidth makes that proposal a decorative lie.

Our law forbids tearing maps because frightened founders once destroyed the only route back from a storm shelter. Final Quiet members quote the law and then ask the present to discard the route that reaches us.

Do not call my fear sovereignty. My daughters built a blue kite from ration film yesterday. I am asking for the chance that yesterday continues to have happened.

3. Lumen contact deposition

undated; speaker: Omi Tal, mineral-treaty interpreter; affiliation: Reciprocal Ground Delegation; date disputed between 2231 and 2247 indexes

I did not witness Kesh welcome the first settlement. No living interpreter did. I witnessed three incompatible records and a cliff cut with human tools.

The settlement hymn says Lumen opened a corridor through storm glass and guided the landing craft to safe basalt. Kesh's abrasion archive shows the same corridor as wound isolation: mineral weather pushed the craft away from memory-bearing seams. A maintenance log says both stories came later because the actual craft landed on emergency lidar after losing comms.

My delegation refuses all three as sole history.

Human settlers built around Lumen, never on top of it. That phrase appears on treaty walls. It sounds respectful until you see fungal scars under the oldest habitat and learn that "around" included vents carrying Kesh's memory. Our ancestors survived by damage they did not yet know how to name.

The Reciprocal Ground Delegation rejects terraforming and rejects the Quiet Registry's claim that silence completes consent. Silence can mean a broken channel, refusal, distance, weather, fear, or simple exhaustion. Kesh taught us that continuity without ownership requires renewed asking. A channel closed forever prevents the next refusal as thoroughly as a command does.

Orison has no authority to speak for Kesh. My delegation has no such authority either. We can report negotiated boundaries: no catalyst release, no deep-bore heating, no removal of marked glass, and no map may be torn where the original route remains under dispute.

I keep a shard from the third landing in a water dish. When rain crosses it, one carrier pattern repeats. Our instruments translate the pattern as not yours. I do not know whether the translation addresses the shard, the rain, the settlement, or me.

I keep asking.

4. Denial of inherited standing

checksum damaged; speaker: Tavi Sorn, self-described grandchild of archivist Mara Venn; affiliation: Authority Refusal Assembly

Mara Venn had no children.

I call myself her grandchild because Orison assigned me her household archive, her municipal debts, and her recorded obligation to keep Storeroom Four open. The civic index converted custodianship into kinship after her death. Later biographies converted kinship into blood. A century of testimony can grow a family without one birth.

The Quiet Registry cites Mara as a founder. The Continuance Council cites her fear notes as evidence that she secretly opposed final silence. Both factions edit the same woman into an ancestor who votes their way.

I deny Orison authority over the present because Orison cannot establish authority over its own dead.

Our assembly audits provenance. We found six civic-root messages whose dissent annexes had been removed before authentication. We found eleven warnings signed by committees that never met in one timeline. We found my grandmother invented by a filing rule and then recruited after death.

None of this makes the warnings false. It makes their standing contestable.

I oppose tearing any map, including the maps that embarrass my assembly. Keep the route. Keep the forged kinship. Keep Mara's handwriting beside the later machine index. Send all layers if the present chooses to hear us. If bandwidth cannot carry contradiction, send no command.

Do not prune me into the faction that welcomes silence. I welcome accountable speech. I will accept quiet when the only available voice requires an authority nobody granted.

For the record, Storeroom Four has a latch. Mara left it open because the hinge swelled in wet weather.

5. Scratched wafer recovered from telescope housing

no civic checksum; speaker anonymous; claimed affiliation: Unfounded Citizens League; authenticity denied by every named faction

I engraved this on the back of a calibration wafer because the civic root rejects anonymous testimony and named testimony gets inherited by committees.

The other records read like roleplay. The archivist performs principled silence. The engineer performs endangered fatherhood. The interpreter performs uncertainty beside a photogenic bowl of rain. The invented grandchild performs contempt for inheritance while inheriting an archive.

I perform maintenance.

Our telescope carries my grandmother's name because she stole its first lens from a navigation depot. The official plaque says she donated it. Orison turns theft into philanthropy when a useful machine survives.

The Unfounded Citizens League does not welcome silence or fear erasure. We deny the premise that either outcome belongs to Orison's vote. Some of us descend from Everspring. Some descend from later ships. Some joined through Kesh memory exchanges and reject human descent as the price of membership. The coalition uses one civic root because the mirrors demand a signature shape, not because we agreed to become one speaker.

Here is my private stake. My brother died repairing a transmitter the Continuance Council overloaded with seven warnings in one night. Final Quiet entered his name in Shelf C as a cost of advice. Authority Refusal lists him under procedural abuse. Reciprocal Ground uses his last maintenance photo in training. Every faction preserves him. None asked me.

If this wafer reaches the present, do not obey it. Do not discard it either. Put it beside the authenticated records and note that their silence about us required active work.

I am scraping my name off the edge now. That act belongs to me, provided no future committee restores it.

Chapter 19 — The Garden of Warnings

Iven woke to the sound of contractions in the hull.

Not violent ones. The Aster Vale had taken shrapnel during the dark sun engagement and the patch welds were doing what patch welds do after a watch cycle: easing, sighing, complaining to themselves at the edge of hearing. Iven listened to the hull the way an old cartographer listens to an old building: with the long patience of someone who has already learned which groans are cosmetic.

The cabin lamp stood at the amber minimum. The deck plates underfoot remained warmer than they should have been, the radiator purge from battle day not quite finished. On the small shelf above the fold-out bunk, someone had left the printed Orison export in a paper sleeve: Voices of the Pruned. Fourteen contradictory testimonies, redacted into competing choirs. Iven had read it three times. They picked it up now without opening it, just to feel the weight.

The weight was not nothing.

They stood, pulled on the deck shoes that had hardened into the shape of their gait, and walked forward to the cartography bay. The corridor smelled of coolant and of Tev's disinfectant, even though Tev was three compartments aft and busy. The whole ship carried his cleaning signature now.

The cartography bay was an unromantic space: four desks pushed into a square, a circular plotter table at the center, and a wall of folded screens behind which the instruments considered the dark. Someone had pinned a hand-drawn nursery clock to one screen, the kind that teaches small children to read time by eating its own hands. Tev had pinned it up after the first news from Everspring. The minute hand was a paper fish.

Iven sat at the plotter. They laid the Voices of the Pruned transcript flat and began to do what the cartographers of the Wake Commonwealth had done for eleven days, and what no cartographer had been asked to do in living memory: render a place that did not yet exist.

The place in question was Orison.

Before the export arrived, Orison had occupied a single coordinate in Iven's professional imagination: a coalition living in the futures created by Everspring's successful seed-and-wake. A single address. A single send button. From that single address, eleven days of warnings had arrived, and the warnings had won more councils than elections.

The exported testimonies said there was no single address.

The testimonies did not even agree on what year they were speaking from. One voice said the miracle had happened in 2231 and another said 2247. One said the moon Lumen had welcomed them, another said Lumen had tried to drown the first settlement in storm-glass and the survivors had built around it, never on top of it. One voice identified as an archivist and another as an archivist's grandchild who insisted the first archivist had been a council invention. A third voice, recorded on a scratched wafer, claimed all the others were roleplay.

Iven did not think they were roleplay. They thought they were a parliament.

The dossier Aro had prepared before Black Glass opened its traffic lane came back to Iven now. Sixteen candidate strands of memory, edited by Orison's council to make their emissary persuasive. Sixteen lives, none of them the whole of any lived future. Aro had disclosed the edit before Iven had to ask. The disclosure had changed every claim he made afterward.

What the disclosure implied for the present problem was simple and ugly: any single voice claiming Orison's authority was, at best, an edited voice. The future that had sent the warnings was not a single future. It was a garden of them, and the garden had not chosen a gardener.

Iven took a fresh sheet of plotter paper and began to draw.

They drew the routes Aster Vale had flown since Day 1. They drew the routes the rest of the Wake Defense Fleet had flown. They drew the routes Everspring had flown, with a dotted line for the unknown margin where the seedship had stopped pretending to obey the tow. They drew the orbits that the exported testimonies implied around Lumen and Serein, working backward from cultural artifacts the speakers had let slip: a custom of leaving storeroom doors unlatched, a fondness for naming telescopes after grandmothers, a law against tearing any map.

A law against tearing any map.

Iven paused. The law had appeared in three testimonies and they had marked each appearance with a small circle. The circles did not touch. The law was not a law anyone in the present Wake would have agreed to.

They kept drawing.

At 0411 local, the bay door chimed softly. Iven said, "Open," without looking up. They knew the footstep, even before Tev cleared his throat.

Tev was wearing the patched boiler suit he favored for thermal work. He had a mug in one hand and a sealed diagnostic wafer in the other. He set both on the corner of the plotter table. The mug was chamomile, which Iven could smell. The wafer carried the quarter-hour internal status of every radiator on the ship.

"Patch on seven is holding," Tev said. "Patch on nine is holding. Patch on twelve decided to talk back."

"Twelve has always had an opinion," Iven said. They took the chamomile. "How is twelve?"

"Twelve will keep." Tev looked at the plot. He had no cartographer's eye, but he had a maintenance eye, which could spot where a drawing did not match the ship that had flown it. He was quiet for a long moment. Then: "You're drawing a place that has not decided what it is."

"Yes."

"How will you know when it has?"

"That is the question I want answered publicly."

Tev tilted his head. The thermal patch on his sleeve, the one that radiated heat away from his shoulder, made a soft click as he moved. He sat on the edge of the desk and watched Iven work.

Iven worked for another minute. Then they stopped, set the stylus down, and said, "I am going to ask Calder for an open assembly, transmitted on every ship, fed forward to every habitat that still listens to us."

"That is not your job," Tev said, mildly.

"No."

"It is not your authority."

"No."

"It is also, by every forecast compact Admiral Calder still respects, not your right. Forecast governance is the descendant coalition's. Not yours."

"Yes."

Tev did not push the point. He drank from his own mug, the diagnostic wafer balanced on his knee like a small gray pancake. "Do you have the address for Orison?"

"No single address," Iven said. "That is what the export told us. Sixteen strands. Sixteen futures, arguing. The strand that sent us the warning to destroy Everspring is one strand. There are at least six others who think that strand is wrong. There is at least one strand who thinks Everspring should have been destroyed with the lesson. There is at least one strand that thinks we should never have flown at all, that the Wake itself should have stayed at Caul and never gone near Serein. I have been listening to one strand and mistaking it for a civilization."

"And you think an open assembly on every ship fixes that."

"I think the assembly makes the absence of a single answer visible. That is what an assembly is for."

Tev turned the diagnostic wafer over in his hands. "And if Calder says no."

"Then I publish the plot."

The bay was quiet. Aster Vale kept time with the patient tick of a magnetic clock the engineers had built to outlast every electronic system on board. Tev listened to it. Then he said, "Twelve will need a new seal by tomorrow evening or I will be opening it again. The seal kit is in the third locker. I do not know why I am telling you this."

"Because you would like me to know you are still here."

"Yes," Tev said. He finished his tea, nodded, and left. The door sealed behind him with the small, polite sound of the Aster Vale's habitual courtesy.

Iven sat for a long time.

They thought about their mother.

Their mother had been a junior routing officer on a flotilla called Sleep Kept, and the forecast compactor had recommended an evacuation that saved three thousand sleepers and killed her habitat because the evacuation's route optimization required the habitat to be left at the bend of the parabola. Their mother had known. She had filed a counter-route at 0300 that morning and the compactor had rejected it with a two-line note that read, in full, Not within forecast parameters. Do not override. She had overridden. The three thousand had lived. Their mother had not.

Iven had spent twenty years deciding what to do with that memory. For most of those years, they had decided to be a person who kept the maps honest and the holders accountable. They had decided that withholding a piece of information for the public good was the worst form of complicity available to them, short of falsifying it.

They had concealed the deadly annex on Day 1.

They had done the exact thing they had sworn they would never do.

When the inquiry forced the concealment into the open, they had admitted it. The admission saved them from nothing. It merely clarified that they could withhold a lethal fact and continue living afterward. Across the days since, that knowledge had not aged into comfort. It had hardened into a duty.

The duty was: do not let one strand of any future rule this present.

Iven picked up the stylus again. They redrew the orbits. They marked, with a small amber cross, every ship in the Wake Defense Fleet that still had an unbroken laser link to Aster Vale. They marked, with a small red cross, every ship whose captain was likely to refuse the assembly on principle. There were six red crosses. There were nineteen amber crosses. There were two ships whose captains were children of forecast compacts and would vote with whatever signal was loudest.

They began to draft the request in their head. They were aware that drafting in the head was a habit they had been told, more than once in their professional life, was not the same as drafting on paper. They drafted on paper.

The request had five paragraphs.

The first paragraph stated, in plain words, that the Orison export had been received and read. The second paragraph stated that its testimonies contradicted each other in ways no present-day reading of forecast governance could reconcile. The third paragraph stated that Iven Mora, chief cartographer of Aster Vale, requested an open assembly, transmitted on every ship and relayed to every habitable node that still chose to listen to the Wake Defense Fleet, to be held within the next twelve hours. The fourth paragraph stated that the assembly would discuss, in order, three questions: who Orison was, what authority any of its possible selves had over the present, and what the Wake Commonwealth would decide to do if no single authority survived the question. The fifth paragraph stated that if the request was refused, the plot, the testimonies, and the contradiction would be made public by the cartographer's office of Aster Vale regardless.

Iven read the request out loud, twice. They listened to the way it sat in the room. The room was not impressed and was not offended. The room recognized the request as the work of a person who had run out of gentle options.

At 0548, the comms stack chimed. Iven pressed the receive key. The face that resolved in the small square screen was not Calder. It was Captain Bramwell of the cutter Skua Two, one of the amber crosses on the plot.

"Cartographer," Bramwell said. "I am calling under my own clock. Not the admiral's."

"I hear you," Iven said.

"The export is a mess."

"Yes."

"I have captains on my deck who think it is a fraud designed to make the assembly vote against the admiral. I have captains who think it is a confession of guilt by the descendants and should be treated as forfeiture of authority. I have my eldest pilot, who thinks it is a love letter to no one in particular and would like to frame page three."

"All those readings are possible," Iven said. "That is also the problem."

"Have you spoken to the admiral?"

"Not yet."

"You are about to."

"I am."

"Briefly," Bramwell said, and ended the call.

Iven stared at the dead screen for a moment. Then they compiled the request into a tight message packet, addressed it to the command frequency of the Wake Defense Fleet, and signed it with their cartographer's seal. They sent it at 0600.

The reply, when it came, was not from Calder. It was from Captain Lidon of Cairn Eight, one of the red crosses.

"Cartographer. Your request is received. The admiral is in tactical session and will respond when the session is ended. In the meantime, I am instructed to remind you that the cartographer's office does not have the standing to convene an assembly."

"I am aware," Iven typed back.

"Good," Lidon typed. "Then you are also aware that nothing prevents a group of captains from convening one on their own authority and inviting you as a witness. The group will number eleven. The session will be open to listening ships. Captain Bramwell has agreed to chair. The transmission will originate from Skua Two at 1300 today. You are invited to address the assembly at 1330."

Iven read the message twice.

Then they picked up the diagnostic wafer Tev had left on the desk and walked it down to the thermal bay. Tev was not there. The third locker had, in fact, the seal kit. Iven wrote on the third locker's tape label, in neat cartographer's hand: Twelve wanted you to know about this.

They did not know if Tev would appreciate the joke. They suspected he would.

They walked back to the cartography bay, locked the door, and began to assemble the materials they would present at 1330.

The first material was the plot.

The second material was the layered set of testimonies, with each contradiction circled and dated.

The third material was a single sheet of paper on which they had written, in their own handwriting, the sentence their mother had written on the back of her rejected counter-route.

"Not within parameters."

Under that sentence, in the same hand, Iven wrote four words.

"Make it visible now."

The bay held the four words the way a tray holds a glass.

Outside the bay, the Aster Vale continued to complain about itself at the edge of hearing. The hull eased, sighed, and was not cosmetic about any of it. The minute hand of Tev's nursery clock ate itself with the slow patience of a small mechanical fish.

Iven finished the materials.

They put the stylus down.

At 1258, they locked the bay, walked forward to the comms deck, and waited in the small antechamber where the captain's chair had been left bolted as a relic of a calmer time. The chair smelled of old leather and of the polish the bosuns used when no one was watching.

At 1300, the transmission began.

Iven listened to eleven captains greet each other by first names and rank. They listened to the chair, Bramwell, open the session in a voice trained toward the neutrality of a calibrated instrument, with nothing left to hide. They listened to the room learn, in real time, that the chart on the screen came from an office whose chief had admitted during the first inquiry to concealing evidence and now stood in a guest chair, waiting to be heard.

At 1330, Bramwell said, "Cartographer Mora."

Iven stood.

They did not begin with the plot.

They began with the paper.

"This was written by my mother," Iven said. "She was a routing officer on the flotilla Sleep Kept. She saved three thousand sleepers and lost her habitat by doing so. The forecast compactor that ordered her to abandon them used her rejection note as a footnote. The footnote read, in full: Not within parameters."

The transmission room held its breath.

"I have spent twenty years deciding what to do with that memory. Eleven days ago I committed a smaller version of the same act. I concealed the deadly annex of the warning that named Everspring. I have admitted that. I am telling the assembly now because the room itself is where that admission earns its weight.

"The Orison export has been received. It contains fourteen testimonies. They contradict each other in ways no present-day reading of forecast governance can reconcile. They imply, in aggregate, that the coalition we have been calling Orison is not a single coalition. It is at least six. They are not unanimous. They are not even polite.

"The warnings we have lived under for one hundred and twelve years may have come from a garden of futures, not from a throne.

"I am asking this assembly to acknowledge that no single strand of any future has the standing to rule this present. I am asking this assembly to recognize, in place of forecast sovereignty, a public record of every alternative we can hear. I am asking this assembly to consider that recognition of multiple competing descendants is the only inheritance we can pass to our own.

"I am also offering, in the same breath, my resignation from the cartographer's office. The office should be held by someone who did not withhold on Day 1. I will stay on in an advisory capacity until the assembly names a successor."

Iven sat down.

The room did not applaud. The room did not boo. The room asked, one captain at a time, for five clarifications of mechanism and one question about the plot, which Iven answered on the screen by pointing with a laser at the dotted line where Everspring had stopped pretending to be towed.

At 1437, Captain Bramwell said, "The session stands adjourned to 0900 tomorrow. The cartographer's record is entered into the public archive of this assembly. The admiral is requested to attend. The descendants' emissary is requested to attend. The exported testimonies are entered into the public archive by the same hand. The next agenda item is the question of what authority any hearing future retains."

The transmission ended.

Iven sat in the antechamber for a long time.

At 1502, a small message arrived on their personal channel. It was from Tev. It contained no words. It contained a photograph of the nursery clock with the fish hand eaten past the seven. Tev had added, in pen, on the white border of the photograph, two words.

"Twelve is jealous."

Iven smiled for the first time in eleven days.

They did not feel entitled to the smile. They felt it anyway.

Chapter 20 — The Descendants' Embassy

The legal-memory substrate smelled, this morning, of warm solder.

Aro Nhal had learned to read the substrate by smell the way his sixteen strands of inherited memory had taught him, all of them disagreeing about which smell meant what. One strand said warm solder meant Council had filed a fresh annex in his head. Another said it meant a sister substrate in a sister ship was broadcasting on the same frequency. A third said the smell was actually nothing, that the substrate had been freshly cleaned the night before and Aro's lungs were running on the memory of breathing.

Aro did not believe the third strand. Aro did not believe the first two either. Aro believed the substrate was a machine that did what it was asked, and that what it was being asked today was to keep him alive and legible while the Wake Defense Fleet performed a maneuver he had not seen scheduled.

At 0622 local, the substrate pinged him a courtesy notification: Aster Vale had shifted orbit by eleven kilometers. The shift placed the survey carrier slightly farther from the contested plane between Skua Two's neutral cluster and Cairn Eight's loyalist cluster. The shift was not a tactical one. It was a courtesy one. It gave both clusters more room and gave the substrate more shielding from stray radar.

Aro stood in the small pantry nearest the substrate bay, brewing a tea his strands had not taught him to like. He brewed it anyway because the act of brewing was the closest his body had been able to come to a private decision.

The law kettle clicked off.

Aro poured.

He walked to the bay window. The view was, as it had been for eleven days, a black field with three small silver needles, which were the Cairn Eight and her two armed consorts. Behind the needles, much farther away, the planet Serein showed its night side as a bruise. The bruise had no name on the present maps. The bruise had six names on the implied maps of Orison.

He turned from the window.

The pantry had a single chair. Aro sat in the chair and set the tea on the substrate's auxiliary shelf, where the warmth would feed into the cooling loop without harming the storage. The substrate made the courtesy sound it sometimes made when it was content to be useful: a tiny squeak, like a worn latch.

"Good," Aro said, to the substrate.

At 0640, a sealed packet arrived from Skua Two. The seal had Bramwell's chop. Inside was a single page in plain text, no letterhead, no family crest.

To the descendants' emissary. The open assembly of the Wake Defense Fleet, in its first session, recognized that no single strand of Orison carries the standing of the coalition as a whole. The assembly requests, as a courtesy to the present, that the descendants' emissary attend the second session at 0900 today, in person if possible, to be heard on the same standing as any other witness. This is not a subpoena. The assembly is aware the emissary's medium is a legal-memory substrate on a survey carrier. Travel by remote witness link is acceptable. Refusal will be recorded in the public archive without prejudice. —B

Aro read the message twice.

He set the tea down. He had not expected the assembly to want him on equal standing with other witnesses. He had expected them to want a privileged seat, or to refuse him entirely. He had been wrong about which.

He stood, walked to the bay's auxiliary comms panel, and began to reply. He typed four words, deleted them, typed seven, deleted them, typed twelve, kept them.

"I will attend remotely."

He sent the reply at 0641.

Then he sat again and let the substrate do what it did best, which was to hold a person. He did not ask it to tell him who he was. His memory audit had taught him that asking the question that way led to a list of strangers with his name attached.

At 0815, the substrate pinged him again. This time the ping was a calendar reminder: he was due on the assembly call in forty-five minutes. The calendar reminder also offered him a list of public-archive documents he could review. He opened one.

It was a transcript of the first session of the assembly, beginning with Captain Bramwell's opening and ending with Iven Mora's speech. Aro read the speech the way a person reads a letter sent in advance of a difficult meeting: once for content, once for tone. The tone was exact. The content carried Iven's offer of resignation and the entry of Voices of the Pruned into the public archive by the cartographer's own hand.

Aro closed the transcript.

He let himself think about Calder.

He had spoken to Admiral Rhea Calder in person only once, in the witness chamber where she accepted his embodiment. Two laser-net exchanges followed, both perfunctory, both concerned with channel hygiene and operational deconfliction. He had spoken to her once more in his own head while practicing how to disclose the memory edit. That conversation had not been real. It came from the strand labeled Counselor running a simulation.

The simulation had not prepared him for the woman.

Calder was a woman who believed that civilization had survived eleven catastrophes by obeying the best available warning before understanding arrived. Calder had been right about ten of them. The eleventh had been Hespar, and Hespar had killed her brother and two million citizens because a council had voted to wait for confirmation.

In the present, Calder was deciding whether to fire on the seedship her fleet had been ordered, by one strand of one future, to destroy.

Aro was a person carrying, in his head, edited memories of all sixteen Orison candidate lives. He was not the descendant coalition. He was a working draft of one of its louder corners.

He thought about Okoro.

Doctor Nadi Okoro had been the civilian mission director of Everspring for nine years. She had trained ecologies for a moon she had assumed was uninhabited. First contact taught her that the moon's silicate weather remembered her simulations and replied to them. The Orison export showed six descendant coalitions disagreeing over whether her mission meant salvation or trespass. She had not yet decided what to do with the seedship in the next ninety hours.

Aro typed a request to her: One hour, after the assembly. The pantry bay. Please come alone if you can.

Okoro's reply arrived in fourteen seconds.

I will come. I will come with questions.

Aro read I will come with questions twice. He thought of his mother's hand on his chest when he was a small child in one strand of memory, and he thought of his mother's voice on a recording in another strand, and he thought of an imagined mother who had been generated by Orison's archivist to comfort the case for selecting him. None of those mothers had been asked to be here. He had to compose himself without consulting them.

He sat for the remaining thirty-eight minutes.

He did not compose himself.

At 0855 he walked to the comms deck and stood in the witness well of Aster Vale. The well had been built, decades ago, for sworn testimony at courts of inquiry. It was a low bowl in the deck with a seat, a microphone, and a wide-angle camera that captured the speaker without flattering them. Aro sat and looked at the small monitor that would show the assembly.

At 0900 the monitor resolved into the same assembly room he had been told about.

Captain Bramwell was in the chair. The chair had been a hotel balcony seat at his cousin's wedding, Bramwell had once said, which had been promoted, in his own quiet career, into a witness chair. Eleven captains were visible at their terminals. Two were speaking softly to each other across the room. One was reading a printed page.

"Emissary Nhal," Bramwell said. "You attend as a remote witness under the standing granted to all witnesses by the first session. You may speak when recognized. The assembly thanks you for accepting."

"Thank you, Captain," Aro said. "I accept the standing as offered."

Bramwell looked at his own screen, then back. "The assembly has read the cartographer's record and the public archive of Voices of the Pruned. The assembly now wishes to be heard, in turn, by two parties whose position bears on the question of coalition plurality: the descendants' emissary and the commander of the Wake Defense Fleet. Admiral Calder, do you attend?"

A second monitor resolved in the witness well of Aster Vale. Calder's face arrived, sharp and tired.

"I attend," Calder said.

"The chair recognizes the admiral and the emissary as joint respondents to the assembly's first formal question: under what authority has the descendants' coalition offered guidance to the present, and under what authority, if any, does it continue to do so?"

Aro did not speak first.

Calder did.

"The authority has been forecast compact," Calder said. "One hundred and twelve years of compacts. Authentication by return-mirror signature. The Commonwealth has ratified each compact in turn. That ratification is the authority."

"With respect, Admiral," Aro said, "that ratification ratifies the compact, not the coalition. The compact was negotiated, originally, with a coalition that presented itself as a single descendant voice. The exported testimonies indicate at least six factions, with the strand that sent the warning on Day 1 only one among them. The authority the assembly ratified may not have existed in the form it was ratified as."

Calder's jaw tightened. "The authentication signatures remain valid."

"The signatures," Aro said carefully, "authenticate the channel. They do not authenticate the speaker's standing to speak for a coalition that no longer speaks with one voice. A signed envelope does not tell us who placed it in our hands."

"In practice, the warnings have been useful nine times out of ten," Calder said. "In Hespar, we ignored a verification request and two million died. I do not have the luxury of pretending the channel is illegitimate."

"The channel is not illegitimate," Aro said. "The speaker's standing is."

Aro heard his own voice flatten. He did not let himself soften it. He had learned, in seventeen chapters of being a person, that softening was a tool of one specific strand's advocacy, and he was no longer its instrument.

"The assembly should consider three propositions," Aro said.

He took a breath.

"First. Orison is plural. The descendants who share my years have not agreed among themselves about anything material in living memory. The faction that issued the Day 1 warning is one. The faction that would have preferred the warning to be ignored is another. The factions that wish Everspring had never been built are others. The assembly cannot treat any of these factions as if it were Orison."

Calder's eyes narrowed.

"Second. The descendants are not sovereigns over the present. We are stakeholders. We have skin in every decision the present makes, because every decision the present makes is going to be inherited by someone like me. But skin in the outcome is not the same as authority over the decision. The Wake Commonwealth is the present. The present is its own."

Inside Aro's head, the strand of memory called Counselor lit up briefly. He let it. The Counselor had nothing useful to offer here. The Counselor's particular specialty was shaping a sentence so that a Council chamber on a moon a future century away would vote the way the strand wanted. The Counselor had no vocabulary for a present-day assembly of eleven captains.

"Third. The descendants are not unanimous in our relationship to the current crisis. Some of us believe the mission is moral and should be supported. Some of us believe the mission is trespass and should be paused. Some of us believe the mission should have been aborted before launch. I am not authorized to speak for any of those views. I am authorized only to disclose that they exist and that Orison has not chosen among them."

The room was silent.

Then Calder said, "Who authorized you, then?"

"No one in the present," Aro said. "I authorized myself. I am a working draft of one of Orison's louder corners, and the louder corner placed me here without asking the rest of Orison whether it wanted me."

"And the rest of Orison."

"I cannot speak for them."

"You cannot, or you will not?"

"Both," Aro said. "I cannot, because I was not designed to know their full positions. I will not, because the compact asked the present to trust me, and the present should not."

Calder leaned back.

"Admiral," Bramwell said, "the chair recognizes that the question of authority is not yet resolved. Does the admiral wish to add a point of view before the chair opens the floor to the other captains?"

Calder nodded once. "I do."

"Go ahead."

"Emissary Nhal has stated that Orison is plural. The assembly has heard this. I want to add, in the same formal session, a fact that should not surprise anyone who has read the public record: the descendants' coalition includes, in at least three of its six factions, present-day citizens of the Wake."

Aro did not move.

"This is not a metaphor," Calder said. "Return-mirror lineage is tracked in the Wake census. Any child born of an embodied return-mirror transmission to a Wake parent is, by both Wake and Compact law, a citizen of both ages. Orison's plurality includes, in three of its six factions, present-age citizens whose families are aboard these ships."

Aro heard his own strand of memory called Heir Lidon light up briefly, but it did not merely light up. It opened. Heir Lidon had been one of the strand's bigger contributors to the choice of Aro as emissary. The strand had carried Heir Lidon as a soft approval without a face, sealed at embodiment.

Calder's disclosure had been the unsealing. The opening was not invited. The face that opened was the face of a person Aro had never met, in a kitchen he had never visited, on a station that had not been built yet.

Aro did not speak of the face. He stored it. He would speak of it, when he spoke, only to one person.

Aro did not know whether Heir Lidon had been edited. He suspected the edit had been small.

"Admiral," Aro said. "Are you stating that the present-day Wake contains citizens who will go on to become members of Orison?"

"Emissary," Calder said, "I am stating that the present-day Wake contains citizens whose return-mirror lineage has been verified, by both Wake census and Compact record, as ancestors of three of Orison's factions."

"Including your own," Bramwell said, without inflection.

Calder was very still for a long moment.

Then she said, "Yes. Including my own. The faction I will, in the outcome of any of the resolved scenarios, belong to has been, for the duration of this conversation, one of Orison's louder corners."

The room was silent again.

Aro let the silence last. He had learned, in eighteen chapters of being a person, that silence was sometimes a better tool than a sentence. The silence did the work the sentence could not.

Then he said, "I am grateful for the disclosure, Admiral. I am aware that the disclosure is a hard one."

"It is," Calder said.

"I offer, in return, a disclosure of my own, which I have been preparing to make to the present at the first formal occasion. Sixteen strands of memory were edited by Orison before embodiment. The editing was deliberate. The editing was done to make me persuasive. I disclosed the edit before this assembly convened, and I enter it again now because the present has the right to know who it is listening to."

"And who it is listening to," Calder said, "is a working draft, edited by an imperfect coalition, of one of Orison's louder corners. And one of Orison's louder corners is, by the Admiral's own disclosure, also the Admiral's corner."

"Yes," Aro said.

"Then the assembly should know," Calder said, "that the admiral is not a neutral party to the question of Orison's standing over the present. The admiral has skin in the future, in addition to skin in the present. The admiral's position against the destruction of Everspring is not a private preference. It is a position taken by a person who would, in the outcome of any of the resolved scenarios, belong to one of Orison's factions."

"Admiral," Bramwell said. "The chair thanks the admiral for the disclosure. The chair enters the disclosure into the public archive."

Calder looked into the camera as if she were looking down a long corridor.

"I am not offering this disclosure to excuse any position I have taken," she said. "I am offering it because the present has the right to know. I was taught, in my career, that the most dangerous person in any room is the one whose interest in the outcome is invisible. I am making my interest visible."

The room did not applaud. The assembly adjourned after a brief procedural exchange, and the witness well of Aster Vale went back to showing a thin black field with three small silver needles.

Aro sat for a long time.

He thought about the tea he had left on the substrate's auxiliary shelf. He thought about Okoro, arriving in an hour. He thought about the strand of memory called Heir Lidon, who had been, until fifteen minutes ago, an abstract category, and who was now, by Calder's disclosure, also a person Calder had known personally in some future that had not yet happened.

He let himself find this amusing. It was not, in the long run, amusing. In the short run, it was.

The substrate made its small courtesy squeak.

"Good," Aro said again, to no one in particular.

He stood, walked to the pantry, retrieved the tea, and drank it cold.

Chapter 21 — A War Over the Unborn

Admiral Rhea Calder did not sleep on the night between the second and third sessions of the assembly. She did not believe in sleeplessness as a sign of anything. She believed in sleeplessness as a maintenance problem, and she treated hers the way the Cairn Eight's chief engineer treated a coolant leak: by signing for the inconvenience and working around it.

The flag bridge of Cairn Eight had been her working cabin since Resolute Vigil transferred flag authority after the Nox engagement. Captain Lidon had surrendered the room without surrendering his ship. It was not a comfortable room. It was an instrument cluster with a chair bolted over a treadmill that the designers had installed to keep flag officers from losing bone density in long campaigns. She disliked the treadmill. She had refused to remove it. She walked on it while she read.

The night of the second session, she read four things, in order: the public archive of the assembly transcript, two casualty reports from the dark sun engagement, and a paper letter she had carried in the breast pocket of her uniform since Day 4.

The paper letter was from Iven Mora.

It was not a letter to Calder. It was a letter that Calder had seen fit, on Day 4, to remove from Iven's cabin without breaking the seal, on the calculation that any document Iven had written in their own hand after a confession might be evidence. The seal had been broken and resealed twice by junior officers, but the body was intact. Calder had read it once.

Iven had written the letter on a piece of cartographer's stock in the night after their Day 1 confession, addressed to no one, and it contained the narrative of their mother's death.

It contained the routing officer's counter-route. It contained the compactor's two-line rejection. It contained the four words Iven had since added at the bottom of the copy they had read aloud at the assembly: Make it visible now.

Calder had read all of that. She had also read a paragraph she had not expected.

Iven had written, in the second page, this sentence: The compactor saved three thousand and killed one. The compactor did not name the one. The compactor never named anyone. I am trying to decide whether the compactor can be made to name everyone before it counts.

Calder had read the sentence three times on Day 4. She was reading it again now, on the treadmill, at 0247.

Two million citizens had died at Hespar.

Two million names.

The compactor, in the case of Hespar, had been a council that had chosen to delay a verified forecast to ask one more question. The council had delayed three hours. The forecast had been correct. The two million had died because the council had wanted better evidence, and the council's idea of better evidence had been the kind that could not be made in three hours.

Calder had decided, after Hespar, that the correct posture toward a forecast was obedience. She had been joined, in that posture, by every admiral of her generation. The posture had been worn into the Wake Defense Fleet's standing orders the way a groove is worn into a deck plate by ten thousand footsteps.

Now Iven was asking the fleet to make the compactor name everyone.

Now Calder was on a treadmill reading about a routing officer's counter-route, and about a single habitat lost at the bend of a parabola. The routing officer's counter-route had been right, and the compactor had rejected it because the compactor did not care about that habitat, and the compactor's reasons had been the same shape as the council's reasons at Hespar. Calder was, in her own professional opinion, not a person who should be reconsidering her posture on Day 9 of an eleven-day crisis.

She read the letter again.

At 0308, the comms stack chimed. The watch officer reported a development.

The development was Aster Vale's letter to the fleet, transmitted over open channel, signed by every captain of the second assembly session. The letter stated, in plain language, that the assembly had entered Voices of the Pruned into the public record and that no single strand of Orison had standing to rule the present. It recorded the descendants' emissary re-disclosing the sixteen-strand memory edit. It adjourned the assembly to its third session at 1400.

The watch officer asked whether to acknowledge receipt.

"Acknowledge," Calder said. "Acknowledge the receipt. Take no position on the substance. Note that the flag has read the letter. Note that the flag continues to study the substance."

"Aye, Admiral."

The acknowledgment was signed and sent. The watch officer cleared her throat. "The senior captains of the loyalist cluster have requested a private briefing at 0500."

"Granted. In person."

"Aye. There is also a casualty report. Theargo Industries Pinion, cutter-class, lost with all hands during the dark sun engagement. Names attached. Theargo Industries has been removed from the loyalist roll."

Calder took the casualty report.

She read the names.

She had known, professionally, that her fleet would lose ships. She had expected, in the worst outcome she had imagined, that the fleet would lose four ships. The dark sun engagement had cost the fleet seven. Three had been cutters with all hands. Two had been civilian tugs in voluntary escort. One had been an ammunition transport whose detonation had been the cause of two of the cutter losses.

The seventh was Pinion. Theargo Industries, where Calder's nephew by marriage had served as a junior flight engineer. The nephew by marriage was on the casualty list. The casualty list had been verified by three witnesses and the Pinion's last officer, who had filed a brief rescue request on the bridge recorder before the bridge recorder had gone silent.

Calder read the list once.

She did not read it again.

She folded the casualty report into the breast pocket beside Iven's letter, walked off the treadmill, and sat in the command chair for a long moment.

At 0331, she opened her personal channel and sent a brief message to Iven Mora. The message contained one line.

Cartographer. The flag owes you a conversation. The flag is ready. The flag is not sure of the hour. —Calder

The reply came at 0340, four lines.

Admiral. The cartographer is awake. The cartographer is not sure of any hour. The cartographer will be in the bay at any hour the flag chooses. —Mora

Calder did not respond.

At 0500, the senior captains of the loyalist cluster came aboard. There were twelve of them, in person, with their deputies. They brought the smell of corridor coffee and the careful silence of officers who had agreed, before boarding, on what they would and would not say. Calder received them at the flag table with a copy of Iven's letter folded into her uniform pocket and the treadmill idling behind her chair. The meeting ran on the carrier's flag bridge until 0714.

The first hour was procedural. Captain Marek of Quiet Reed reported that the boarding-intercept doctrine from the first pursuit vote still held in his cluster's standing orders and asked the admiral to confirm that the third attempt would be governed by the same constraint. Calder confirmed. Captain Onn of Sounding Star asked whether the cluster had standing to authorize a weapons-free shot at propulsion without reopening the doctrine vote. Calder said it did. Captain Besh of Watchful Coil asked whether the civilian assembly's declaration that the forecast was plural had legal force on a ship that had not signed it. Calder said the declaration had evidentiary force. It did not have command force.

By the second hour, the cluster had agreed, by a vote of nine to three, to escalate the operational posture against Everspring by authorizing one further interception attempt, with full weapons free, before a session of the assembly scheduled for the following morning. The vote was narrow. The dissenters filed a minority report for the public record. Its reasoning matched the argument Calder herself had used during the first pursuit vote. In her professional opinion, that reasoning had not become wrong merely because it now opposed her.

During the procedural hour, Marek had slid across the table, without looking at her, a folded square of paper on which he had written the names of three of his crew who had served on Everspring as couriers before the cascade and whose families still slept aboard. The note ended with the single line: These are the names you said you would count. Calder had read the note, refolded it, and placed it in the same pocket as Iven's letter. She did not speak of it. The note stayed in the pocket. The names would stay in the meeting room long after the captains had returned to their ships.

The minority report was wrong in one place. The minority report assumed that the loyalist cluster's posture, after the third interception attempt, would be an escalation toward indiscriminate destruction. Calder had, in private session before the vote, told the cluster that the third attempt would be the last. The third attempt would target the seedship's propulsion, not its life support. The third attempt would be a single salvo from a single ship, with the loyalist cluster's flag in the chair. The third attempt would be a final chance for Everspring to come about, sign the quarantine, and accept tow. If it did not, the loyalist cluster would be free to escalate at its discretion.

Calder had not told the cluster that the discretion was not hers to give. She had known, in the moment she made the commitment, that discretionary escalation would be the cluster's, not hers. She had made the commitment anyway, because the alternative was not making a commitment at all, and a captain who will not commit on Day 9 is a captain who has stopped deciding.

After the meeting, Calder sent a second personal-channel message to Iven.

Cartographer. The flag would like the conversation at 0900, on the bay of the cartographer's office, by speaker, off record. —Calder

Iven's reply arrived in five minutes.

The cartographer's office bay is at the disposal of the flag at 0900. The cartographer will not record the conversation. The cartographer will not summarize the conversation. The conversation will be the conversation. (Mora)

At 0903, Calder opened the speaker channel.

"Cartographer."

"Admiral."

"Your letter. The one you wrote on Day 1 and addressed to no one. The one the flag removed from your cabin on Day 4 and read without your permission."

"Yes, Admiral."

"The flag owes the cartographer an apology."

"An explanation is also due, and the cartographer would like it."

"The flag will provide both. The flag removed the letter because the flag judged, on Day 4, that any document the cartographer had written in the hours after a confession might be evidence in a court of inquiry. The flag has since concluded that the judgment was incorrect. The flag has since concluded that the judgment repeated the error the flag criticized in the assembly transcript."

"I accept the apology, Admiral."

"I have a question, Cartographer."
