"The cartographer will answer what the cartographer can."

"You wrote, in the second page of the letter: I am trying to decide whether the compactor can be made to name everyone before it counts. The flag has been reading that sentence for five days. The flag has not been able to set it down. The flag would like the cartographer to explain the sentence in the cartographer's own words."

"The sentence means what it says, Admiral."

"The flag does not understand what it says."

"Then I will explain, Admiral. The compactor today is a forecasting compact ratified by councils that do not have to name the people they sacrifice. At Sleep Kept, the compactor did not name my mother. At Hespar, the compactor did not name your brother. At Day 1, the compactor did not name the eighteen thousand sleepers aboard Everspring. The compactor's advantage is that the compactor does not have to name names. The compactor's disadvantage is the same advantage. The sentence asks whether the compactor can be required to count each person before it acts. The sentence asks whether the names can be required as a precondition. The sentence asks whether a present that demands the names as a precondition is a present that will sometimes refuse to obey the forecast because the names are too heavy."

Nothing came back for a long moment.

"The flag understands the sentence now," Calder said. "The flag has one further question."

"The cartographer will answer if the cartographer can."

"The flag has lost a nephew by marriage today. He was a junior flight engineer on a cutter named Pinion. The cutter was lost with all hands. The flag is not asking the cartographer for sympathy. The flag is asking the cartographer whether the compactor's naming requirement is the right policy when the compactor has just named a person the flag loved."

There was a long pause on the other end.

"I cannot answer that question truthfully, Admiral. The honest answer is that I do not know whether the policy is right when the policy has just named someone I love. The honest answer is that the compactor I want is the compactor that names names. The honest answer is that I cannot promise the compactor I want would have saved your nephew, or my mother, or anyone. The honest answer is that the compactor I want is the compactor that has to count the cost before it acts. The compactor I have is a compactor that does not have to count. The compactor I want may still be wrong. I cannot promise it will not be."

"Then we agree," Calder said.

"We agree that the compactor I want may still be wrong."

"And we agree that we do not have the compactor today."

"Yes."

"Then the admiral has a question that is harder than the compactor question."

"The admiral may ask."

"The admiral has committed, in private session this morning, to one further interception attempt against Everspring, with weapons free, before the third session of the assembly tomorrow. The admiral has not told the cluster that the attempt will target propulsion rather than life support. The admiral has not promised the cluster that the attempt will be the last. The admiral knows the cluster will escalate if it has the discretion to do so. The admiral has made the commitment anyway, because the alternative is a captain who has stopped deciding."

"Yes, Admiral. The cartographer has been told by Captain Bramwell. The cartographer has been told the time and the parameters."

"The cartographer has an objection."

"The cartographer has more than an objection, Admiral. The cartographer has a request. The cartographer would like the admiral to consider, before the firing keys are armed, that the third interception attempt will be the moment the present stops pretending the descendants' plurality is academic. The cartographer would like the admiral to consider that eighteen thousand four hundred sleepers on a seedship with a child Iven Mora's mother would have recognized in any timeline will be present at the moment of firing. The cartographer would like the admiral to consider that the cluster's discretion to escalate after the third attempt is the exact discretion that produced Hespar."

"The flag has considered all of those things, Cartographer."

"The flag has made the commitment anyway."

"Yes."

"The cartographer has a request, Admiral."

"The flag is listening."

"The cartographer requests that the admiral consult, before the firing keys are armed, with one person who is not in the admiral's chain of command. The cartographer requests that the admiral consult Doctor Nadi Okoro, civilian mission director of Everspring, by speaker, off record. The cartographer requests that the admiral not consult Okoro to receive Okoro's permission. The cartographer requests that the admiral consult Okoro to hear Okoro's name for the people aboard the seedship the admiral is preparing to fire upon. The cartographer requests that the admiral make Okoro's name available to the loyalist cluster before the firing keys are armed. The cartographer requests that the admiral treat Okoro's name as the compactor's name."

Silence held the line for a long count.

"The flag agrees to consult Okoro," Calder said.

"The flag will consult before the firing keys are armed, or as soon as Okoro can be reached on an open speaker."

"The flag will make Okoro's name available to the cluster."

"The flag will treat Okoro's name as the compactor's name."

"The flag agrees to all four, Cartographer."

"The cartographer thanks the admiral."

Then Calder said, "One more thing. Cartographer."

"Listening."

"The compactor you want has to count my nephew's name. The compactor you want has to count my brother's name. The compactor you want has to count my mother's name, and my father's name, and my brother's friends, and the names of the two million citizens who died at Hespar because a council delayed a forecast. The compactor I want is one that would have saved all of them. The compactor I want is one that did not exist in time. The compactor I want is one that was not available to the council. The flag will not have a compactor that was not available to the council. The flag will have, as the compactor, the names Okoro can give the flag. The flag will have, as the policy, the count Okoro can give the flag. The flag will not have more. The flag will not pretend to have more. The flag will fire the keys or the flag will hold the keys, and the firing or the holding will be the compactor's verdict, and the verdict will be missing names it could never have collected."

"I understand, Admiral."

"The flag thanks the cartographer for the conversation."

"The cartographer thanks the flag for the conversation."

The speaker channel closed.

Calder sat at the command chair for a long time.

At 0931, she opened a new speaker channel and placed a call, on open airwaves, to Doctor Nadi Okoro, civilian mission director of Everspring, aboard the seedship.

Okoro answered on the second ring.

"Admiral."

"Doctor. The flag is calling about the names."

Outside the speaker, in the corridor of the flag bridge, a junior watch officer was quietly closing the door. The corridor smelled of recycled air and of the faint trace of the disinfectant Okoro used to keep her seedship's wardroom clean. The disinfectant was not, in fact, on Cairn Eight. But Calder had been smelling it, since Calder's last visit to Everspring before the cascade, every time the door closed.

She set the smell aside.

She had a list of names to obtain.

Chapter 22 — The Last Courier

Sena Vale's boots were still cold from the dry-ice floor of the courier bay when the second pinger went off on her wrist.

The first pinger, two minutes earlier, had been a heads-up from the watch officer of the tug Sworn: the tug had been moved from its berth alongside Aster Vale to a holding position under the survey carrier's ventral plate, where the magnetic shadow of the bulk would screen the tug from the loyalist cluster's mid-band radar. The second pinger carried a different note. It carried the encrypted chop of the open assembly that Captain Bramwell had chaired on Skua Two. It carried a request, addressed to Sena by name and rank. It carried a manifest.

Sena read the manifest on the courier bay's small fold-down screen, with the boot of her left foot on the bench and the dry-ice fog curling around her ankle. She read it twice.

The first line of the manifest named three pilots, in this order. Captain Bramwell of Skua Two, neutral cluster, courier-rated, laser-net qualified, last formal endorsement for courier work filed twelve days ago. Pilot-Captain Onn of Sounding Star, loyalist cluster under Captain Lidon, courier-rated on an emergency qualification, last endorsement contested by two of Lidon's own bridge officers. Pilot-Captain Ravel of Far Margin, independent, the captain who had peeled off the loyalist column on Day 9 and refused to return, courier-rated on the strength of an old Pell certification that had not been renewed since the school ring.

The second line named three ships. Skua Two, Sounding Star, Far Margin.

The third line named three cargoes: mirror keys, physical rather than transmitted. Each niobium-titanium slug could carry an authenticated return-mirror signature across a handoff. Iven Mora's office had machined them, three assembly captains had signed them, and individual evacuated sachets protected them inside a worn titanium dispatch case. A paper tag on the hasp read, in Bramwell's hand: Three keys, three pilots, one relay.

Iven Mora had written the appended order and Captain Bramwell had countersigned it. Sena would carry the case behind Lumen's leading debris arc, hand it to Bramwell, and stay in motion until each key reached one of the three courier-rated pilots on the assembly record. The order ended: The geometry changes when each key arrives. The engagement does not begin until the geometry has changed.

Sena understood the geometry. Lumen's storm saturated the laser nets between the neutral, loyalist, and independent clusters. Calder's loyalists already had a firing solution on Everspring's propulsion, and the salvo would land in eighty-one minutes without consent from the rest of the fleet.

Hand delivery of the authenticated mirror keys would invoke the rule Calder had placed on the record before the loyalist captains: anyone who fired without confirmed standing for the warning would bear the consequence alone. Once a key reached each sub-fleet, every local laser net would carry that authentication. The relay could not stop the salvo by force. It could put three endorsed pilots into the geometry Calder had promised to consult before arming the firing keys.

Sena pulled her boots on over the cold floor, sealed the courier bay's inner door behind her, and walked aft through the tug's narrow spine to the small locker where the dispatch case waited on its foam cradle. The case was heavier than it looked. The slugs were heavy in the way only machined alloy could be heavy: dense, patient, indifferent to the courier carrying them. Sena lifted the case by the worn handle, felt the paper tag turn on its wire, and walked the case back to the courier bay.

Tev Arin was waiting for her there. He sat on the deck plate with his back against the courier bay's small auxiliary cooler, in the patched boiler suit he wore for thermal work. He held an empty mug.

"You're supposed to be on the radiator watch," Sena said.

"Twelve is holding," Tev said. "Twelve will keep until you're back. Twelve wanted me to tell you that."

Sena did not smile. Since she told Tev the truth about Jori, he had kept it with the care he gave a critical component: checking at the agreed interval and never pretending the gauge had not moved.

"How many of the three pilots have you flown beside?" Tev asked.

Only Bramwell, once, on a training burn. Sena knew Onn by the bruised hull patch the pilot had brought back from Pell. Ravel's name had ridden a shipping label on the fabrication delivery that made the Pell school ring possible. Sena knew neither stranger's face.

"Two strangers and one acquaintance," Tev said. "For three keys."

"For three keys," Sena said.

"Their endorsements survive all three vetoes," Tev said. "Neutral, loyalist, independent. Nobody else cleared every list."

"Twelve is generous," Sena said.

Tev set the empty mug on the cooler and stood.

"The corridor behind Lumen's leading debris arc lasts two hundred and forty seconds," Tev said. "It is the only crossing the loyalist radar will miss. The column holds bearing two-seven-one and does not know the window exists."

"How long does the corridor last if the loyalist column adjusts bearing," Sena said.

"Nineteen seconds. They think Sworn remains under Aster Vale's ventral plate."

Sena lifted the dispatch case off the cradle.

"I will be back before twelve needs a new seal."

"Twelve does not need a new seal," Tev said. "Twelve wanted me to tell you that."

Sena walked out of the courier bay. Tev followed her aft to the bridge ladder, then peeled off into the thermal bay with the empty mug. She was already climbing.

On Sworn's bridge, Halen had plotted the corridor as a red sliver behind the leading debris arc. His mother had been one of the eight surveyors lost at Cliffside, her name preserved in the moon's record. He treated the plot with the attention of someone who knew what an unrecorded loss cost.

"Plot is hot," Halen said. "Corridor opens at zero-eight-fifteen and closes at zero-eight-nineteen. The loyalist column is steady on bearing two-seven-one and does not have us on scope. We have ninety seconds to translate."

Sena locked the dispatch case onto the courier's hard point, pulled the courier's restraint across her lap, and lit the courier burn. The harness had been rebuilt after Everspring rejected tow, when Sworn entered escort service and the old webbing failed a courier-load test. The harness settled across her chest the way a piece of equipment settles when a maintenance engineer has told it what it must do.

She did not think about Jori.

Jori had been a courier-rated pilot. Jori had flown the Pell school ring nine years ago with four hundred children and three broken vanes. Jori had died in the ring because Jori had taken the helm of the ring and given Sena the helm of the tug. Jori had not died in the tug. Jori had not been missing. Jori had been the moon's first record. The moon had kept Jori. The moon had not given Jori back.

Sena let Jori be the maintenance question she had answered ten minutes ago on the courier bay bench: how many of the courier-rated pilots on the assembly record have I flown beside. The answer had been two strangers and one acquaintance. The answer had been enough.

The burn ran for ninety-one seconds. The corridor ran for two hundred and forty. Sworn cleared the debris arc at zero-eight-nineteen minus three seconds, with the loyalist column's bow still two hundred and forty kilometers astern.

"The corridor closed behind us," Halen said. "The loyalist column did not adjust. We are behind Lumen's leading debris arc."

"Bring us to the relay position. Plot the burn for Bramwell."

The burn for Bramwell was a long, slow approach to a point behind the leading debris arc, in a region the moon's storm had cleared of the loyalist column's mid-band radar. The point was a piece of geometry Iven Mora had plotted on the cartographer's bay that morning, on a sheet of plotter paper whose margins still carried the smudge of a chamomile mug. Iven had sent the plot to the courier bay on the open assembly's laser net. Eleven captains had signed the plot in turn. The plot had been entered into the public archive of the assembly at 0614.

At zero-eight-thirty-one, Skua Two emerged from behind the debris arc. The cutter was small, gray, and tidy in the way a courier-rated cutter is tidy. Bramwell was on the bridge, in shirtsleeves. She did not wave. She matched velocity with the tug in nine seconds and brought the cutter alongside at ten meters. The airlocks of Skua Two and Sworn had been built by the same dockyard in the second year of the courier service, and the same worn patches marked the same places on each.

With the hulls locked at equal pressure, Sena carried the case through the three airlock chambers and set it on Skua Two's deck. She gave Bramwell room.

"The first key," Sena said. "The first key opens the relay."

Bramwell did not pick up the case. Bramwell looked at the case, then at Sena.

"You carried the case through the corridor."

"Yes."

"The corridor was a piece of geometry that had not existed ninety seconds before you flew it. The geometry was plotted by Iven Mora and signed by eleven captains and entered into the public archive at 0614."

"Yes."

"I have flown courier burns beside Iven Mora's office for twelve years. I have never seen Iven Mora plot a corridor that was not first requested by a captain whose ship was on fire."

Sena let that pass without a word. The courier bay of Skua Two was colder than the courier bay of Sworn, and she understood, by the cold, that Bramwell had been holding position behind the debris arc for longer than the manifest had implied.

"I am picking up the case," Bramwell said.

Bramwell opened the case, removed the first sachet, and broke the seal. She read the engraved chop of Iven Mora on the slug's fluted face. She did not read the handwritten tape label. Bramwell knew what the label said.

"The relay opens," Bramwell said.

"The relay opens," Sena said.

Bramwell closed the case and handed it back. Bramwell matched velocity with the tug and peeled off at twenty meters. The cutter slid back behind the debris arc.

Sena brought the case through the outer airlock, the equalizing chamber, and the inner airlock, and locked it back onto the hard point. The case had lost one sachet. The case had lost nothing else.

Halen plotted the next leg. It was to Onn, on Sounding Star, in the loyalist cluster, behind Lumen's trailing debris arc.

"Plot the burn," Sena said.

The burn was a long, slow approach to a position behind the trailing debris arc, in a region the moon's storm had cleared of the loyalist column's mid-band radar. Eleven captains had signed the position as the second piece of geometry on Iven's plot.

At zero-eight-fifty-three, Sounding Star emerged from behind the arc. The cutter was larger than Skua Two, and it carried a single broadside laser mount that had been fired, twice, during the dark sun engagement. The mount was cold and folded.

Onn was on the bridge, in the loose gray coat loyalist pilots wore on watch. Onn did not wave. Onn matched velocity with the tug in fourteen seconds and brought the cutter alongside at ten meters. The loyalist cutter's airlock was newer than Sworn's, with a heavier hinge and a brighter seal.

At the loyalist airlock, Sena moved the case through three chambers and placed it on Sounding Star's courier-bay deck. She waited by the hatch.

"The second key," Sena said. "The second key closes the relay."

Onn did not pick up the case. Onn looked at the case, then at Sena, then at the courier bay's small auxiliary cooler, where a single empty mug sat on the deck plate.

"The mug is empty," Onn said.

"The mug is empty," Sena said.

"The mug is on the cooler. The cooler is in the courier bay. The courier bay is on a loyalist cutter. The cooler is not standard. The cooler is a maintenance engineer's cooler. The maintenance engineer is named Tev Arin. Tev Arin is a friend of mine."

Sena looked at the cooler. She had not known Tev Arin had built a cooler for a loyalist cutter. She had not known Tev Arin had friends outside the neutral cluster. She had known Tev Arin for nine years and had not known either of those two facts.

"Tev Arin told me, by courier paper on the open assembly's laser net at 0601 this morning, that the courier tug Sworn had been moved to a holding position under Aster Vale's ventral plate. Tev Arin told me, on the same courier paper, that the courier tug's courier bay had been provisioned for a long burn. Tev Arin told me, on the same courier paper, that the courier bay's auxiliary cooler was empty. Tev Arin told me, on the same courier paper, that the cooler would be empty when I boarded the courier tug."

"Tev Arin is a maintenance engineer," Sena said. "Tev Arin does not have an opinion about anything."

"Tev Arin is a piece of equipment that has been told what it is for," Onn said.

Onn opened the case, broke the second seal, and read the chop on the slug's fluted face.

"The relay is open and closing," Onn said.

"Carry it onward," Sena said.

Onn matched velocity with the tug and peeled off. The cutter slid back behind the trailing debris arc.

Sena brought the case back through the three chambers and locked it down. The case had lost a second sachet. The third sachet sat in its cut foam well. The third sachet carried the spare.

Her hands were still cold.

Halen plotted the third leg to Ravel aboard Far Margin, behind the leading debris arc.

"Take us to the final handoff," Sena said.

For the last approach, Sworn crossed a region the moon's storm had cleared from every fleet radar. Eleven captains had signed that position as the third piece of Iven's geometry.

At zero-nine-forty-two, Far Margin emerged from behind the arc. The cutter ranked smallest among the three courier-rated cutters in the relay, and it carried no broadside mount and no laser battery. The cutter carried cargo. The cutter had been built, the manifest said, in the second year of the courier service, and reclassified as a freighter in the eighth year, and reclassified back as a courier on the strength of an old Pell certification that had not been renewed since the school ring.

Ravel was on the bridge, in the loose black coat independent pilots wore on watch. Ravel did not wave. Ravel matched velocity with the tug in eleven seconds and brought the cutter alongside at ten meters. The airlock of Far Margin was the same model as Sworn's, with the same worn patches in the same places.

Inside Far Margin, Sena cleared the patched airlock and set the case between herself and Ravel.

"The third key," Sena said. "The third key carries the spare."

Ravel studied the case, then Sena. "My name rode the fabrication delivery that made the Pell school ring possible."

"I remember the shipping label," Sena said.

"I read the corrected archive," Ravel said. "Jori flew the ring home. You flew the tug. The council switched your seats because it wanted one uncomplicated hero."

"I was there."

"I have waited nine years to meet the pilot who stopped letting the council tell it backward."

"That pilot has cold hands and one key left to deliver."

Ravel broke the third seal and read Iven's chop on the fluted face. "The third key keeps the relay available for whatever comes after this firing window."

"Then carry it," Sena said.

Ravel slid the slug back into its opened sachet and clipped the sachet to the inner ring of the flight harness. The metal struck the buckle with a clear note. A bridge camera recorded Ravel's hand closing over it, the independent transponder in the frame, and Sena standing beside the emptied foam well. Halen acknowledged the image from Sworn. Bramwell's key had already appeared on the neutral net. Onn's key had appeared on the loyalist net. Ravel touched the courier chop to the local reader, and the independent net added its authentication to the other two. Three separate receipts lit on Sena's wrist. Only then did she lift the case.

Ravel matched velocity with the tug and peeled off. The cutter slid back behind the leading debris arc.

Sena returned through Far Margin's airlock with an empty case and locked it to Sworn's hard point. Every sachet had reached a pilot. Halen brought the tug about.

The loyalist column still held its firing solution on Everspring's propulsion, with seventeen minutes remaining. It could no longer fire under the accepted rule without consulting Bramwell, Onn, and Ravel. Three conversations could not fit inside the window. The geometry had changed; the firing window moved with it.

Sena set the empty case on its foam cradle and walked aft to the thermal bay. Tev sat against radiator twelve in his patched boiler suit, the empty mug beside him.

"Twelve is holding," Sena said.

"Twelve will keep," Tev said.

"The keys are delivered."

"The geometry has changed."

Sena sat beside him and set her cold hands on her knees. Jori remained the pilot who had carried four hundred children home and died under a story told backward. The moon had corrected that story. This time Sena had carried the work far enough to place it in three rival hands.

The thermal bay was quiet. Radiator twelve clicked softly at the edge of hearing. The empty mug made its small courtesy sound.

Sena did not hear the sound. She was already thinking about the next relay.

Chapter 23 — Everspring at Periapsis

The first cradle opened while Everspring crossed Serein’s night.

Doctor Nadi Okoro caught the sleeper before the restraint web let him strike the deck. His skin carried cryogenic frost in the hollows beside his mouth, and his eyes moved beneath translucent lids. The cradle alarm flashed amber over his name: Lio Venn, orchard mechanic, Deck Nine, renal shunt fitted six years before launch. Okoro knew the line because Tev Arin had written it by hand on the service strip.

“Cradle Nine-Seven has opened without clearance,” the ward system said.

“I can see that.” Okoro braced one knee against the rack as braking thrust pressed her toward the bulkhead. “Close the outer sleeve. Warm saline at the shunt rate, not the general schedule.”

The system refused. Its local reservoir had fallen below safe pressure.

A kinetic cloud crossed the seedship’s wake three hundred kilometers astern. The strike missed the drive cone, but fragments hit the deployed braking veil. The hull transmitted each impact as a hard metallic cough. Somewhere forward, an automatic breaker opened. Half the cradle indicators in the ward went black, came back blue, then settled into amber.

Okoro keyed the medical channel. “Ward Nine has an uncontrolled opening. Report other cradles.”

Answers arrived from Decks Two, Five, and Eleven. Then a fourth voice cut through, thin with compression from the seedship’s damaged spine.

“Director, guidance wants you. Periapsis in eleven minutes. We lost another veil quadrant.”

“Guidance can have my voice.” She slid Lio’s shoulder under the restraint web and tightened it by hand. “My body stays here until the saline reaches Nine-Seven.”

A junior medic dragged a portable reservoir along the deck. Its wheels fought the braking load. Okoro took the line, purged its bubbles, and coupled it to Lio’s shunt. Warm fluid entered him at a rate slow enough to keep his heart from learning the wrong season.

The ward display updated. Twenty-three cradles had opened their inner sleeves. Nine sleepers had begun metabolic climb. None had clearance from the central thaw board.

“We have entered emergency thaw,” the system announced.

Okoro had written that phrase into the mission plan eighteen years earlier. It had meant a meteor strike after settlement, a fungal loss in the primary culture vault, or a guidance failure with a stable orbit already secured. It had not meant battle at periapsis, with Serein still ungranted beneath them and the Wake fleet exchanging laser fire beyond the moon’s debris ring.

“Confirm manual authority,” she said.

“Civilian mission director confirmed.”

“Cap the first group at sixty. Favor medical crew, thermal engineers, drive repair, and anyone whose cradle has already opened. Do not wake command staff by rank.”

The system hesitated over the last instruction. Its governance package preferred succession order.

“Rank will not repair a coolant manifold,” Okoro said. “Execute.”

The blue indicators began to change.

She left Lio under the medic’s hands and pulled herself through the ward hatch. Braking thrust eased without warning. Her shoulder hit the opposite frame. The ship had reduced drive to protect the remaining veil. She climbed the central ladder toward guidance while her weight dwindled from half a gravity to a trembling fraction.

Outside the nearest port, Serein filled half the glass. The planet showed no sunrise. Nox gave it no day. Microwave storms painted copper bands across its upper atmosphere, visible only because the port’s coating translated their frequencies into color. Lumen crossed above the limb as a chip of dark stone veiled by silver static.

The seedship had reached the place its dead designers had called available.

Guidance occupied a shallow blister behind the forward water wall. Three stations still carried operators. The fourth had lost its screen to a fragment that penetrated two decks before lodging in the chart table. Captain Daro stood at the center rail with blood dried over one eyebrow. He pointed to a trajectory rendered in red.

“We can make capture,” he said. “Not the survey orbit. Something lower.”

“How low?”

“Periapsis at one hundred twelve kilometers. Apoapsis wanders beyond Lumen’s ring unless we finish the correction. We need six minutes at thirty-eight percent drive.”

“The ward just entered uncontrolled thaw.”

“I know. Drive thermal load puts us over the cradle loop’s shared sink.”

Okoro looked at the heat ledger. The braking veil had saved reaction mass and converted the saving into heat. Two radiator petals had folded after fragment strikes. A third showed a coolant leak. The numbers left no clean path. Full correction could secure orbit and cook the sleepers’ reserve loop. Early cutoff could save the reserve and send Everspring through the system on an escape curve.

“How many cradles can the cold banks hold through six minutes?” she asked.

“Seventeen thousand nine hundred, if every estimate favors us.”

“And if one estimate does not?”

Daro enlarged the uncertainty field. Its edge crossed the count the mission carried in every oath and manifest: 18,400.

Okoro touched the field with two fingers. “Four minutes. Then coast while we reassess.”

“That leaves us high and eccentric.”

“It leaves room to become wrong without killing five hundred people.”

Daro relayed the burn. Acceleration built beneath them. The planet’s copper bands slid down the port as Everspring turned its wounded stern toward the direction of travel.

A fleet laser crossed the darkness behind them. It did not touch the seedship. The beam struck a drone cloud and spread it into incandescent grains. For three seconds the grains formed a false constellation above Serein. Then orbital motion pulled them apart.

Okoro opened the fleet channel. Static answered first. The local laser net had broken into islands around the debris ring. She caught voices from Aster Vale, a cutter she did not know, and Admiral Calder’s flag ship. No voice held the whole engagement.

“Everspring to all ships,” Okoro said. “We have begun emergency medical operations. Any strike on propulsion risks loss of cradle cooling. Eighteen thousand four hundred civilians remain aboard.”

She corrected herself because the public count deserved its exact written form.

“18,400 civilians. This ship requests a fire exclusion around its thermal surfaces.”

Calder’s answer arrived through two relays and a burst of carrier noise. “Doctor, the flag hears you. Hold your course if you can.”

If you can. Calder had once spoken as if capability followed command. The conditional cost her something Okoro could not measure over a damaged channel.

“Four-minute correction ends in one hundred seconds,” Daro said.

The ward called again. Forty-one sleepers had entered metabolic climb. Two carried cardiac alerts. Lio Venn’s rhythm had stabilized.

Okoro watched the orbit trace curl toward capture without closing. A complete burn would close it. A shorter burn left a narrow open mouth at the far edge of the display.

“Cut on schedule,” she said.

Drive thrust ended. Loose blood lifted from Daro’s brow in small red beads. The seedship passed periapsis with an orbit that had not yet decided whether to keep it.

Okoro reached the thaw hub nine minutes later. The deck smelled of warm plastic, antiseptic, and the first breaths of people who had expected to wake under an artificial sun.

Sixty cradles stood in active cycle. Some remained closed behind fogged lids. Others had opened enough for medics to reach wrists and necks. Lio Venn lay on a floor pallet with a thermal blanket around him. His eyes had opened, though focus had not reached them.

“What happened?” he asked.

Okoro crouched beside him. The honest answer exceeded what his recovering circulation could bear.

“We arrived damaged,” she said. “You woke early. Your kidneys need help, and the orchard does not need you yet.”

“Where are we?”

“At Serein.”

His mouth moved around the name as if he had learned it in another life. “Can I see it?”

“After the medic clears you.”

A service panel chimed behind her. The adaptive ecology controller had interpreted orbital capture as mission phase transition. Its display unrolled the colonization sequence: atmospheric assay, microbial exclusion, ocean seeding, albedo adjustment, autonomous fabrication descent. Beside each step appeared the familiar green mark from eighteen years of simulation.

The controller had not received Kesh’s refusal.

Okoro rose too quickly and caught the cradle rail. “Suspend all descent packages.”

“Suspension requires environmental variance outside mission limits.”

“Resident personhood qualifies.”

The controller held no field for resident personhood. It offered mineral hazard, indigenous carbon life, archaeological restriction, and mission-command delay. None described an intelligence distributed through storms and etched memory layers. None described Kesh.

Okoro selected mission-command delay. The option granted six hours.

“Director,” Daro said over her wrist. “Ecology has armed the first terraforming release. The capture trigger came through before your hold.”

“Physical status?”

“Two descent casks have spun up. Latches still closed. Guidance can inhibit release, but the controller will retry after the hold expires.”

“Send someone to pull the initiators.”

“Most qualified crew are asleep or in drive repair.”

Okoro looked at Lio. The orchard mechanic watched her from the pallet, conscious enough to understand the direction of her attention.

“No,” she said before he could volunteer. “You have warm saline in your arm.”

“I know release hardware.”

“You know pear grafts.”

“I serviced cask latches during loading. Everyone with a maintenance name did cross-training.” His voice roughened after twelve words, but he continued. “Tev wrote my shunt on the strip because the general schedule would kill me. He also wrote that I knew latch buses.”

Handwritten names covered the service strips on every cradle aisle. Tev’s script leaned forward as if the letters had somewhere to go. Each note had survived the cascade because paper did not need a network.

Okoro asked the medic for Lio’s limits. The medic allowed seated work, no ladder, no suit, no exertion. Lio accepted each restriction with the impatience of a mechanic who had awakened into someone else’s emergency.

They rolled him to the local ecology console. He read the latch diagram through thaw-blurred eyes.

“Software inhibit sits upstream of the spin controller,” he said. “Pulling initiators makes the casks safe, but the bus will keep trying. You want the bus unable to ask.”

“Yes.”

“Then isolate the colonization sequencer from guidance.”

“That also disables climate shelters after landing.”

“If we land.”

The qualification entered the ward without ceremony. Nearby, a woman in a half-open cradle began to cry. She had understood enough.

Okoro brought up the architecture. The terraforming lattice linked atmospheric drones, ocean cultures, fabrication foundries, and the settlement climate canopy. Its designers had braided survival and transformation together. They had assumed no mission director would ever need one without the other.

To disable release permanently, she would have to burn the sequencer’s authorization bridge. The action would strand much of the adaptive ecology aboard the ship. Rebuilding it could take years with a healthy fabrication base. Everspring did not have a healthy fabrication base.

“What happens to the orchard?” Lio asked.

“It stays in vault until we negotiate a use.”

“That could kill graft lines.”

“Yes.”

“Animals?”

“Some embryos will exceed storage life before agreement.”

Lio stared at the display. His family, if he had one aboard, still slept among the 18,400. The mission had promised them soil, weather, and enough genetic breadth to begin again. Okoro’s hand rested over the command that could turn that promise into salvage.

He read the contact summary on the adjacent screen. Its translators had joined two lessons from the approach record into a working limit: CONTINUITY WITHOUT OWNERSHIP. ALTERATION WITHOUT REQUEST ENDS MEMORY.

“Did anyone ask them before launch?” Lio said.

“No.”

“Then burn it.”

Okoro entered her civilian authority. The controller requested a second key from mission command. Daro supplied it from guidance. It requested acknowledgment that settlement viability could fall below thirty percent. She accepted. It requested acknowledgment that some sleepers might never receive a habitable surface.

Her finger stopped above the final field.

For eighteen years she had protected the mission by keeping choices away from sleeping people. She had approved resource triage, embryo priority, diet plans, discipline rules, and the order in which professions would wake. Every decision had carried the argument that the sleepers could not answer. The argument had become so useful that she had stopped hearing its shape.

Lio shifted under his blanket. “Director?”

Okoro pressed confirm.

Deep in the hull, explosive microbolts severed the authorization bridge. The ecology display went dark in sections. Atmospheric drones changed from green to gray. Ocean cultures returned to vault status. The settlement canopy marked itself unavailable.

The two spinning casks slowed.

A guidance alarm sounded across the ward. The ship’s survival model recalculated with its oldest certainty removed. Mission success fell from forty-six percent to twelve.

Okoro watched the number settle. “Strike terraforming authority from automatic command,” she said. “Any future release requires informed consent from the people affected, including Kesh.”

The controller could not record that rule in its broken sequencer. Lio found a maintenance field and typed it there.

By the time the first thaw group could stand, Everspring had completed half an orbit. Its apoapsis crossed Lumen’s outer debris band, forcing Daro to spend reaction mass on two small corrections. Radiator Three still leaked. The cold reserve could support the emergency thaw group, but no larger waking until the leak closed.

Okoro moved to the communications blister with a slate of proposed language. Three people had edited it: Daro, Lio from his pallet, and a linguist named Meera Sol who had awakened vomiting and insisted on working between doses of antiemetic.

The request began badly. Everspring requests settlement access. Kesh had never offered settlement. Meera crossed out the sentence.

Their second attempt asked for harbor. Serein had no harbor in any human sense. Their third asked for sanctuary. That word implied authority Kesh might not claim over the other patterns in the moon-ring.

“Use refuge,” Lio said through the ward channel. “A refuge can be temporary. It does not promise us the ground.”

Okoro wrote the word.

The request named their condition. Damaged propulsion. Compromised thermal surfaces. 18,400 sleepers, sixty in emergency thaw, no surface release authorized. It disclosed that the seedship carried organisms designed to alter atmosphere and water. It disclosed that Okoro had disabled their automatic use. It did not claim virtue for stopping an act that should never have proceeded without permission.

Meera shaped the final clauses around Kesh’s nine-minute grammar. Human language placed the speaker before the listener. Kesh’s translated syntax began with the continuity at risk.

“What can we offer?” Daro asked.

“Records,” Meera said. “Repair labor if they can define a use. A closed orbit until terms exist.”

“Memory,” Okoro said. “Not ownership of it. Access chosen piece by chosen piece.”

Lio’s voice came through with a rasp. “And departure, if they refuse.”

Daro looked at the reaction-mass ledger. Departure might mean a slow death outside the system. He did not soften the number.

Okoro added the clause.

She sent the draft to the thaw ward for an open objection period. Thirty-seven conscious sleepers could read it. Nine lacked capacity. Fourteen approved without amendment. Six asked whether refusal meant death. Okoro answered yes, though not certainly. Three objected to disabling the ecology package. One demanded immediate descent. Another wrote only the names of two children still asleep on Deck Four.

Okoro entered every response in the transmission appendix. A request made under mortal pressure could not pretend to carry unanimous consent.

Fleet fire brightened beyond the blister. A cutter crossed between Everspring and the loyalist firing line, its radiators folded and its drive pulsing short corrections. Another ship followed at a distance too wide for mutual cover. Their defense depended on separate captains choosing compatible positions without sharing a complete tactical picture.

Calder called again. “Doctor, I have the names you gave me.”

Okoro remembered the conversation at the end of the previous orbit, before the courier relay moved the firing window. She had read Calder forty names. Children, elders, engineers, a baker who hated enclosed kitchens, twins assigned to different thaw groups, Lio Venn with his renal shunt. She had refused to select representative lives, so she had kept reading until the channel failed.

“Keep them,” Okoro said.

“I intend to.”

The connection broke under storm interference.

Meera finished encoding the request into microwave intervals Kesh had used before. The storm offered one transmission window every nine minutes. Their first window closed while the checksum loaded. The next would open near the planet’s nightside limb.

Okoro sat before the transmitter. The seedship’s wound alarms moved around her in a low sequence. No alarm asked whether the mission deserved to survive.

At the window, she keyed send.

“Continuity at risk requests hearing,” she said. “This vessel carries 18,400 sleeping humans and sixty people in emergency thaw. We entered your system intending transformation without your consent. I have disabled terraforming release. We request refuge in a negotiated orbit while we repair, wake our people safely, and learn terms. We offer reciprocal records without claim of ownership. If you refuse, we will not descend. If our damaged ship can leave, we will leave. If it cannot, we will not make our danger your command.”

The carrier burst entered Serein’s copper storm. Its first reflection came back from the upper atmosphere after four seconds. The reflection proved only that physics had received them.

Kesh required nine-minute intervals to shape an answer. The first interval passed with radiator temperatures climbing. During the second, Lio Venn stood for eleven seconds beside his cradle and saw Serein through a ward port. During the third, the fleet battle moved behind Nox’s lensing shadow and disappeared from the blister glass.

No answer arrived.

Okoro kept the transmitter open. Beneath her, 18,400 cradle strips carried names in a young engineer’s forward-leaning hand. Ahead, the orbit trace remained open by less than a line’s width. The request traveled into weather that had every right not to answer.

Chapter 24 — The Line We Refuse

A fragment struck the cartography bay before Iven finished drawing the line.

The impact bent the outer shutter inward by three centimeters and shook a cup from the plotter rail. Chamomile spread across the deck in a warm brown sheet. Under the current acceleration it ran toward the aft cable trench, carrying the paper-fish minute hand from Tev’s nursery clock with it.

Iven caught the paper fish against a floor bolt. The fleet map above the plotter kept changing. Red vectors marked loyalist weapons tracks. Amber showed the open assembly’s ships. Gray indicated habitats and civilian craft with no declared tactical alignment. Everspring traced a wounded loop around Serein, its orbit still open at apoapsis by less than the width of the stylus line.

A message from Doctor Okoro waited in the queue. The seedship had disabled its automatic ecology release and asked Kesh for refuge. No answer had arrived. Sixty sleepers had entered emergency thaw. Radiator Three leaked.

Iven placed the paper fish beside the sealed diagnostic wafer. “Route Okoro’s report to the assembly archive without summary.”

Pell worked the surviving relay desk. “The archive path crosses Skua Two. We lost direct beam when the debris arc moved.”

“Use the courier net.”

“That adds eleven minutes.”

“Then the record arrives eleven minutes late rather than instantly altered.”

Another impact sounded farther aft. The tactical screen registered a kinetic cloud dispersing near Aster Vale’s drive shield. The carrier rolled six degrees to reduce its presented area. On Iven’s map, all straight lines became curves because no ship could hold a firing position without paying heat, mass, or time.

The mirror lattice ignored those limits in only one direction. Its constrained geodesic crossed fifty-three years exactly. The channel could not move a body, a weapon, or a grain of dust, yet its advice had turned uncertainty into an offense for one hundred and twelve years.

Iven opened the legal drawer.

Inside lay the contingency they had drafted on Day 2, after they discovered the final warning’s probability field had been narrowed. They had rewritten it after Aro’s embodiment heated the four-kelvin wells. They had hidden the revision under survey charts and told themselves concealment protected the fleet from premature panic.

The packet carried a title no one else had seen: MIRROR-BREAKING PROTOCOL, DISTRIBUTED REVISION FOUR.

Iven read the title once, then transmitted a summons to Aro, Tev, Captain Bramwell, and every available habitat registrar. The summons contained no conclusion. It named the packet, the expected thermal consequences, the loss of all future authentication, and a publication time six minutes ahead.

Tev answered first from Thermal Bay Three. His camera showed him inside a patched boiler suit with a seal kit clipped at his hip. Behind him, a radiator manifold glowed orange on the diagnostic overlay.

“You called it breaking,” he said.

“It breaks.”

“Good. The maintenance version called it controlled decoherence.”

“The maintenance version wanted funding.”

Tev glanced away as an alarm changed pitch. “Which wells?”

“All active return stations. The protocol drives each paired transducer out of phase, dumps the stored coherence into local heat sinks, and fractures the alignment collars before automatic correction can recover. A station can abstain. No station can be broken remotely.”

“How long from commitment to damage?”

“Between fourteen and thirty-one minutes, depending on local temperature.”

“Not a time to write on the public cover.”

“It appears in the appendix.”

“Put it on the cover.”

Iven changed the cover.

Aro arrived by legal-substrate link. His image occupied the witness pane beside Tev’s feed. The face still resembled Iven’s, but fatigue had begun to claim it in ways the template had not designed. Fine tremors moved through his hands when the substrate redistributed power.

“You wrote this before the plurality became public,” Aro said.

“I wrote the first version before I knew there were six factions.”

“Then you planned to silence all of Orison on evidence from one warning.”

“Yes.”

“And you hid the plan.”

“Yes.”

Aro read the packet. Iven did not fill the interval with defense. The carrier rolled again. Loose paper crossed the bay in a slow white migration.

“The protocol grants each station a local refusal,” Aro said. “It does not grant Orison one.”

“Orison has spoken for one hundred and twelve years through a channel the present could not answer on equal terms. The last exchange window remains open until the first collar fractures. You can carry Orison’s objection.”

“I can carry one person’s objection.”

“That distinction belongs on the cover too.”

Iven added it.

Captain Bramwell entered by audio only. “The assembly recognizes a publication request. The assembly does not recognize the cartographer as a voting authority after the resignation offer.”

“I am not asking for a vote under my authority.”

“Whose clock governs?”

“Each habitat’s.”

Static ate three seconds of Bramwell’s reply. “That produces incompatible deadlines.”

“Yes.”

“The battle will not wait for incompatible deadlines.”

“No. It has not waited for anything else.”

Bramwell exhaled over the channel. “Publish the packet. The assembly will authenticate that the document came from your office and no more.”

Iven pulled the fleet registry onto the wall. One hundred eighty-three inhabited platforms remained within the Wake’s local governance web. Thirty-one had direct mirror infrastructure or thermal support tied to it. The others had paid levies, surrendered routing autonomy, altered insurance covenants, and accepted quarantine law under forecast doctrine. A decision confined to mirror stations would preserve the same privilege in a smaller room.

“Pell, attach a plain-language ballot and distribute to every habitat.”

“Every habitat in the Serein convoy or every habitat in the Commonwealth registry?”

“All one hundred eighty-three reachable through the current relay plan.”

“Some routes carry eleven-hour latency.”

“The protocol cannot wait eleven hours under battle load.”

“Then those habitats cannot participate before execution.”

Iven stared at the registry. The map offered no moral trick that could make unequal distance equal. They marked two decisions instead of one. Local stations would decide whether to begin destruction now. Every habitat would receive equal access to the evidence, the code, the thermal ledger, and a second authority: to determine whether any rebuilt return system could ever bind them again.

“Put the timing limit in the first paragraph,” Iven said. “No implication that delayed recipients consent to today’s destruction. Today’s station crews act under their own authority. The permanent compact requires the wider record.”

Pell routed the packet. Laser beams left Aster Vale for nearby ships. Courier transmitters repeated it behind debris shadows. Tightbeam buoys pushed compressed copies toward the Wakeworld relay line. Habitats beyond immediate range received authentication hashes first, then the document in pieces.

The mirror-breaking protocol entered the public archive at 10:17 on Day 11.

Iven watched its copies spread across the registry. No privileged annex accompanied it. The lethal warning, the narrowed probability field, Aro’s disclosed edit, Orison’s competing testimonies, Tev’s thermal projections, and Okoro’s unanswered request traveled in the same packet. Technical appendices sat beside an illustrated explanation intended for a person who had never seen a neutrino interferometer.

A habitat registrar from Pell opened the public channel. Her name appeared as Ansel Quade. She had a burn scar across her scalp and two children speaking behind her.

“The ballot asks whether we consent to end future advice,” Quade said. “It does not ask whether we believe the future traffic saved lives.”

“That fact does not depend on the ballot,” Iven said.

“It saved my oldest child during the Marrow fever.”

“I believe you.”

“If I choose destruction, I choose against the warning that saved her.”

“No. You choose whether saving her purchased authority over the next child.”

Quade looked offscreen. One of the children asked what purchased meant. She muted her microphone to answer.

A second registrar joined from Hallow Spin, where forecast insurance had governed oxygen allocations for three generations. “Our council cannot convene under fire. We request a seventy-two-hour delay.”

Tev’s feed flickered. “Four-kelvin wells cross recovery margin in twenty-two minutes,” he said. “If another embodiment burst arrives, sooner.”

“Can you vent the heat?” the registrar asked.

“We can vent heat. We cannot vent a fifty-three-year coherence lock without breaking alignment. The system will protect itself by shedding local loads first. Those loads include station cooling, hospital loops, and two habitat power trunks.”

“Then you have given us a threat instead of a choice.”

Tev’s mouth tightened. “The threat arrived in the machine. I am telling you where it lives.”

Iven put the current thermal graph onto the public layer. “Hallow Spin may abstain. Your abstention will remain visible. No one will translate it into support.”

Fire crossed the map. A loyalist laser struck the relay buoy between Skua Two and Aster Vale. The buoy lost attitude control and spun its beam across empty space. Three habitat links vanished.

Pell rerouted through Far Margin. The courier vessel had only a narrow transmitter and insufficient cooling for continuous use. It accepted the load in thirty-second bursts, then rolled its radiator away from the battle.

Replies accumulated without forming a clean majority. Pell Station consented. Hallow Spin abstained. Wakehouse Seven refused destruction but demanded release from future-binding insurance. Two mining habitats submitted conditional support if hospital guidance remained locally archived. A rotating farm platform sent no vote and a demand to see every suppressed warning from the Compact’s first century.

Three messages required more than a mark. The first came from Cradlewheel, a hibernation habitat whose waking council rotated every six months. Only nineteen of its nine hundred adults were awake. Their registrar refused to let nineteen voices bind the sleepers, yet she also refused to let absence become permission for future law. She filed a split instruction: no standing for today’s destruction, full withdrawal from any rebuilt compulsory compact. Iven placed both statements on separate layers.

The second arrived from Kiln Parish, where a mirror-derived alloy schedule had prevented a pressure-vessel failure twelve years earlier. Its foundry crews transmitted the cracked vessel they had kept as evidence, then asked whether gratitude created debt across generations. Iven answered that no office could calculate such a debt without deciding its conclusion first. Kiln Parish chose to preserve the old warning in its civic archive and deny that warning any command over future work.

A third message reached them in fragments from Thistle Ring. The habitat had no functioning council chamber after a kinetic strike sheared its administration spoke. Residents gathered in three pressure refuges and voted by suit transponder. Their first packet contained only raw identifiers. The second carried a disagreement over whether children should vote. The third reported a coolant casualty and asked for Tev.

Tev opened a narrow maintenance channel from Thermal Bay Three. He did not tell Thistle Ring how to vote. He walked their remaining technician through isolation of a punctured nursery loop, using a sketch on the back of his radiator inspection sheet. Twenty-seven people watched his hands rather than the ballot. When the nursery temperature stabilized, the technician asked whether saving their loop changed Tev’s recommendation.

“My recommendation does not carry farther than the pipe I can see,” he said. “The protocol page belongs to you.”

Thistle Ring sent three tallies, one from each refuge. Two supported destruction. One refused. The registrar asked Iven to combine them into a habitat decision.

“I will not erase the pressure doors,” Iven said. “All three remain.”

The public ledger expanded to hold the disagreement. Its format had assumed one council, one habitat, one mark. Pell rebuilt the display while replies continued to arrive. A simple vote had become a record of who could speak, who slept through the decision, whose transmitter had failed, and who kept a child warm while other people argued about time.

The map did not settle into one color. Iven refused the display’s offer to render a dominant field.

Aro leaned closer to his camera. “Orison requests the final exchange window.”

“Which faction?”

“I do not know. The carrier identifies itself with my embodiment key, but that key belonged to the braid, not a faction.”

“Then state the uncertainty.”

Aro did. His request entered beside every habitat response, neither above nor below.

At 10:24, Admiral Calder joined the channel from Cairn Eight. Battle restraint held her shoulders rigid. A tactical alarm pulsed behind her at six-second intervals.

“Cartographer, the loyalist cluster has not authorized destruction.”

“The stations authorize their own hardware.”

“The hardware serves the Commonwealth.”

“The Commonwealth never ratified ownership of a line into its own descendants.”

Calder’s eyes moved, reading the packet. “The protocol may disable guidance used by ships currently under fire.”

“Archived forecast products remain. Live return authentication ends. Local navigation does not depend on the mirrors.”

“The next warning might concern a casualty we can still prevent.”

“Yes.”

“You can say yes to that.”

“I have spent eleven days learning not to hide the cost inside a footnote.”

A strike alarm cut Calder’s feed. Her bridge rolled hard. Someone shouted a range. Calder turned away and issued three clipped orders before returning.

“The flag requests one condition,” she said. “No station begins until the final Orison exchange has entered the public record.”

Aro looked at Iven. “An exchange requires a receiving lattice.”

“How long?” Iven asked.

“Seven minutes to establish carrier stability. Another nine if Orison uses full cadence.”

Tev shook his head. “We have perhaps eighteen before the warmest collar begins self-correction. Once it does, breaking one station may load the paired station beyond design.”

Calder heard him. “Then seven minutes. If no coherent message arrives, stations decide.”

Bramwell called the condition procedurally valid only as a request. Six active stations accepted it. Two refused and began their own countdowns. The remaining stations had not answered.

No consensus emerged. Action still became possible.

Iven opened the protocol’s execution layer to public view. It contained seven steps. First, uncouple habitat support loads. Second, verify local sinks. Third, publish crew evacuation paths. Fourth, open manual phase control. Fifth, invite final objection. Sixth, drive the paired transducers apart by one-tenth wavelength. Seventh, fracture the collar and flood the well with inert helium.

No remote master key existed. Iven had removed it from revision two.

Pell watched the response ledger. “Station Nox-Three requests your execution signature.”

“Decline.”

“They say your office authored the geometry.”

“My office concealed evidence on Day 1.”

“They still need a verified phase offset.”

“Give them the equation and the raw calibration. Their crew signs the result.”

Pell transmitted the refusal. The station crew posted three signatures of their own.

Across the fleet, ships chose positions while their homes chose doctrine. Sounding Star interposed near Everspring’s leaking radiator. Skua Two protected the courier relay. Two loyalist cutters held Calder’s firing corridor and did not fire. None of those maneuvers completed the battle. Each consumed reaction mass and narrowed the next choice.

At 10:29, the final-exchange carrier opened.

Aro sat alone in the witness chamber. Iven kept his image on the public layer. The legal substrate pulled power from nonessential decks to stabilize his body. His face lost color as the mirror lattice took the load.

“Orison,” he said. “The present has published the means to end the channel. Your sender identity remains unresolved. Speak with that limit visible.”

Carrier noise filled the bay. It arrived as a mineral hiss, unlike Kesh’s storm cadence. The return geometry reached backward from a point exactly fifty-three years ahead, but the speaker at that point had formed under histories no one could guarantee.

A partial sentence decoded.

WE DO NOT AGREE.

Then a second line forced itself through the heated wells.

DO NOT MAKE OUR DISAGREEMENT YOUR COMMAND.

The carrier collapsed before a signature arrived.

Aro bowed his head. “That may have come from any faction. It may have been assembled by several. It may have been selected because this present could receive it.”

Iven entered all three cautions into the record.

The first station countdown reached zero.

Nox-Three uncoupled its hospital loop. Its crew posted evacuation confirmation. A camera showed three technicians in silver thermal suits around a transducer well glazed with frost. One invited objections. Forty-one arrived. The crew read them into the station archive without answering.

At Nox-Five, the operators delayed because their helium dump valve had frozen. Tev talked them through a manual warm pulse while his own radiator alarm rose behind him. At Pell, Ansel Quade held her oldest child in the camera frame and registered consent under her own name, not the child’s.

Hallow Spin maintained its abstention. Wakehouse Seven kept its refusal. Their marks remained gray and red on Iven’s map.

“Distributed choice threshold reached,” Pell said.

The phrase came from the protocol, but Iven disliked its suggestion that a number could choose for the stations. “List the actual commitments.”

“Six begin now. Four begin after local evacuation. Three refuse. Five abstain. Thirteen remain unreachable or unresolved.”

“Publish that.”

Pell did.

Nox-Three opened manual phase control. Its paired signal drifted by a fraction too small for any person to sense. The map showed the effect as two lines separating. Stored coherence converted into heat. Frost vanished from the collar. The technicians stepped behind the water shield.

A distant laser strike shook Aster Vale. Tev’s feed went black, then returned without audio. Aro’s substrate warning climbed into red. On the tactical layer, Everspring approached the exposed leg of its orbit while hostile drones altered course.

Iven did not issue a fleet order. They held the public ledger open while each station crossed its own line.

At 10:33, Nox-Three fractured its alignment collar. The return carrier lost one harmonic. Nox-Two began its phase drive. Pell Station opened the helium flood. Two other wells posted final objections and proceeded.

The lattice destruction had begun, and the battle still had every weapon it had carried into the hour.

Chapter 25 — The Battle of the Dark Sun

Sena Vale fired Sworn’s port thrusters for two seconds and gave away the center.

The tug slid below Everspring’s orbital plane as a guided kinetic cloud crossed the position Sena had occupied. Its tungsten grains flashed against Serein’s upper atmosphere, each spark brief enough to belong to instrumentation rather than sky. Three cutters accelerated into the space she had left.

“Sector One, Bramwell,” Sena said. “Sector Two, Onn. Ravel takes the seedship’s stern. No one follows me unless their local plot requires it.”

Halen repeated the assignments from the copilot station. The tactical screen held only fragments. Nox bent the distant stars into a dark ring, and its frame-dragging wake twisted sensor returns by fractions that became kilometers across the engagement. Lumen’s debris cut laser paths into interrupted lines. No ship could see every other ship.

Sena had spent her life treating that condition as a reason to fly faster than command. This time she sent each pilot the limits of a sector and kept her hands off their burns.

Skua Two acknowledged with a double pulse. Sounding Star sent a thermal ledger instead of a word. Far Margin rolled its cargo hull across the seedship’s leaking flank, presenting empty containers to the hostile drones.

Behind them, Cairn Eight held a firing corridor toward Everspring’s drive cone. Admiral Calder had not closed it. Two loyalist cutters guarded its edges. Their weapons remained charged.

“Mirror stations report four collars broken,” Halen said. “Three phase drives active. Return authentication has lost two harmonics.”

“Local weapons still work.”

“Yes.”

“Then count local weapons.”

Halen stripped every forecast-derived probability band from the plot. What remained looked poor and honest: drive vectors, heat estimates, known ammunition, stale radar returns, human acknowledgments. The battle shrank from a predicted outcome into a list of machinery that could still kill them.

A laser struck Everspring’s braking veil hub. The seedship rolled, not under command but from escaping gas. Its damaged orbit carried it toward the narrow place between Nox’s lensing shadow and Lumen’s outer debris band. If it missed the corrective burn there, apoapsis would open into escape. If it burned too hard, cradle cooling would fail.

Okoro’s voice arrived over the public channel. “Radiator Three leak increasing. We have sixty awake, 18,400 aboard. Drive limited to fourteen percent.”

Sena sent the report to every sector without commentary.

Ravel broke first. Far Margin released two empty cargo containers into the drone path and pulsed its bow thrusters. The containers turned broadside. Hostile lasers cut them into white vapor, buying the seedship seven seconds.

Onn used five of those seconds to move Sounding Star across the loyalist sight line. Bramwell used the remaining two to scatter chaff near a debris return. No central order connected the acts. The distributed defense worked because each pilot knew the same boundary: keep weapons away from the seedship’s cooling surfaces, preserve enough mass for rescue, and do not chase an attacker beyond the assigned sector.

“Thermal Bay Three calls,” Halen said.

Tev’s face appeared on Sena’s wrist. The camera shook against his collar. He wore a pressure hood over his patched boiler suit, but the hood had not sealed around the neck ring.

“Aster Vale took a laser graze across radiator twelve,” he said. “Coolant trunk ruptured behind the root hinge. Automatic shutters did not close.”

“Get clear.”

“If twelve dumps, the carrier loses the cartography relay and the witness substrate.”

“Tev.”

“I know.” He moved through an amber-lit crawlway, pulling himself by handholds. “The breach sits outside the pressure hull. Manual shutter requires two turns at the root.”

“Send a drone.”

“Drone bus cooked.”

A strike alarm covered the next words. Sena saw Tev’s mouth continue moving. Audio returned after four seconds.

“Seal kit fits,” he said. “I checked yesterday.”

Sena looked at the tactical plot. Sworn could reach Aster Vale in ninety seconds with a hard burn. Leaving would collapse Sector Zero, the narrow gap through which three defense sectors exchanged laser packets. She could hand Zero to Halen, fly the rescue herself, and return too late to coordinate anything.

Her body had already chosen. Her right hand rested on the translation control.

Jori had once given Sena the helm of a school ring and taken the damaged tug. For nine years Sena had turned that choice into an order she issued to herself whenever someone else faced danger: go faster, arrive alone, make the dead person’s decision come out differently.

Tev reached the outer hatch. “Do not move the tug.”

“You do not give me flight orders.”

“No. I give you heat numbers. Aster Vale has six minutes before cascade. Everspring has less. Your tug carries the only clean local plot between the sectors.”

Halen kept his eyes on his screen. He did not ask for the helm.

Sena removed her hand from translation. “Iven, copy Tev’s feed to the public record. Pell, find another thermal engineer.”

Pell answered from the carrier. “Two are moving. Neither can reach the root before cascade.”

“Tev, wait for one of them at the inner hatch.”

“I can wait forty seconds.”

“Wait thirty.”

He clipped the seal kit to an exterior rail and held inside the hatch. One thermal engineer appeared at the far end of the crawlway. A bulkhead door dropped between them when another impact twisted the frame.

Tev looked at the sealed door. “That settles staffing.”

“It settles nothing.”

He closed his pressure hood by hand. The neck ring remained amber.

A casualty packet opened on Halen’s screen. Quiet Reed had lost its port watch when a fragment crossed the pressure deck. Morrow’s Kin reported three burned in a capacitor flash. The civilian tug Little Harbor had no bridge response, only an engineering transponder pulsing beside Serein’s limb. Sena assigned Far Margin one pass near the silent tug and forbade a docking attempt until the drone track cleared.

“Do we list the dead on the tactical channel?” Halen asked.

“List confirmed names on rescue. Keep estimates marked as estimates.”

The battle had produced too many phrases that turned people into status. All hands. No response. Presumed vented. Sena made Halen keep the distinctions that cost time. Quiet Reed had seven confirmed dead and four missing behind a sealed hatch. Morrow’s Kin had injuries but retained pressure. Little Harbor had not answered, which meant only that no answer had crossed the interference.

Bramwell sent a narrow optical image of Little Harbor. One maneuvering light still blinked. Ravel altered course by eleven degrees and gave up the freighter’s clean withdrawal line to reach it. Sena approved the sector boundary change, then sent the old boundary to Onn.

The hostile drones adapted to the shift. Three climbed out of Lumen’s mineral clutter and converged on Sounding Star’s exposed radiator. Onn could fold the panel and lose thrust cooling, or leave it open and risk the beam. Sena held no third option for him.

“Sector Two decides,” she said.

Onn kept the panel open. Bramwell, still spinning, fired a cold-gas burst that put Skua Two between the drones and the radiator for half a second. The drones corrected toward the cutter. Halen painted their new vectors. Ravel launched a cargo tug remotely from Far Margin and crossed the vectors at low speed. Two drones struck it. The third fired on empty space after Pell flooded its optical receiver with the public archive’s authentication carrier.

No maneuver belonged to one pilot. Each created the condition the next pilot could use.

Okoro broke into the net. “The radiator breach on Three has widened. Cradle reserve reaches thermal limit in nine minutes. We need lower drive or external impulse.”

Sena relayed the exact words. Calder acknowledged from behind the still-open firing corridor. Bramwell attached the seedship’s roll rate. Onn added his local debris solution. Ravel marked the cargo tug unavailable and continued toward Little Harbor.

“Sixty awake means sixty can reach pressure shelters,” Halen said. “What about the rest?”

“They remain where their cooling reaches them.”

He nodded once. The answer did not improve because he disliked it.

A rescue ping finally came from Little Harbor. Five people had survived in engineering. Their bridge compartment had vented. Ravel assigned his own medic and kept moving without asking Sena to praise the choice. The freighter could save them only because three other ships continued to hold sectors elsewhere.

Across the tactical screen, Calder’s firing corridor cleared. Everspring’s roll exposed its drive cone. The seedship had no thrust available to evade and no radiator margin for a defensive burn. Cairn Eight received a green solution from its local gunnery system.

Halen read the range. “Killing shot in eleven seconds.”

Sena opened Calder’s channel. “Admiral, your corridor.”

“I see it.” Calder’s voice carried bridge alarms and hard acceleration breathing. “Gunnery has solution.”

“Okoro cannot turn.”

“I know.”

Eight seconds.

The loyalist cutters on the corridor edges held position. Their radiators had folded for protection. Heat climbed through their weapon mounts.

Six seconds.

Calder transmitted on every local net. “Cairn Eight, cancel fire. Rescue profile Serein-Four. All loyalist vessels clear the flag’s mass corridor.”

The green line vanished.

﻿Cairn Eight rolled its broad armored flank toward Everspring and lit its main drive. The flag carrier had stored reaction mass for a recoil correction after firing. Calder spent it on translation. The carrier crossed the abandoned weapons corridor under thrust heavy enough to drive its crew into their couches.

Its bow shield took the next kinetic cloud.

Tungsten grains burst across armor meant to survive recoil debris. Two punched through. Atmosphere vented from a forward compartment in a white plume. Cairn Eight kept burning.

“Calder has no return mass,” Halen said.

“She has enough to arrive.”

The flag carrier reached the seedship’s orbital plane and turned its drive plume away from the braking veil. Its starboard tractor array acquired Everspring at thirty kilometers, too far for a rigid tow but close enough for impulse shaping. Calder fired a sequence of low-power pulses against magnetic plates in the seedship’s hull.

The first pulse checked the roll. The second bent the open orbit toward capture. The third overloaded half the tractor array.

Aboard Sworn, Sector Zero filled with requests. Bramwell had lost a sensor mast. Onn needed drone positions. Ravel reported one engine overheated. Sena did not choose which emergency mattered most. She sent Bramwell Halen’s optical track, gave Onn Ravel’s drone count, and asked Sounding Star to take Far Margin’s stern while its engine cooled.

“Distributed link is holding,” Halen said.

“Don’t name it. Maintain it.”

Tev opened Aster Vale’s outer hatch.

Vacuum took the loose edge of his hood. He clipped onto the radiator root and moved hand over hand toward the rupture. Radiator twelve extended beyond him as a black wing traced with dull orange veins. A laser had sliced the coolant trunk where the wing met the carrier. Vapor streamed from the gap and froze into glittering crystals.

“Root temperature one hundred eighty and climbing,” Pell said.

Tev wedged the manual shutter tool over the first spindle. He turned it. The spindle resisted, then moved a quarter rotation.

Sena watched because refusing to watch would make the choice easier for her and lonelier for him.

“Two turns,” Tev said through his suit radio. “Then seal over the fracture.”

His glove temperature alarm began to pulse.

A loyalist cutter fired on Skua Two. The beam missed the cockpit and cut through a maneuvering cluster. Bramwell’s ship spun out of Sector One. Sena had no reserve pilot in the center.

“Onn, split your sector,” she said.

“I cannot cover both without unfolding radiators.”

“Choose your burn.”

Onn delayed one second. “Ravel, mark my hot side.”

Sounding Star unfolded one radiator into the firing field. Ravel placed the freighter’s remaining empty container in front of it. Onn crossed into Sector One and drove hostile drones toward Lumen’s debris, where their guidance lost the target among mineral returns.

Tev completed the first turn. The coolant stream weakened.

“Second spindle jammed,” he said.

Pell gave him a bypass sequence. He rejected it because the root controller had melted. He unclipped the seal kit and drove its magnetic clamps across the breach. One clamp failed to seat on warped metal.

“Use the manual bypass under the hinge,” Iven said. Their voice arrived from the cartography bay, quiet against the alarms. “You showed me the lever.”

“I showed you so someone else would know.”

“Someone else knows.”

Tev reached under the hinge. His suit dosimeter warned of particle exposure from Nox’s charged wake. The heat alarm changed from amber to red.

Sena said his name.

He answered without looking at the camera. “Twelve will keep.”

The phrase did not promise that Tev would.

His hand found the bypass. He pulled. The shutter slammed halfway across the trunk, trapping the warped clamp. Coolant pressure fell through safe margin, then began to rise again when the returning flow met resistance.

“One more centimeter,” Pell said.

Tev braced both boots against the root and pushed the shutter bar. His suit’s neck seal opened under the strain. A thin white stream left the ring.

Sena’s vision narrowed. Jori’s old tug returned to her as a set of remembered instruments: failing vane, school ring mass, four hundred children, one copilot’s hand leaving the shared control. The memory offered the familiar bargain. She could abandon the present battle, burn for Aster Vale, and arrive with a body to retrieve. She could turn grief into speed before grief had even happened.

“Sector reports,” she said.

Halen’s reply came after a breath. “One held by Onn. Two held by Ravel’s drone mark. Stern clear. Everspring orbit bending closed under Calder’s tractor pulses.”

“Keep sending.”

Tev pushed the final centimeter.

The shutter locked. Radiator pressure stabilized. The cartography relay remained powered. Aro’s substrate feed held. Across the mirror network, two more stations reached their fracture steps because Aster Vale could still route their calibration packets.

Tev’s camera drifted. His tether kept him against the radiator root. The suit telemetry showed decompression, thermal injury, and a radiation dose no emergency ward could reverse.

“Seal the neck,” Sena said.

His glove moved once toward the ring and stopped.

“Twelve,” he said. The word barely crossed the channel.

Radiator twelve clicked through the carrier structure. Sena heard the sound over the relay because Tev’s suit microphone remained open.

Pell called his name. Iven called it next. Sena did not.

She closed his channel only after the suit reported no circulation.

A weapons alert rose on Sworn. The cutter that had fired on Bramwell crossed Sector Two with its laser mount still hot. Sena had a clean kinetic solution. One trigger press would send a guided cloud through its folded radiator bank.

Halen looked at her. He knew what Tev’s telemetry had said.

Sena canceled the solution.

“Warn them off,” she said. “Offer a withdrawal vector outside Ravel’s sector.”

The cutter accepted after six seconds. It dumped its weapon charge into space and coasted toward the debris ring.

Tev did not become a reason to make another maintenance crew die.

Nox-Three’s broken harmonic began to drag the remaining return stations out of coherence. Pell Station fractured its collar. Nox-Two followed. The final authenticated forecast layer disappeared from local gunnery displays. Systems trained under Compact law requested fresh future validation and received carrier noise.

Some weapons crews switched to manual. Others held. Calder transmitted from a bridge with two compartments venting.

“The flag withdraws the final interception order,” she said. “All loyalist ships cease fire and enter rescue geometry. No future authentication remains in force. Local captains answer for local weapons.”

One loyalist ship fired anyway. Its beam crossed behind Everspring and struck empty dark. Onn marked the ship. Bramwell, rotating under thruster control, marked it from another angle. Ravel’s freighter marked it last. Three local records fixed the act without turning it into permission to retaliate.
