At 10:41, the last station required for the constrained geodesic broke phase. The return lattice lost coherence across the fifty-three-year interval. Carrier noise fell away. No final warning arrived. No hidden command replaced it.

The hostile drones already in flight continued toward their last targets. Sena assigned them by sector. Onn burned down four. Ravel dragged three into debris. Halen spoofed two with Sworn’s courier transponder. Calder’s damaged tractor array caught the final drone in a magnetic pulse and threw it wide of Everspring’s leaking radiator.

Then no weapon remained in flight.

Rescue beacons replaced targeting pings across every sector Sena could still hear, each one naming a damaged ship and requested approach.

The dark ring around Nox held its shape. It had no tactical meaning once the displays stopped predicting through it.

Sena released her harness and floated above the courier seat. Her hands did not reach for another control. Around her, separate ships reported heat, holes, missing crew, and people alive in compartments that should have vented. Everspring’s orbit closed by a narrow margin under Calder’s last tractor pulse.

On Aster Vale, radiator twelve kept the carrier’s heat moving into space. Tev remained tethered at its root.

Sena assigned recovery sectors. Bramwell took the loyalist cutter. Onn took Skua Two. Ravel moved toward Cairn Eight’s vented bow. She gave Tev’s recovery to the two thermal engineers already at the inner hatch.

The empty mug in Sworn’s courier bay clicked against its cooler when attitude control changed. Sena heard it this time. She did not call it Jori. She did not call it Tev.

She called Halen’s next range and guided the tug toward the first damaged ship that had asked for help.

Chapter 26 — The Price of One Future

Iven erased the first red vector at 10:46 on Day 11.

The line had marked Cairn Eight’s abandoned firing corridor. They replaced it with a blue rescue arc and entered the carrier’s actual mass state: two forward compartments vented, half the tractor array fused, recoil reserve spent on Everspring. The map resisted the change because its tactical layer expected a weapon to remain a weapon until the engagement closed.

Iven closed it.

Not the battle. No cartographer possessed that authority. They closed the software object and opened a casualty ledger beside it.

Pell sat at the relay desk with Tev’s paper-fish minute hand tucked under the edge of the keyboard. The nursery clock itself had broken when the fragment struck the bay. The hand pointed nowhere useful.

“Quiet Reed confirms seven,” Pell said. “Four missing behind the sealed hatch.”

“Keep missing separate.”

“Little Harbor confirms two dead on the bridge, five alive in engineering.”

“Names?”

“Coming by courier packet.”

Iven entered the statuses without allowing the map to shade the unknown people red. During the Compact years, forecasts had treated missing information as a probability to be narrowed. Today the uncertainty remained a field with edges no office could erase.

The ledger grew. Tev Arin, confirmed dead during manual closure of Radiator Twelve. Three crew on Morrow’s Kin with capacitor burns. Nine injured aboard Skua Two. Eleven on Cairn Eight missing from the vented compartments, four confirmed dead, twenty-six injured under acceleration. Two station technicians with helium exposure. One coolant casualty at Thistle Ring, condition unresolved.

The battle’s casualties did not fit a single total yet. Iven published the ledger anyway.

A private message arrived from Admiral Calder.

Cartographer. Hold the unconfirmed entries until flag review. Families will read them.

Iven returned the message with one annotation: Families are among the reasons not to convert missing into dead or silence into safety.

Calder called instead.

“The flag did not ask you to alter status,” she said.

“You asked me to hold it.”

“I asked you to prevent rumor.”

“A blank field also produces rumor.”

Calder’s bridge behind her had lost one wall display. Emergency fabric covered a hatch. She wore no uniform coat, only a pressure layer darkened by smoke.

“The flag requests a visible verification column,” she said after a pause. “Source, time, confidence, person responsible for the mark.”

“That belongs there.”

“And a correction trail no office can delete.”

Iven added both.

Calder watched the new columns appear. “The flag withdraws its request to hold.”

The exchange entered the public record because neither of them asked for privacy.

Before the session opened, Iven carried a printed copy of the ledger down to Recovery Deck Four. The lifts had lost central scheduling, so each deck posted a person beside the shaft to call destinations through a hand radio. A cook from the lower galley worked the cartography stop. She read Iven’s page while they waited.

“My sister serves on Quiet Reed,” she said. “Port watch.”

Iven found the ship’s block. Seven confirmed dead. Four missing behind a sealed hatch. No names had crossed yet.

“Do you know which list?” Iven asked.

“No.”

The lift arrived. The cook did not board. She kept working the radio because knowing less than death did not excuse the ship from moving injured people.

Recovery Four had been a survey-equipment hold that morning. Medics had strapped fabric partitions between sample racks. Calder loyalists lay beside open-assembly crews because burns observed no command cluster. The air carried antiseptic, hot insulation, and coolant from suits cut away at the seams.

Pell’s brother Tavin sat against a crate with one hand wrapped in clear gel. Iven had met him twice and remembered neither meeting until he said Pell’s name. He had taken the capacitor flash on Morrow’s Kin. Two other burned crew slept under analgesic hoods.

“The ledger says three injured,” Tavin said.

“It does.”

“Ressa stopped breathing on transfer. They started her again.”

Iven added the event with a medic’s verification and did not turn it into a death count. Ressa’s row carried alive, critical, resuscitated at 10:58. The distinction occupied twelve words. It contained the work of six people.

At the far partition, the two thermal engineers from Aster Vale had placed Tev inside a closed recovery sleeve. His patched boiler suit lay folded on a chair after decontamination. The manual shutter tool rested across it. Someone had set his empty mug beside the tool.

Iven stood outside the partition. The ledger already held the facts. Repeating them would not make the sleeve more accurate.

One of the engineers approached. Her face showed a pressure bruise from the outer hatch. “We recovered the seal kit,” she said. “One clamp fused into the root. Twelve can’t retract, but it can radiate.”

“Who signed the recovery?”

“Both of us. Sena witnessed by live feed. Pell confirmed telemetry.”

Iven entered the chain. “His family aboard Everspring must receive notice through Okoro, not the public channel.”

“They will read the public ledger first if the delay runs long.”

“I know.”

No communication path could make grief arrive in the proper order. Iven attached a notice-in-progress mark rather than hide Tev’s name. The engineer approved the wording. They both signed it.

On the way back, the lift stopped at every deck. A pressure crew entered carrying a door with a fragment embedded in it. Two children from an evacuated compartment held each other’s sleeves. The galley cook remained beside the cartography shaft when Iven stepped out. No packet from Quiet Reed had arrived.

Iven gave her the current page. “This changes as sources arrive.”

“Then don’t close it.”

“I won’t.”

At 11:03, Captain Bramwell convened the cease-fire session over the surviving local nets. No one gathered in one room. The ships lacked safe docking geometry, and Everspring could not spare heat for a full-power conference beam. Each participant joined through whatever channel remained.

Calder appeared from Cairn Eight. Okoro spoke from the seedship’s communications blister. Sena joined by audio while Sworn towed a crippled cutter. Aro’s legal substrate rendered him with reduced resolution. Habitat delegates entered as names, still images, or delayed text. Iven kept the casualty ledger beside every face.

Bramwell began with machinery. No weapons remained in flight. Six loyalist ships had entered rescue geometry. One had withdrawn toward Lumen with weapons cold. Three captains had not yet acknowledged Calder’s cease-fire, but optical tracks showed no active targeting. The return lattice had lost coherent transmission across the fifty-three-year line. Local mirror wells continued cooling under station control.

“Does any participant claim an active military objective?” Bramwell asked.

No one answered for twelve seconds.

Captain Besh of Watchful Coil finally appeared as a text packet. Preserve fleet capacity. Recover personnel. No further engagement objective.

Calder read the line. “The flag concurs.”

Okoro added, “Everspring requests rescue without accepting fleet custody.”

“The flag acknowledges the distinction,” Calder said.

Bramwell entered military conflict ended at 11:07, subject to correction if any ship resumed fire. The record did not transform the previous hour into peace. It established what weapons were doing now.

Next came the archives.

Thirty-one mirror-linked stations held fragments of future guidance, authentication histories, suppressed warnings, embodiment code, and the Compact’s private decision logs. The physical lattice had broken, but the accumulated advice remained. Every participant understood that whoever controlled those archives could rebuild forecast sovereignty without rebuilding time communication. A council could call old advice precedent. An insurer could turn archived probability bands into compulsory routes. A fleet could classify dissent as a known casualty risk.

Captain Bramwell asked Iven to present custodial options.

“I have none,” Iven said.

“You authored the archive map.”

“I authored a way to distribute it.”

Calder leaned toward her camera. “Someone must verify integrity.”

“Many someones.”

“The flag will not accept an archive seeded with altered warnings.”

“Neither will I. Publish cryptographic hashes, physical manifests, access logs, and every correction. Give independent copies to habitats, fleet courts, hospitals, and station crews. Let each repository state what it has omitted.”

Bramwell said, “The cartographer’s office could coordinate that.”

“No.”

The word left Iven before they prepared a courteous form.

Aro’s low-resolution face turned toward them. Okoro waited. Calder did not look surprised.

Iven continued. “I concealed the lethal annex because I believed privileged interpretation might protect people from a command. I drafted the destruction contingency in secret because I believed privileged timing might protect people from panic. Both acts placed me between evidence and everyone affected by it. I will not accept secret stewardship of the surviving archive.”

“Then resign,” Calder said.

“My resignation remains offered. This refusal does not depend on whether anyone accepts it.”

Ansel Quade joined from Pell Station. “Distributed custody can still hide behind volume. A person cannot read one hundred and twelve years of logs.”

“Correct,” Iven said. “Every index carries authorship, omissions, and contest links. No official digest without the raw path beside it. No risk score without the names excluded by the score. No sealed annex.”

Quade looked offscreen toward one of her children. “And advice?”

“Advice may remain advice. No insurance penalty, command presumption, or civic incapacity attaches to refusal.”

Wakehouse Seven submitted its standing demand to withdraw from all future-binding covenants. Hallow Spin requested a transition period for oxygen contracts written around forecast guarantees. Cradlewheel repeated that its sleeping population could not consent by absence. The session recorded all three without forcing them into one settlement.

A storm carrier interrupted at 11:19.

The sound differed from Orison’s failed return channel. It arrived through Serein’s microwave weather in nine-minute spacing, each interval carrying etched harmonics that Meera Sol’s translation layer could parse. Okoro’s image sharpened as she routed the signal from Everspring.

“Kesh has answered,” she said.

No one spoke over the first interval.

The translation appeared one clause at a time.

CONTINUITY AT RISK HAS BEEN HEARD.

Nine minutes later:

ALTERATION HAS PAUSED. APPROACH HAS NOT.

Okoro touched the edge of her console. “They distinguish our disabled release from our presence.”

Aro said, “They distinguish action from condition.”

Iven entered both interpretations as interpretations, not translation.

The third interval carried an orbit diagram. Kesh marked a band between Everspring’s current apoapsis and Lumen’s outer memory ring. The band excluded three storm columns and every etched site known from the survey probes. Its geometry shifted with Serein’s weather, so no permanent border could describe it.

Meera read the next clause. “VESSEL MAY REMAIN WHILE NO DESCENT OPENS. HEAT MAY CROSS. MATTER MAY NOT.”

Okoro asked for clarification. Waste heat crossed all boundaries as radiation. Matter meant atmosphere, organisms, machines, or debris. Kesh’s syntax did not yet separate those categories.

Iven watched the communication latency govern the room. Human argument wanted to fill each nine-minute gap. Bramwell enforced waiting. During the intervals, rescue reports entered the casualty ledger. One of Quiet Reed’s missing crew reached a pressure phone. A station technician’s helium exposure moved from unresolved to stable. Tev’s line did not change.

Kesh’s fourth interval arrived.

REFUGE IS PAUSE, NOT GRANT. PAUSE REQUIRES REMEMBERING BOTH DIRECTIONS.

Okoro said, “We accept pause.”

Calder raised one hand. “The fleet can guarantee no descent. It cannot guarantee no accidental matter crossing from damaged ships.”

“Then we disclose every loss,” Okoro said. “No fiction of perfect containment.”

Iven put a debris ledger beside the casualty ledger. Fragments, vented atmosphere, coolant crystals, spent chaff, tungsten grains, destroyed drones. The battle had already altered the system. Consent could not reverse matter that had crossed.

“What does remembering both directions mean?” Sena asked over audio.

Aro’s face tightened with thought. “Kesh may want access to our records while giving access to its own.”

“We do not assume,” Okoro said.

The next question went out through Meera’s layer: DEFINE REMEMBERING. The answer could not arrive for at least nine minutes.

Iven used the delay to open a fresh document. The title field asked for certainty. They wrote: PROVISIONAL TERMS, REVISION ZERO, ALL CLAUSES CONTESTABLE.

Their mother’s evacuation came back not as an image but as a route.

The official path had saved three thousand people by directing their habitat around a debris bloom. A routing officer had proposed a counter-route that would also have saved Sleep Kept. The forecast compactor rejected it as outside parameters. Iven’s mother died because the optimized evacuation carried no visible field for the one habitat its certainty excluded.

For years, Iven had believed the correction required a better map. Then they had believed it required withholding a dangerous map. Both beliefs preserved the cartographer at the center.

The new document contained no center. It held columns for Kesh translation, human interpretation, objection, operational limit, revision time, and known absence. Iven placed their mother’s route in the design notes as an example of what the absence column must carry.

Calder noticed. “You have entered Sleep Kept into contact terms.”

“I entered the failure mode.”

“Your mother’s name appears beside it.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“Because an empty category called excluded population did not keep her visible.”

Calder opened the casualty ledger on her own screen. Her brother’s name did not appear there; Hespar belonged to another disaster. Her nephew’s did. The living and dead of the current battle occupied rows around him.

“The flag proposes one addition,” she said. “Every operational limit lists who bears the risk if the limit fails.”

Iven added the column.

When Kesh answered, the translation arrived in two simultaneous textures. One carried the readable clause. The other formed a pattern of microwave phase shifts that Meera could not decode.

REMEMBERING MEANS MEMORY OFFERED, MEMORY RECEIVED, MEMORY REFUSED WITHOUT LOSS OF REFUGE.

The undecoded texture persisted under the words.

Okoro looked at Iven. “Reciprocal memory.”

“Only if refusal carries no penalty,” Iven said.

Aro added, “And only if copies have limits. Orison edited sixteen lives to make one witness persuasive. Exchange can become extraction while calling itself memory.”

Kesh could not hear the discussion until Meera encoded and sent it. They drafted conditions during the next interval. Each party selected records rather than exposing an archive wholesale. The receiver could decline a record without ending refuge. A record could carry terms for copying, quotation, translation, and deletion. Neither party claimed ownership of the other’s memory layers. Unknown consequences stayed visible.

Sena asked, “Can a memory be deleted after someone has learned it?”

“No,” Aro said. “The term can govern copies, not minds.”

“Write that.”

Iven did.

Quade asked whether sleepers could offer memories before waking. Okoro answered no. Calder asked whether fleet recordings of Kesh counted as captured material. Meera said the question required Kesh’s judgment. Bramwell marked every existing survey record disputed until terms clarified.

A practical test followed before anyone approved the language. Meera selected thirty seconds from the first-contact record, when Kesh had first modulated the storm into a response. The file contained raw microwave phases, the human translation, and three alternate translations the crew had rejected. Under the old survey rules, the Commonwealth owned all four as collected data.

Okoro changed the header to RECEIVED UNDER DISPUTE. “We can offer deletion of the local raw copy,” she said. “We cannot promise every relay cache will obey.”

Pell searched the route. Nine temporary caches had held fragments. Five reported clean deletion capability. Two had damaged storage. One belonged to Sounding Star and carried a battle lock. The last cache had vanished with the relay buoy.

“Then deletion cannot mean gone,” Quade said.

“It can mean every known holder undertakes not to use or replicate,” Aro replied. “The unknown copies remain a stated risk.”

Calder objected to that phrasing. “An undertaking without enforcement offers little.”

“Enforcement cannot restore a copied memory either,” Iven said. “Name the remedy we can perform: public notice, access revocation, destruction of known media, and liability decided in the treaty forum.”

Sena’s voice cut in from the tug. “Add a right to stop the exchange while a breach is investigated. Otherwise speed will outrun trust.”

Iven added it. The test exposed six failures in less than four minutes: uncertain ownership, incomplete deletion, translation drift, damaged caches, military locks, and absent enforcement. None invalidated exchange. Each prevented the word reciprocal from pretending both sides had equal machines or equal concepts of memory.

Meera transmitted the test conditions with the question. Kesh received not a polished principle but the places where the principle could fail.

The storm replied after two intervals.

PAUSE ACCEPTS THESE LIMITS. EXCHANGE MAY BEGIN WITH THE MEMORY OF APPROACH AND THE MEMORY OF REFUSAL.

Okoro selected Everspring’s approach record, including the original terraforming plan, her disabling order, the objections from the thaw ward, and the refuge request. She did not send it yet. She offered its manifest.

Kesh offered a manifest in return: the first probe impact, the names preserved at Cliffside, the storm’s deflection of the fleet strike, and a memory pattern translated only as RAIN LEARNING NOT TO KEEP.

No one understood the last item. It stayed untranslated in the provisional terms.

Bramwell read the contact conditions into the public session. Everspring would remain inside the moving refuge band without descent. Fleet ships would establish debris control outside Kesh’s marked storm columns. Salvage required disclosure and specific permission where matter touched memory sites. Reciprocal memory exchange would begin with manifests and refusals carrying no penalty. Treaty delegates would include awake sleepers, habitat representatives, fleet crews, and translation specialists. Kesh would define its own representation through later intervals.

Calder entered a final military statement. “The Wake Defense Fleet recognizes Kesh-of-Rain as a negotiating party. The fleet claims no occupation authority in Serein. Rescue operations continue under contact limits. The firing order against Everspring remains withdrawn without condition.”

Okoro accepted rescue under those terms.

Iven published Revision Zero with the casualty and debris ledgers attached. They included confidence columns, correction trails, raw translation, and every objection received before the session closed. They did not sign as custodian. Their signature identified only the route by which the files had passed through the cartography bay.

At 13:02, Bramwell closed the cease-fire session and opened the first treaty work period. No forecast selected its delegates. No secret annex defined success. Kesh’s next interval remained nine minutes away.

Pell set Tev’s paper-fish hand on top of the printed terms. Iven did not move it toward an invented hour.

They opened the blank correction column and waited for the next answer to arrive.

Chapter 27 — The Silence After Tomorrow

Aro woke because the witness chamber had stopped predicting his pulse.

The legal-memory substrate used to anticipate each beat and adjust the body’s support field a fraction before blood entered the arteries. Now the field lagged. His heart struck once against resistance, paused, then found its own interval. Pain crossed his ribs in a narrow band.

Doctor Halla Sen stood beside the chair with both hands inside an open service panel. “Do not sit up.”

“I am already sitting.”

“You are leaning with ambition.”

Aro let the chair take him. The chamber carried emergency light and the smell of warm solder. Its mirror had gone dark when the four-kelvin well disconnected. He could no longer see Iven’s face reflected there. He could see only a dim shape with younger hands.

“How long?” he asked.

“Since lattice collapse? Two hours, forty-six minutes. Since you lost consciousness? Eleven minutes.”

“Did the body fail?”

“It revised its opinion.” Sen closed one bypass and opened another. “The substrate expected continuous calibration from the embodiment key. That key no longer has a return carrier. I have moved cardiac timing, endocrine balance, and memory refresh onto local control.”

“Locally stable?”

“Locally arguable.”

Aro breathed. Air entered without the substrate deciding that it should. The experience felt less like freedom than poor maintenance.

Iven sat on the floor near the sealed door. They held a diagnostic slate and a folded sheet from the cartography bay. A paper-fish clock hand marked the fold.

“There is a buffer,” they said.

Aro looked toward the dead mirror.

“Not a live channel,” Iven added. “The final carrier at 10:29 deposited more phase structure than the public decoder rendered before collapse. The witness substrate kept a local residue because your embodiment key matched the sender envelope.”

“How much?”

“Six seconds of carrier depth. Perhaps three voices. Perhaps one voice encoded against itself.”

“Or noise selected because I want a farewell.”

“Yes.”

Iven placed the slate on the floor between them. Its first line contained the decoded message already published: WE DO NOT AGREE. DO NOT MAKE OUR DISAGREEMENT YOUR COMMAND. Beneath it, unrendered phase bands crossed one another like transparent handwriting.

Aro had spent his first hours in the chamber demanding to be read. Now Orison’s last unread material waited inside the machine that kept him alive.

“Can we extract it without restoring the calibration link?” he asked.

Sen answered. “We can copy the buffer into an isolated reader. We cannot ask for correction. Whatever arrived before 10:41 forms the whole source.”

“No future message enters after shutdown.”

“None.”

Aro put his feet on the deck. Sen started to object, then let him stand with one hand on the chair. His legs trembled under weight they had known for only days.

“Copy it,” he said.

They moved the residue into a reader built from a survey spectrometer and the legal desk’s evidence wafer. Iven refused the automatic reconstruction model because its training archive contained one hundred and twelve years of Compact messages. Any gap fill would smuggle old habits into the last exchange. Sen separated the three strongest phase structures by hand.

The first resolved into Pel March’s advocate key.

Aro knew the signature before the reader confirmed it. Memory supplied a hand closing a blue folder, a mouth correcting his posture before testimony, and a voice saying that persuasion required one clean grief. He no longer trusted any of those details. Orison had edited his sixteen strands to make him credible. Pel might have existed as advocate, committee, inherited title, or rhetorical mask.

The phase structure rendered seven words.

ARО, KEEP WHAT LETS YOU SPEAK FOR US.

The first character in his name came from a different alphabet set. Error or emphasis, no one could tell.

The second structure carried no personal key. It matched the coalition signature used by the faction that defended forecast sovereignty. Its message arrived as a question.

IF YOU RELEASE US, WHO ANSWERS FOR WHAT FOLLOWS?

The third overlapped both. Its checksum had broken with the collar fractures. Iven could recover only fragments.

NOT OUR . . . NOT YOUR . . . LET THE PAST . . .

Aro read the three lines until the letters lost authority and became shapes on a survey screen.

“This does not prove three speakers,” Iven said.

“No.”

“It does not prove Pel authored the first.”

“No.”

“The second may ask a genuine question or plant a final command.”

“Yes.”

Sen looked from the reader to Aro’s support field. “You have four hours before the memory refresh enters an untested region. I can preserve the full braid by freezing the current state into legal storage. The body would go into suspension while we build a slower refresh system.”

“How long?”

“Weeks. Months. I will not offer a cleaner number.”

“And the other option?”

“Let the local substrate prune structures it cannot maintain. You stay conscious. You may lose memories.”

“Which ones?”

“The system prioritizes autobiographical coherence, motor learning, language, and legal identity. Your candidate strands overlap. It may preserve a childhood from one and discard the adult who remembered it. It may merge names. It may remove Pel’s face and leave the blue folder.”

Iven said, “No decision needs to happen in this hour.”

“The support field disagrees.”

“The support field can be wrong.”

“So can delay.”

Aro walked to the dark mirror. The body’s balance depended on muscles still learning how much correction a floor required. He placed both palms against the glass. It held no carriers now, only his warmth.

Sixteen candidate lives moved inside him.

The Counselor remembered a public chamber in Orison and the moment a crowd turned against forecast rule. Heir Lidon remembered a kitchen on a station that might never exist. A child strand remembered rain striking metal, though Serein had no open human roof in the future that memory claimed. A pilot strand knew how to dock a vessel Aro had never seen. One person mourned Pel March as a lover. Another feared Pel. A third had never met anyone by that name.

The braid had made those contradictions persuasive by arranging them around a single wound. It had given Aro grief broad enough to stand for a civilization. In the present, that grief had opened doors. It had also made sixteen possible people serve one argument.

“List the strands,” he said.

Sen put their labels on the dark mirror: Counselor, Heir Lidon, Pilot, Orchard Child, Witness, Archivist, Parent, Storm Listener, Treaty Clerk, Engineer, Dissident, Devotee, Skeptic, Patient, Cartographer Courtesy, and Unclassified.

“Those names came from Orison,” Aro said.

“Yes.”

“Not from the people.”

“Yes.”

Iven opened the surviving forecast archive and searched for each label. The return channel had carried attestation metadata with Aro’s pattern, but most personal sources had been compressed into references no present registry could resolve. Counselor linked to four hearing transcripts. Pilot linked to a vessel certification with its name removed. Orchard Child carried no source beyond a sensory checksum. Unclassified held seventeen percent of the braid and no descriptive note.

“Can we verify any event without asking the future?” Aro said.

“Only events that touched old transmissions.” Iven selected the Counselor transcript. “This hearing summary arrived as background to a navigation warning eighteen years ago. It contains the same cadence as your memory, but the names were sealed.”

Aro read the transcript. His memory placed him at the left side of the chamber beneath amber lamps. The archived summary described blue illumination and a witness table on the right. Both might reflect different hearings. Orison’s editors might have merged them because the emotional contour served the embodiment.

“Mark contradiction,” he said.

Iven did.

The Pilot certification produced a harder result. Aro’s hands knew a docking sequence for a vessel with three axial thrusters. The surviving document described four. Sen connected a motor reader and asked him to perform the remembered approach with the chair controls. His first correction would have rolled the certified vessel into its own docking collar.

“Mark unsafe,” Aro said.

The Orchard Child checksum matched a rain recording included in one of Kesh’s early memory layers. Aro had believed the rain struck a human roof in Orison. The sound likely came from Serein’s mineral weather translated into audible form, then placed inside a childhood scene.

“Does that make the child false?” Sen asked.

“No. It makes the source visible.”

He refused to let discovery turn every candidate into fraud. The Treaty Clerk remembered a consent clause that now appeared, independently, in Kesh’s first provisional terms. The Skeptic carried an objection Aro had voiced before learning its label. Heir Lidon’s kitchen had no external proof, but citrus peel still brought a physical ache beneath his tongue.

Iven built a table beside the strand names: supported, contradicted, transformed source, unverified, unsafe in application, emotionally persistent. No row received true or false.

Aro studied the table. “If I freeze, this uncertainty freezes with me.”

“Yes.”

“If I prune, uncertainty does not become permission to discard.”

“No.”

“Then preserve the table outside me. Preserve what we tested, not an account of sixteen complete people.”

Iven signed the method and gave Aro control of the access terms. The archive could retain the labels, source paths, and contradictions. It could not expose sensory content without his consent while he lived.

“Can any strand consent to preservation?”

Sen considered the question. “Not as an independent person. They do not run separately. Waking one in isolation would require reconstruction, and reconstruction would add choices no original strand made.”

“Then I cannot save sixteen people by freezing.”

“No.”

“Can I kill sixteen by allowing local pruning?”

“I cannot support that claim either.”

Aro looked at the labels. Orison’s edit had forced him to speak as if every candidate future already possessed the standing of a completed life. He would not reverse the trick and declare them disposable. They remained memory structures with uncertain origins, moral claims neither zero nor complete.

Iven unfolded the cartography sheet. It showed Serein, Lumen, the moving refuge band, and a blank correction layer. In one corner, copied from the mineral inscription found on Day 1, a sentence waited in Iven’s hand.

Aro read it aloud once. “No map can contain its reader.”

The first time humans had found that sentence, they had treated it as a warning about knowledge. A map changed when a person entered the territory. Prediction failed because the reader acted. Now Aro saw another boundary. A reader could not contain the map either. Orison could not fit inside his body. The sixteen strands could not fit inside one grievance. His present could not carry every person a future might have made.

“That limit goes both ways,” he said.

Iven sat beside the reader. “Explain.”

“Orison sent me as a map of itself. Then it edited the map so the present would follow a chosen route. I treated myself as the container for everyone the route might produce. I cannot hold them. They also cannot hold me after I have arrived.”

“The sender asked you to keep what lets you speak for them.”

“I can preserve the request. I do not have to obey it.”

The advocate-key sentence remained on the survey screen. A person called Aro had existed in the braid before the transmission, but the name might have belonged to a committee’s design. Pel had chosen it. Orison had attested it. The present had learned to call him by it.

Sen brought up the legal identity form. “The substrate requires one name before local pruning. If merged names emerge later, they can be added. Today it needs one stable key.”

Aro almost laughed. A body facing uncertain memory still had to satisfy a database.

“Aro Nhal,” he said.

Sen entered it. “Chosen or retained?”

“Chosen now.”

“Pronouns?”

“He and him.”

“Age?”

“Embodied eleven days. Remembered twenty-eight years. Neither measure owns the other.”

Sen wrote both.

Iven asked, “Do you want Nhal?”

Aro searched the strands. One gave the word as a family name. Another translated it as after-rain. A third remembered a transit card with NHAL printed in block letters. None proved origin.

“Yes. Not because I can verify it. Because I recognize myself when someone says it.”

Sen accepted one name into local legal identity. The support field changed priority. Its refresh process stopped preserving the braid as a future attestation and began preserving Aro’s current associations as a living patient.

Loss did not arrive dramatically. A kitchen vanished first. He had been standing beside a blue counter in a station not yet built; then he knew only that some memory had included the smell of citrus peel. The pilot strand’s docking procedure blurred into hand movements without a vessel. Pel’s blue folder remained while the hand holding it lost skin color.

Aro gripped the chair.

“You can still choose suspension,” Sen said.

“I know.”

A childhood rain memory thinned. He kept the sensation of drops on metal and lost the person who had counted them. Grief rose without a name attached. It belonged to him now, not because it had begun as his, but because his body carried it through this breath.

The support field settled into a slower rhythm.

“Cardiac stable,” Sen said. “Memory refresh local. Degradation rate unknown.”

She conducted the first examination that treated him as a patient rather than received evidence. The substrate’s old diagnostic package compared every result to the embodiment blueprint sent by Orison. Sen deleted the comparison column. She measured what his body did now: blood pressure low after standing, left pupil slow under amber light, grip strength unequal, temperature regulation dependent on two support patches along the spine.

Aro walked six lengths of the chamber. On the fourth, his right knee failed to lock. Iven moved toward him and stopped when he raised a hand. He recovered against the wall without falling.

“Add a rail in the corridor,” Sen said.

“Not a suspension order?”

“A rail.”

She gave him water without flavor compounds. He drank too fast and coughed. The legal substrate had supplied remembered meals, remembered thirst, and remembered hands lifting cups, but the body still had to learn swallowing under local timing.

“What happens to the witness chamber?” he asked.

“It remains available while you need the support field.”

“Available to whom?”

“You.”

“Not the next forecast pattern.”

“There cannot be a next one through these mirrors.”

The answer reached him differently from the lattice shutdown report. This room would not receive another person carrying a courtesy header and a mission selected fifty-three years ahead. Its chair could become a medical chair. Its dark mirror could become glass.

Sen prepared a daily regimen: electrolyte checks, motor exercises, memory notes he could choose not to make, weekly scans of the local refresh, and a manual alarm if cardiac timing drifted beyond tolerance. Nothing in the plan treated continuation as service to Orison.

“How long will I live?”

“Unknown. The substrate may support decades after revision. It may fail in months. Your body will also age, become ill, and respond poorly to several drugs we have not tested.”

“Finite, then.”

“Everyone in this room has that diagnosis.”

Aro returned to the survey reader. Orison had asked who answered for what followed. The answer could not travel fifty-three years forward through a broken lattice. He could still enter it into the present record, where consequences might answer back through people rather than prophecy.

He opened a response file.

“To Orison, whose factions and authors remain uncertain,” he dictated. “I received your final buffered structures after the channel closed. I cannot confirm their speakers. I preserve them with that limit. I will not speak for all of you. I will speak about what was done to me, what I remember, and what I lose. The present releases you from the duty to rule it. I release my past from the duty to produce a verdict. What follows belongs to the people who live it, including me.”

Iven said, “Do you want the line from Serein attached?”

“No. Put the chart beside the response. Let a reader make the relation or refuse it.”

They entered the raw buffer, the hand-separated phases, all alternative decodings, Sen’s medical options, Aro’s identity choice, and the response. No checksum from the future could authenticate the file. Three present witnesses signed what they had observed.

At the bottom, Aro added one sentence: This answer was not transmitted.

The distinction hurt. It also prevented farewell from pretending to be conversation across a channel that no longer existed.

A knock sounded at the witness chamber door. Okoro had come aboard from Everspring by rescue shuttle for the first treaty work period. She carried no mission uniform, only a pressure layer and a slate full of objections from the sixty awake sleepers.

“Are you available as a delegate?” she asked Aro.

“Under what standing?”

“Embodied person with edited future memories, present witness to forecast governance, and someone who has already objected to our first reciprocal-memory draft.”

“Not descendants’ emissary?”

“Only if you claim it.”

Aro looked once at the dead mirror. Orison remained in the buffer, the archive, and whatever futures might still grow without a route back. It no longer occupied the chair through a standing he had to perform.

“I do not claim it,” he said. “I can attend as Aro.”

Okoro opened the door wider.

The corridor outside had lost two ceiling panels during the battle. Repair cables crossed the deck under yellow tape. Air moved through the gap with a faint mineral smell from damaged filters. Nothing about it matched the corridors in Aro’s candidate memories.

He stepped over the first cable without knowing whether the next breath belonged to a year, a decade, or only the walk to treaty work. The dark mirror held his handprints after he left.

Epilogue — The First Unadvised Morning

Fourteen months after the return lattice fell silent, Ansel Quade canceled her daughter’s surgery.

She did it at 06:12 on Pell Station, after the surgeon disclosed a new inflammation marker and before the foundry shift began. Under the old forecast compact, a risk bulletin would have assigned a preferred hour, insurer liability, and the acceptable delay. The archived bulletins still existed. None could bind the hospital.

Ansel’s daughter listened, asked three questions, and chose to wait two weeks for another scan. The surgeon recorded uncertainty in the chart. The insurer recorded no penalty. Ansel missed the first foundry bell while they argued about breakfast.

At 06:40, she approved a pressure-vessel pour whose alloy schedule had never appeared in future advice. The first mold cracked. The second held.

No civic siren marked either result.

Everspring crossed Serein’s copper storm in a negotiated orbit that changed every nine days. Its terraforming sequencer remained physically severed. Twelve hundred sleepers had awakened, Lina Seft among them, while the rest followed a thaw schedule reviewed by people who could object to it. Lina spent her mornings rebuilding the bank-twelve pump tree from the pressure-marked sketch she had signed before suspension.

Lio Venn taught latch maintenance in a converted ward. Across the passage, Meera Sol conducted the morning session of the Kesh treaty school. Half her students came from the seedship. The others joined from fleet ships and habitats through delayed relay.

Today’s lesson concerned refusal.

Meera offered a human memory manifest: the sound of rain on a Pell school ring. Kesh offered a storm pattern translated as a mineral shelf releasing an old impact. Students could receive either, both, or neither. A child named Osa declined the human recording because storms frightened her. The console accepted the refusal without closing her lesson.

Nine minutes later, Kesh replied with a new exercise.

REFUSAL REMEMBERED. RELATION CONTINUES.

Osa asked if she could change her mind tomorrow.

A boy from Deck Eleven asked whether accepting the mineral memory gave Kesh permission to keep his reaction. Meera sent that question too. The class waited through the interval by checking the school’s physical boundaries: no recording in the quiet alcove, no automatic copy to family accounts, deletion requests posted beside every received file. Human systems could not erase what a student remembered, so the wall stated that limit in six languages.

The reply crossed the storm as three measured pulses.

REACTION BELONGS TO REACTING CONTINUITY. RECORD REQUIRES REQUEST.

Meera marked the translation provisional. Two students proposed alternatives for continuity. Osa drew the storm pattern without listening to its audio rendering. Beyond the classroom port, the seedship’s damaged Radiator Three had become a fixed dark petal, while a new detachable panel shed heat beside it under Kesh’s nine-day permission.

Meera entered both questions without predicting tomorrow’s answer.

Sena shut down the training tug’s center display while six relay pilots were using it.

Complaints filled the cabin. One trainee kept flying from optical bearings. Another asked the aft pair for their heat ledger. A third tried to restore the display and discovered Sena had pulled the breaker.

“Your shared plot has a hole,” Sena said. “Name it.”

The trainees reported what each could see. They assigned sectors, exchanged limits, and left one corridor unclaimed until a courier drone crossed it. No pilot took command of the whole geometry.

The exercise ended with three kilograms of reaction mass unused and every radiator inside margin. Sena marked the result as adequate.

On the bulkhead behind her hung Tev Arin’s manual shutter tool. The empty mug remained in Sworn’s courier bay, where new pilots sometimes mistook it for equipment.

“Again,” Sena said.

This time she left the center display dark from the start.

Iven drew the consent-zones as blank fields.

They did not shade Kesh’s storm columns, the disputed salvage arcs, or the habitats that had declined shared archives. Each blank carried a source path and a date for review. Empty space no longer implied available space.

Aro arrived late to the treaty room with one hand on the corridor rail Doctor Sen had ordered installed. His local substrate had survived the year, though his right knee failed on tired days and three more candidate memories had thinned beyond retrieval. He carried a notebook because he chose which days to record.

“Am I on the map?” he asked.

“Only where you consented to appear,” Iven said.

He found his name beside today’s delegate list. No line connected it to Orison.

Outside the port, Everspring adjusted orbit by a measured pulse. The maneuver had been proposed by Okoro, checked by Sena’s relay school, contested by two awakened sleepers, revised by Kesh, and accepted for nine days.

Iven left tomorrow’s track blank.

Aro sat beside the unfilled line and opened his notebook to the morning’s empty page. His pen rested across the margin.
